Engineering Management — June 2023

EXAMINATION OF MARINE ENGINEER OFFICER · Function: Marine Engineering Management at Management Level · M.E.O. Class I

Examination instructions, as printed
  • Answer SIX questions only.
  • All questions carry equal marks.
  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage
  • Blank pages if any, to be struck by (X) at the end of each question.

Instruction 2 on the paper states that all questions carry equal marks, and six answered questions are required against a printed 'Total Marks - 100'. Every question is therefore recorded at 16 marks, which is the figure the paper itself prints on Q4 and Q7, so six answered questions total 96 against the printed 100. The discrepancy is printed on the source and is not corrected. Marks are printed on four questions only - Q3 (8+8), Q4 (16), Q7 (16) and Q8 (8+8); Q1, Q2, Q5, Q6 and Q9 carry no printed marks at all and are flagged printed_marks_absent.

Understand this first

Strip the two limbs down and they are one idea seen twice. The first asks what the law of the sea means by pollution and what it therefore requires of the State whose coastline is at risk; the second asks who actually pays when the pollution happens anyway. The definition is deliberately enormous - anything a person puts into the sea, whether a substance or merely energy such as heat or noise, that is likely to do harm, even if no harm has yet occurred. Because it is that wide, the duty it creates has to be split up, and the way the Convention splits it is the real content of the first limb: a State has almost a free hand over what it does on its own land, and almost none over what it demands of a foreign ship, because a ship moves and belongs to somebody else. So the rules for ships are made through the international organisation, and the coastal State's power to enforce them grows as the evidence and the seriousness grow. The second limb follows from the last piece of the first: having imposed a duty, the Convention also requires that victims can actually recover. For oil that recovery is built in layers, and the reason it is layered is that no single shipowner could carry the cost of a bad spill and no insurer would write unlimited cover. So the cost is shared between the ship and the cargo - the owner pays first, strictly but only up to a limit, and beyond that the people who receive the oil pay through funds they finance. Once you see that the whole scheme is ship-then-cargo, the details stop being a list to memorise.

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  4. The coastal State's obligations - enforcement, and its limits

Memory cue. Limb A walks outward: DEFINE (1) - DUTY (2) - RULES (3) - ENFORCE (4). Inside the definition, the five earning words: MAN, INDIRECT, ENERGY, LIKELY, AMENITIES (1). Inside rule-making, go seaward and watch the discretion shrink: LAND, DUMP, SHIPS, AIR (3). Inside enforcement: FLAG, PORT, COASTAL, CASUALTY (4). Limb B is SHIP, CARGO, CARGO (6, 7) - and in every row answer the same three columns, WHO PAYS, WHERE FROM, WHAT PAPER (7), whose third answer is certificate, none, none. Finish on what it is not: BUNKERS IS NOT A TIER (8).

Model written answer

1. The Convention's definition of pollution, and what makes it unusually wide

UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) defines "pollution of the marine environment" as the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as: harm to living resources and marine life; hazards to human health; hindrance to marine activities, including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea; impairment of quality for use of sea water; and reduction of amenities.

Five features of that wording earn marks, and each should be stated as a point:

  1. "By man" - a natural seep or a volcanic discharge is not pollution under the Convention, however damaging.
  2. "Directly or indirectly" - a river carrying industrial waste to the sea is caught, so the polluting act need not happen at sea at all.
  3. "Substances or energy" - heat, noise and radiation are pollution. This is the element most candidates omit and it is the one that makes the definition modern.
  4. "Results or is likely to result" - no damage need have occurred. Likelihood is enough, which is what allows preventive regulation.
  5. "Reduction of amenities" - purely aesthetic and recreational harm counts. Oil on a beach is pollution even where nothing is killed.

Note finally that the definition is source-neutral. Nothing in it mentions ships. Ships are one source among land-based sources, dumping, seabed activities and the atmosphere, and that is why the coastal State's obligations are structured source by source.

2. The general obligation, and why it is not confined to any zone

Article 192 states the whole of Part XII in one sentence: States have the obligation to protect and preserve the marine environment. It is free-standing and not zone-limited - the rest of the Convention allocates rights zone by zone, but this imposes a duty across all of them, including the high seas.

Article 193 then makes the balance explicit: States have the sovereign right to exploit their natural resources pursuant to their environmental policies and in accordance with their duty to protect and preserve the marine environment. Development is permitted; it is not unconditioned.

Article 194 is the operative obligation:

  • 194(1) - all measures necessary to prevent, reduce and control pollution from any source, using the best practicable means at their disposal and in accordance with their capabilities, endeavouring to harmonize policies. Note that the standard is expressly capability-relative.
  • 194(2) - activities under a State's jurisdiction or control must not cause damage by pollution to other States, and pollution from incidents under its control must not spread beyond its zones.
  • 194(3)(b) - for vessels specifically: preventing accidents, dealing with emergencies, ensuring safety of operations, preventing intentional and unintentional discharges, and regulating design, construction, equipment, operation and manning.
  • 194(5) - measures to protect rare or fragile ecosystems and the habitat of depleted, threatened or endangered species.

Two short articles complete the frame and are easy marks. Article 195 forbids a State, in taking measures, from transferring damage or hazards from one area to another, or transforming one type of pollution into another - so scrubbing a stack emission into the sea is not compliance. Article 196 covers harmful technologies and the introduction of alien or new species, which is the treaty root of ballast water and biofouling control.

3. The coastal State's obligations - rule-making

Part XII deals with pollution source by source, and the coastal State's freedom of action differs sharply between them:

SourceArticleWhat the coastal State must doHow much discretion
Land-based207Adopt national laws and regulations, taking into account internationally agreed rules and standardsWide - only 'taking into account'
Dumping210National law; and dumping within the territorial sea, EEZ or on the continental shelf requires the express prior approval of the coastal StateWide, and a positive control power
Vessels211Act through the competent international organization; national law must have at least the same effect as the generally accepted international standardNarrow - the tightest constraint in Part XII
Atmosphere212Laws for airspace under sovereignty and for ships of its flagWide

Article 211 is the one that matters for ships, and its internal structure is the answer to "key features":

  • 211(1) - rules and standards are established through the competent international organization, that is the IMO, and are re-examined from time to time.
  • 211(2) - a flag State's own laws must have at least the same effect as those international standards. Stricter for your own ships, yes; laxer, no.
  • 211(4) - in the territorial sea a coastal State may legislate for foreign vessels including those in innocent passage, but such laws shall not hamper innocent passage.
  • 211(5) - in the exclusive economic zone it may legislate only conforming to and giving effect to generally accepted international rules. It cannot invent its own standard.
  • 211(6) - the one escape route: for a particular, clearly defined area of its EEZ where the international rules are inadequate, it may submit scientific and technical evidence to the IMO; the IMO determines within 12 months; the measures bind foreign vessels only 15 months after submission; and they may never impose design, construction, manning or equipment standards other than the generally accepted international ones.

That last prohibition is the single most quotable limit in Part XII. It is why a coastal State can impose a discharge or routeing requirement but cannot demand a different hull, and it is the legal basis on which Special Areas and Particularly Sensitive Sea Areas are created through the IMO rather than declared unilaterally.

4. The coastal State's obligations - enforcement, and its limits

Rule-making and enforcement are separately allocated, and confusing them is the commonest error on this question.

  • Article 217 - the flag State. The primary enforcement duty: compliance by ships of its flag wherever the violation occurs.
  • Article 218 - the port State. Where a ship is voluntarily in port, proceedings may be taken for a discharge outside that State's own waters - a jurisdiction that exists nowhere else in the Convention.
  • Article 220 - the coastal State, and its powers are deliberately graded: proceedings where the ship is voluntarily in port for a violation in its territorial sea or EEZ (220(1)); on clear grounds for a territorial-sea violation, physical inspection and, where the evidence warrants, proceedings including detention (220(2)); and for an EEZ violation, first only the power to require information - identity, port of registry, last and next port of call (220(3)) - escalating to inspection and then to detention as the evidence and the gravity of the discharge increase.
  • Article 221 - after a maritime casualty, the right to take and enforce measures beyond the territorial sea, proportionate to the actual or threatened damage, to protect the coastline and related interests including fishing, where major harmful consequences may reasonably be expected. The article carries its own definition of maritime casualty.
  • Article 235 - States are responsible for fulfilling their international obligations and must ensure recourse for prompt and adequate compensation for pollution damage.

The safeguards in section 7 of Part XII then constrain how all of this is exercised. The shape of the coastal State's position is therefore: a duty everywhere, rule-making power that narrows as you move seaward, and enforcement power that is graded by evidence and by seriousness. And article 235 is the bridge to the second limb - the compensation regime exists because UNCLOS requires that recourse.

5. Why the oil pollution compensation regime has three tiers

Before listing the tiers, say why there are tiers. A major tanker spill can cost more than any shipowner or his insurer could reasonably carry. Unlimited liability would have made tankers uninsurable; leaving victims uncompensated was politically and legally unacceptable. The compromise reached was that the shipowner is liable strictly but with a limit, and the balance is placed on the cargo interest - those who receive the oil.

The defining idea of the regime is therefore that the cost of an oil spill is shared between the ship and the cargo. Every feature below follows from it.

6. Tier one - the shipowner and his insurer

Tier one - Civil Liability Convention 1992 (CLC 92)
Who is responsibleThe registered owner of the ship, strictly - no fault need be proved. Liability is channelled to him, so claims may not be brought against the master, crew, pilot, charterer, manager, operator or salvor acting as such
Source of fundsThe shipping side - the owner and, in practice, his P&I club under the compulsory insurance
Certification carriedA certificate of insurance or other financial security, issued or certified by the flag State (or by a State Party where the ship is not registered in one), carried on board, for ships carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo. It is issued against a 'blue card' from the insurer
  • There is a right of direct action against the insurer, so the victim is not defeated by the owner's insolvency.
  • The owner may limit his liability by reference to the tonnage of the ship. No figure is given here - the limits are amended over time and every figure carries a date.
  • He loses the right to limit if the damage resulted from his personal act or omission, committed with intent to cause damage, or recklessly and with knowledge that damage would probably result.
  • The exceptions are narrow: act of war; a wholly exceptional, inevitable and irresistible natural phenomenon; damage wholly caused by a third party's intentional act; or the negligence of an authority responsible for navigational aids.

7. Tiers two and three - the Funds, and who actually provides the money

Tier two - Fund Convention 1992Tier three - Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003
When it respondsWhere tier one gives no compensation, or is financially inadequate to meet the established claimsWhere tier two is insufficient to meet the established claims
Who is responsibleThe 1992 International Oil Pollution Compensation Fund, an intergovernmental bodyThe Supplementary Fund
Source of fundsLevies on receivers of contributing oil - crude oil and heavy fuel oil received by sea above a threshold quantity in a State PartyThe same - contributions from receivers of contributing oil in States that have opted into the Protocol
Certification carried by the shipNONE. The ship carries no Fund certificate; the ship's document is the CLC certificate aloneNONE, for the same reason

Three points here separate a good answer from a list:

  1. The cargo interest pays for tiers two and three - not the shipowner and not governments. The Funds are financed by the oil receivers in the member States. This is the fact most candidates get wrong.
  2. The tiers are cumulative and sequential, each with its own overall ceiling for any one incident, and each tier is reached only when the one below it is exhausted or unavailable.
  3. Tier three is optional. A State must already be a member of the 1992 Fund and must separately accede to the 2003 Protocol. So the compensation available for the same casualty depends on where it happens.

Note the answer to "certification required by ship under each tier" precisely: only tier one produces a certificate carried by the ship. Saying so, and explaining why - the Funds are financed ashore, not by the ship - is worth more than inventing two more certificates.

8. What the three tiers do NOT cover - and the trap

  • The regime is about persistent oil carried in bulk as cargo - in substance, the laden or residue-carrying tanker case. It is not a general marine pollution compensation scheme.
  • Bunker oil spilled from a non-tanker is the Bunkers Convention 2001: strict liability on a wider group of owners, compulsory insurance and a certificate, but a single tier with no fund behind it. A candidate who offers Bunkers as one of the three tiers has misread the question.
  • Hazardous and noxious substances have their own convention, which is not in force at this sitting. It cannot be cited as a tier or as current law.
  • Geography matters. The tiers respond only in States party, and the third only where that State has opted in.
  • In India, domestic effect to the civil liability and fund regimes is given through the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.

Close by joining the two limbs, because the examiner printed them together: UNCLOS article 235 obliges States to ensure recourse for prompt and adequate compensation for pollution damage, and the three tiers are how that obligation is discharged for oil. The first limb states the duty; the second shows the machinery.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Two limbs, no printed marks, and a very wide subject. The risk is not ignorance - most candidates know something about UNCLOS and something about CLC - it is drift: writing a general essay on marine pollution and never answering either printed instruction. The route defends against that by anchoring each limb on the words the examiner actually used.

Limb A prints two demands - "define" and "discuss the key features of obligations of coastal states". Definition is a quotation task: reproduce article 1(1)(4) as closely as you can and then dissect it, because every element of it is a scoring proposition. Obligations is a structure task: the marks are in showing that the Convention allocates rule-making and enforcement separately, and that the coastal State's freedom narrows as it moves seaward.

Limb B prints three demands and they are easy to leave one out: who pays, the source of funds, and the certification required by the ship, in each case. Three demands times three tiers is nine propositions, and they are best written as a table. Step 5 - why there are tiers at all - is what turns the table from a memorised list into an explanation, and step 8 is where the examiner separates candidates by asking, in effect, what the regime is not.

The definition is worth learning word for word

Very few provisions repay verbatim memory as well as article 1(1)(4), because the marks are distributed across its clauses. If you can write "the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as..." you have earned several propositions before you have explained anything.

The five deleterious effects are: harm to living resources and marine life; hazards to human health; hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea; impairment of quality for use of sea water; and reduction of amenities. Reduction of amenities and or energy are the two most frequently omitted, and both are distinctive enough to be noticed.

Rule-making and enforcement are different questions

Candidates routinely answer "what are the coastal State's obligations" by listing enforcement powers. The Convention separates them, and saying so is itself a scoring point. Rule-making runs 207 land-based, 210 dumping, 211 vessels, 212 atmosphere - and the coastal State's discretion is widest for land-based sources and narrowest for ships. Enforcement runs 217 flag, 218 port, 220 coastal, 221 casualties - and the coastal State's power is graded rather than absolute.

The reason for the asymmetry is worth one sentence: a State may regulate its own territory as it likes, but a ship is a moving object subject to another State's flag, so uniform international standards are the only workable basis. That is why article 211 keeps referring back to the competent international organization.

The 12 and 15 month periods in article 211(6)

If you can carry two numbers into this answer, carry these. Where a coastal State wants special mandatory measures for a clearly defined area of its EEZ, it communicates to the IMO with scientific and technical evidence; the IMO determines within 12 months; and the laws become applicable to foreign vessels only 15 months after the submission. They may never impose design, construction, manning or equipment standards beyond the generally accepted international ones. That paragraph is the legal skeleton of the Special Area and PSSA machinery, and quoting the two periods proves the Convention was opened.

Common mistakes

  • Paraphrasing the definition loosely and losing "or energy", "indirectly", "is likely to result" and "reduction of amenities" - four separate propositions gone.
  • Answering limb A entirely with enforcement powers and never mentioning the rule-making obligations.
  • Treating article 192 as applying only inside national zones. It is not zone-limited.
  • Offering the Bunkers Convention 2001 as one of the three tiers. It is a separate, single-tier regime for bunkers from non-tankers.
  • Offering the HNS Convention as a tier, or describing it as in force. It is not in force at this sitting.
  • Saying the Funds are financed by shipowners or by governments. They are financed by receivers of contributing oil.
  • Inventing a Fund certificate or a Supplementary Fund certificate carried by the ship. Only tier one produces a ship's certificate.
  • Quoting SDR limits from memory. They are amended over time and a stale figure is worse than none.
  • Writing that the coastal State may impose its own construction standards on foreign ships in its EEZ. Article 211(6) expressly forbids it.
  • Forgetting that the owner loses the right to limit for his own intentional or reckless conduct with knowledge.

Examiner traps

  • "Is noise pollution?" Yes - the definition says substances or energy. So is waste heat.
  • "Must damage have occurred?" No - 'results or is likely to result'.
  • "Can a coastal State demand a double hull in its EEZ?" No. Article 211(6) permits special measures for a defined area but not design, construction, manning or equipment standards beyond the generally accepted international ones.
  • "Who may be sued under CLC 92?" The registered owner, and liability is channelled to him - not the manager, charterer, crew, pilot or salvor.
  • "Who pays for tiers two and three?" Receivers of contributing oil. Say cargo, not ship.
  • "What does the ship carry for tier two?" Nothing. Only the CLC certificate is a ship's document.
  • "Is the third tier available everywhere?" No - it is optional, and requires 1992 Fund membership first.
  • "What links the two limbs?" Article 235 - the duty to ensure recourse for prompt and adequate compensation.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Define pollution under UNCLOS. Give the article. Article 1(1)(4).
  • Which article states the general obligation? 192. And what qualifies the right to exploit resources? 193.
  • Which article deals with pollution from vessels? 211. What standard must national law meet? At least the same effect as the generally accepted international rules.
  • What can a coastal State do about a suspected discharge in its EEZ? Require information first; inspect on clear grounds; detain only where the evidence and the gravity warrant it - article 220.
  • What may a State do after a casualty outside its territorial sea? Measures proportionate to the actual or threatened damage - article 221.
  • Name the three tiers. CLC 1992, the 1992 Fund, the Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003.
  • Who is strictly liable under tier one and can he limit? The registered owner; yes, by tonnage, unless the damage resulted from his personal intentional or reckless act with knowledge.
  • What certificate does the ship carry, and who issues it? A certificate of insurance or other financial security under CLC 92, issued or certified by the State, on the strength of the insurer's blue card.
  • Is the Bunkers Convention one of the tiers? No - separate regime, one tier, no fund.

Memory framework

For limb A, DEFINE - DUTY - RULES - ENFORCE. Inside the definition, MAN, INDIRECT, ENERGY, LIKELY, AMENITIES - the five words that carry the marks. Inside rule-making, walk seaward: LAND (207) - DUMP (210) - SHIPS (211) - AIR (212), discretion shrinking as you go. Inside enforcement, FLAG - PORT - COASTAL - CASUALTY, that is 217, 218, 220, 221.

For limb B, SHIP - CARGO - CARGO. Tier one is the ship and its insurer; tiers two and three are both the cargo receivers. Then the three demands in every row: WHO PAYS - WHERE FROM - WHAT PAPER. And remember the answer to the third column is certificate, none, none.

Regulation and source map

  • UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) - definition of pollution of the marine environment
  • Article 192 general obligation; 193 sovereign right subject to the duty; 194 measures, including 194(3)(b) vessels and 194(5) rare or fragile ecosystems
  • Article 195 no transfer of damage, no transformation of pollution; 196 technologies and alien or new species
  • Article 207 land-based; 210 dumping; 211 vessels; 212 atmosphere - rule-making
  • Article 211(2) at least the same effect; 211(4) territorial sea, not hampering innocent passage; 211(5) EEZ conforming and giving effect; 211(6) special measures, 12 months and 15 months, no design or construction standards
  • Article 217 flag; 218 port; 220 coastal, graded; 221 maritime casualties; 235 responsibility, liability and recourse for compensation
  • UNCLOS in force 16 November 1994, unamended
  • CLC 1992 - strict channelled liability of the registered owner, tonnage limitation, compulsory insurance, certificate on board, direct action
  • Fund Convention 1992 - second tier, financed by receivers of contributing oil
  • Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003 - optional third tier, same financing
  • Bunkers Convention 2001 and the HNS Convention - named to mark the boundary, not as tiers
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 - the Indian statute at this sitting

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

Limb A rests on UNCLOS read verbatim at source. The Convention entered into force on 16 November 1994 and is unamended, so the text MIW holds is the June 2023 text. There is no edition question and no reversal to perform. That is the strongest evidential position on this paper, and it belongs to this limb alone. Articles 207, 210, 212, 217, 218 and 235 are cited by identity, with their substance carried from MIW's own verification record for the September 2023 sitting of a related question; the section 7 safeguards are referred to only in general terms.

Limb B is deliberately weaker and says so. MIW holds no text of CLC 1992, the Fund Convention 1992 or the Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003 - the corpus classifies them as citation and index only with verified status facts. Accordingly no monetary or Special Drawing Right figure is asserted anywhere: not a CLC tonnage-band limit, not either Fund ceiling, not the contributing-oil threshold. Those limits are amended by tacit acceptance, so every figure carries a date, and a figure recalled from a textbook is likely to be a superseded one. No article number of any of the three instruments is asserted and no text of any of them is quoted. What the question asks for - who pays, from what source, against what certificate - is answered in full without any of that.

One currency instruction for a later editor: do not add SDR figures to this object without sourcing them and without confirming they are the position at or before June 2023. If figures are wanted, they must be dated.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________A The Convention's definition of pollution, and what makes it unusually wide
  2. __________A The general obligation, and why it is not confined to any zone
  3. __________A The coastal State's obligations - rule-making
  4. __________A The coastal State's obligations - enforcement, and its limits
  5. __________B Why the oil pollution compensation regime has three tiers
  6. __________B Tier one - the shipowner and his insurer
  7. __________B Tiers two and three - the Funds, and who actually provides the money
  8. __________B What the three tiers do NOT cover - and the trap
Flashcards 12

1 The definition and what makes it wide. 2 The general obligation, not zone-limited. 3 Coastal State rule-making, source by source. 4 Coastal State enforcement, graded. 5 Why there are three tiers at all. 6 Tier one, the shipowner. 7 Tiers two and three, the Funds. 8 What the tiers do not cover.

Steps 5 and 8 are what lift limb B out of a memorised list; step 3 is where most candidates lose limb A.

Article 1(1)(4): the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water, and reduction of amenities.

The marks are distributed across the clauses, so a loose paraphrase loses several propositions at once.

'By man' - a natural seep is not pollution. 'Indirectly' - a river carrying waste to the sea is caught. 'Or energy' - heat, noise and radiation are pollution. 'Is likely to result' - no damage need have occurred, which is what permits preventive regulation. A fifth, 'reduction of amenities', makes purely aesthetic harm count.

Each is a separate scoring proposition and each is distinctive enough for an examiner to notice its absence.

Rule-making runs source by source - article 207 land-based, 210 dumping, 211 vessels, 212 atmosphere - and the coastal State's discretion is widest for land-based sources and narrowest for ships, where it must act through the IMO and legislate only to at least the same effect. Enforcement is separately allocated - 217 flag, 218 port, 220 coastal, 221 after a casualty - and the coastal State's power is graded by evidence and gravity.

Answering 'obligations' with enforcement alone is the commonest way to lose half of limb A.

For a particular, clearly defined area where the international rules are inadequate, it may communicate to the IMO with supporting scientific and technical evidence; the IMO determines within 12 months; the resulting laws apply to foreign vessels only 15 months after submission. It may never impose design, construction, manning or equipment standards other than generally accepted international ones.

The two periods prove the Convention was opened, and the prohibition is the legal skeleton of Special Areas and PSSAs.

Where the ship is voluntarily in port, proceedings may be instituted for a violation in the territorial sea or EEZ. For a territorial-sea violation, on clear grounds, physical inspection and, where the evidence warrants, proceedings including detention. For an EEZ violation, on clear grounds, first only a power to require information - identity, port of registry, last and next port of call - escalating to inspection and then detention as the evidence and the seriousness of the discharge increase.

The grading is the point of the article; describing it as a single power to detain misstates the Convention.

Tier one, CLC 1992 - the registered owner of the ship, strictly, with liability channelled to him. Tier two, the Fund Convention 1992 - the 1992 IOPC Fund. Tier three, the Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003 - the Supplementary Fund. The tiers are cumulative and sequential, each reached only when the one below is exhausted or unavailable.

The stem asks 'in each case' who is responsible, so all three must be named separately.

From levies on receivers of contributing oil - crude oil and heavy fuel oil received by sea above a threshold quantity in a State Party. The cargo interest funds both Funds, not the shipowner and not governments. Tier three additionally requires that the State has opted into the 2003 Protocol, having first joined the 1992 Fund.

This is the single most commonly wrong statement on the question, and it is also the idea that explains the whole regime.

Only under tier one. A certificate of insurance or other financial security under CLC 1992, issued or certified by the State on the strength of the insurer's blue card, carried on board by ships carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo. Neither Fund tier produces a ship's certificate, because the Funds are financed ashore by cargo receivers rather than by the ship.

The stem asks specifically about certification per tier, and the correct answer is certificate, none, none - with the reason.

No. It is a separate regime for pollution by bunker oil from ships other than tankers carrying persistent oil as cargo - strict liability, compulsory insurance and a certificate, but a single tier with no fund behind it. The HNS Convention is likewise not a tier, and is not in force at this sitting.

Offering Bunkers as a tier is the classic misread, and naming the boundary correctly is itself worth marks.

UNCLOS article 235. Having imposed the duty to protect and preserve the marine environment, the Convention also requires States to ensure that recourse is available for prompt and adequate compensation for pollution damage. The three tiers are the machinery by which that obligation is discharged for oil.

The examiner printed the two limbs together; saying why converts two half-answers into one argument.

Because the CLC tonnage-band limits and both Fund ceilings are amended by tacit acceptance over time, so every figure carries a date and a number recalled from a textbook is likely to be superseded. The question asks who pays, from what source and against what certificate - none of which requires a figure.

A confidently stated stale number reads worse to an examiner than an accurate description with no number in it.

Quick revision
15-second recall
LIMB A. Definition - UNCLOS article 1(1)(4): the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in deleterious effects: harm to living resources and marine life; hazards to human health; hindrance to marine activities including fishing; impairment of quality for use of sea water; reduction of amenities. Five earning words: by man / indirectly / or energy / likely / amenities. Duty: art 192 protect and preserve - not zone-limited; 193 the right to exploit is subject to it; 194 all measures from any source by best practicable means, per capabilities (194(3)(b) ships; 194(5) fragile ecosystems); 195 no transferring damage or transforming pollution; 196 technologies and alien species. Rule-making by source: 207 land-based (taking into account - wide discretion), 210 dumping (express prior approval), 211 vessels (through IMO; at least the same effect), 212 atmosphere. Within 211: (4) territorial sea, must not hamper innocent passage; (5) EEZ, only conforming and giving effect; (6) special measures for a clearly defined area - IMO decides in 12 months, binds foreign ships 15 months after submission, and never design, construction, manning or equipment standards. Enforcement: 217 flag (primary), 218 port (voluntarily in port, discharges outside its waters), 220 coastal (graded: information, then inspection on clear grounds, then detention), 221 casualties (measures beyond the territorial sea, proportionate), 235 recourse for prompt and adequate compensation - the bridge to limb B. LIMB B. Why tiers: a big spill exceeds any owner's capacity; unlimited liability makes tankers uninsurable; so the cost is shared between ship and cargo. Tier 1 - CLC 92: registered owner, strict, channelled; funds from owner and P&I club; ship carries a certificate of insurance (blue card); direct action; limits by tonnage - quote no figure; limit lost for personal intent or recklessness with knowledge. Tier 2 - Fund 1992: where tier 1 gives nothing or is inadequate; funded by receivers of contributing oil; no ship certificate. Tier 3 - Supplementary Fund 2003 Protocol: where tier 2 is insufficient; same cargo financing; optional, needs 1992 Fund membership; no ship certificate. Not tiers: Bunkers 2001 (non-tanker bunkers, one tier, no fund); HNS (not in force). India: Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.
Keywords
by man, directly or indirectly, substances or energyresults or is likely to resultreduction of amenitiesarticle 192 is not zone-limited211 - at least the same effect211(6) - never design, construction, manning or equipment220 - graded enforcement235 is the bridge between the limbscost shared between ship and cargoreceivers of contributing oil finance BOTH fundsonly tier one produces a ship's certificateBunkers 2001 is not one of the three tiers
Critical numbers
  • UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) - the definition of pollution of the marine environment
  • Articles 192, 193, 194, 195, 196 - the general obligations
  • Articles 207, 210, 211, 212 - rule-making, source by source
  • Article 211(6) - IMO determines within 12 months; measures bind foreign vessels 15 months after submission
  • Articles 217, 218, 220, 221 - flag, port, coastal and casualty enforcement
  • Article 235 - recourse for prompt and adequate compensation
  • UNCLOS in force 16 November 1994, unamended
  • Three tiers: CLC 1992, Fund Convention 1992, Supplementary Fund Protocol 2003
  • NO SDR or monetary limit is asserted - the limits are amended by tacit acceptance and every figure carries a date
Critical regulation
UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) defines pollution of the marine environment as the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water and reduction of amenities. Article 192 imposes the general obligation to protect and preserve the marine environment, and article 211 requires that rules for vessel-source pollution be established through the competent international organization, with national law having at least the same effect.
Major trap
Two traps, one in each limb. In limb A, answering "the obligations of coastal states" with a list of enforcement powers and never mentioning the rule-making obligations under articles 207 to 212 - and in particular missing that the coastal State's discretion is widest for land-based sources and narrowest for ships, and that article 211(6) forbids it from imposing design, construction, manning or equipment standards on foreign ships even in a special area. In limb B, offering the Bunkers Convention 2001 as one of the three tiers, saying the Funds are financed by shipowners or governments rather than by receivers of contributing oil, and inventing Fund certificates carried by the ship - only tier one produces a ship's certificate. And quote no SDR figure: the limits are amended over time and a remembered number is probably a superseded one.

Also on the platform: QP2306 Q2 - the origin and authority of IMO, which is the 'competent international organization' article 211 keeps referring to · QP2306 Q5 - anti-fouling paints, a worked instance of article 194(3)(b) becoming a specific IMO instrument · QP2306 Q7 - the SEEMP, the same UNCLOS-to-IMO mechanism operating on greenhouse gases

Repeated — same wording Returns in the same words from March 2023 Q1. Also set at September 2023 Q8, April 2024 Q9.

Understand this first

The question hides one idea behind four tasks. That idea is that the body which writes the world's shipping rules cannot enforce a single one of them. It was set up after the war as a forum where governments could agree technical standards for ships trading internationally, and for its first three decades even its name said it was only there to consult. What it does is produce texts. Those texts do nothing until a government decides to be bound by one, tells the depositary so, and then writes it into its own law and polices it. Everything else in the question is a consequence of that. The long formal process by which a State accepts a text exists because acceptance is the only thing that makes the text law. A shortcut had to be invented for keeping those texts up to date, because the full process was so slow that improvements were arriving years after the accidents that prompted them; so the rule was reversed, and a proposed change now takes effect unless enough governments object. And because the organisation can only ever look at governments, its two oversight tools both point at government rather than at industry: one examines what went wrong after a ship is lost, on the strict understanding that it is looking for causes and not for someone to punish, and the other examines whether a government is actually equipped to do what it promised when it accepted the texts in the first place. Hold that single thread and the four parts stop being four things to remember.

Knowledge map
The Origin and Authority of IMO, Ratification, and Two Oversight Instruments

      • first international body devoted exclusively to maritime affairs

      • related administrative and legal matters; removal of discriminatory action and unnecessary restrictions

      • COUNCIL - the EXECUTIVE organ; FORTY Members in THREE categories; supervises between Assemblies; appoints the Secretary-General subject to Assembly approval

Memory cue. Limb a) is three beats - BORN (1), POWERS (2), BINDING (4) - with the organs slotted between as the machinery (3) and tacit acceptance as the shortcut (5). Born is 48, 58, 82. Powers is one sentence: rules for States, never for ships. Binding is ADOPT, ACCEPT, DEPOSIT, FORCE, LAW (4), and the test is COUNT AND TONNAGE (4). Amendment is SILENCE IS CONSENT (5). Limb B is a pair pulling opposite ways - BACK (6) versus FORWARD (7), ship versus State - and they meet at (8).

Remember8 route headings
Cover55 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 55 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The origin - from a 1948 conference to the Organization of today
  • UN Maritime Conference, GENEVA, 6 MARCH 1948 adopts the convention establishing IMCO
  • first international body devoted exclusively to maritime affairs
  • entered into force 17 MARCH 1958 - TEN YEARS later, which is itself the worked example of ratification
  • first Assembly JANUARY 1959; first task the 1960 SOLAS Convention
  • renamed INTERNATIONAL MARITIME ORGANIZATION with effect from 22 MAY 1982 - 'Consultative' dropped
  • a specialized agency of the United Nations, headquartered in London
2. The authority vested in IMO - and the decisive limitation
  • machinery for COOPERATION among Governments on technical matters affecting international shipping
  • encourage and facilitate adoption of the HIGHEST PRACTICABLE STANDARDS for safety, efficiency of navigation and prevention of pollution from ships
  • related administrative and legal matters; removal of discriminatory action and unnecessary restrictions
  • THE AUTHORITY IS LEGISLATIVE IN FORM AND WHOLLY INDIRECT IN EFFECT
  • IMO adopts conventions, protocols, codes, guidelines and resolutions
  • IMO CANNOT ENFORCE ANY OF THEM AGAINST ANY SHIP - no inspectors, no detention, no prosecution
  • it regulates STATES, not vessels; an instrument reaches a ship only through the State's own law
  • enforcement rests with FLAG, PORT and COASTAL States - the UNCLOS Part XII allocation
3. The organs through which the authority is exercised
  • ASSEMBLY - all Member States, regular session EVERY TWO YEARS; work programme, budget, elects the Council, adopts A-series resolutions
  • COUNCIL - the EXECUTIVE organ; FORTY Members in THREE categories; supervises between Assemblies; appoints the Secretary-General subject to Assembly approval
  • A.1152(32) of December 2021 would enlarge the Council to 52 but requires acceptance by TWO THIRDS and is NOT IN FORCE at this sitting
  • FIVE MAIN COMMITTEES - MSC, MEPC, Legal, Technical Cooperation, Facilitation
  • MSC and MEPC are where the technical amendments are adopted
  • SUB-COMMITTEES do the detailed technical work and report to MSC and MEPC
  • SECRETARIAT under the Secretary-General - administration, depositary functions, circulation
4. The ratification process - briefly, as the question asks
  • ADOPTION by the relevant organ or by a diplomatic conference - creates NO obligation on anybody
  • CONSENT TO BE BOUND by signature subject to ratification, acceptance or approval, OR by accession
  • ratification and accession have IDENTICAL legal effect - they differ only in whether the State signed first
  • DEPOSIT of the instrument with the SECRETARY-GENERAL as depositary, who notifies all States
  • ENTRY INTO FORCE on the convention's own conditions - for a technical convention, a number of STATES AND a share of WORLD MERCHANT TONNAGE, often plus a further period
  • the tonnage condition exists because a convention on ship construction is worthless if it binds only States with no ships
  • APPLICATION - the State legislates and administers; until then the convention reaches no ship
5. Keeping a convention current - tacit acceptance
  • explicit amendment proved unworkably slow; some early amendments never entered into force at all
  • an amendment is DEEMED ACCEPTED on a stated date and enters into force on a second stated date
  • UNLESS objections are received from a specified number of Contracting Governments
  • THE BURDEN IS REVERSED - SILENCE IS CONSENT
  • this is why SOLAS and MARPOL can be kept current, and why the amendment set in force at a given date must always be checked
6. Short note - the Casualty Investigation Code
  • adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 MAY 2008
  • MANDATORY through SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 from 1 JANUARY 2010 - the Code is not binding of itself
  • older underlying duties: SOLAS I/21, MARPOL Article 12, UNCLOS Article 94(7)
  • Part I general provisions and definitions; Part II mandatory standards; Part III recommended practices
  • a marine safety investigation is MANDATORY for every VERY SERIOUS MARINE CASUALTY
  • very serious marine casualty - TOTAL LOSS OF THE SHIP, A DEATH, OR SEVERE DAMAGE TO THE ENVIRONMENT
  • the sole objective is PREVENTION; the investigation is SEPARATE AND INDEPENDENT FROM, and must not prejudice, any blame or liability proceeding
  • the FLAG STATE is the marine safety investigating State; substantially interested States may participate; the report goes to the Organization
7. Short note - the IMO Member State Audit Scheme
  • it audits the MEMBER STATE, not the ship and not the company - because IMO cannot enforce against ships
  • began VOLUNTARY, MANDATORY from 1 JANUARY 2016 by amendments to SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines and its 1988 Protocol, Tonnage and COLREG
  • THE AUDIT STANDARD IS THE III CODE - A.1070(28) of 4 December 2013
  • the III Code requires an overall STRATEGY with a methodology to monitor and assess it
  • national LEGISLATION giving full and complete effect; adequate enforcement and administrative arrangements
  • PERIODIC SELF-EVALUATION against performance indicators; organised by the three State roles - flag, coastal, port
  • the audit examines law, administration, resourcing, oversight of recognized organizations and performance measurement
  • outcome: findings and observations, then a CORRECTIVE ACTION PLAN; results feed technical cooperation and capacity building
  • the supporting list of auditable obligations at this sitting is A.1157(32)
8. What the two short notes have in common
  • both are OVERSIGHT mechanisms answering the same weakness - IMO makes rules it cannot enforce
  • the Code looks BACKWARDS at a casualty; the Scheme looks FORWARDS at whether the State can prevent one
  • the Code's subject is the SHIP and the event; the Scheme's subject is the STATE and its system
  • the Code's purpose is PREVENTION NEVER BLAME; the Scheme's is CAPABILITY and corrective action
  • both illustrate limb a) exactly - a resolution acquires binding force from the convention that points at it, not from its own words
Model written answer

1. The origin - from a 1948 conference to the Organization of today

  • The United Nations Maritime Conference met in Geneva and, on 6 March 1948, adopted a convention establishing the Inter-Governmental Maritime Consultative Organization (IMCO). It was the first international body devoted exclusively to maritime affairs.
  • The convention did not enter into force until 17 March 1958 - a delay of ten years, because it needed ratification by a set number of States including several with substantial tonnage. That delay is itself the best illustration of the ratification process the same limb asks about.
  • The first Assembly met in January 1959, and the Organization's first task was the 1960 SOLAS Convention.
  • The name was changed to the International Maritime Organization with effect from 22 May 1982. The word "Consultative" was dropped, and its removal marks the Organization's shift from an advisory forum to the body that makes the international rules for shipping.
  • It is a specialized agency of the United Nations, with its headquarters in London.

2. The authority vested in IMO - and the decisive limitation

The Convention gives the Organization four purposes, and they define the whole of its authority:

  1. to provide machinery for cooperation among Governments in governmental regulation and practices relating to technical matters of all kinds affecting shipping engaged in international trade;
  2. to encourage and facilitate the general adoption of the highest practicable standards in matters of maritime safety, efficiency of navigation, and the prevention and control of marine pollution from ships;
  3. to deal with the related administrative and legal matters; and
  4. to encourage the removal of discriminatory action and unnecessary restrictions affecting international shipping.

Now the point that separates a good answer from a recital. The authority vested in IMO is legislative in form and wholly indirect in effect. The Organization:

  • adopts conventions, protocols, codes, guidelines and resolutions;
  • cannot enforce any of them against any ship. It has no inspectors, no ships, no power of detention and no power to prosecute;
  • regulates States, not vessels. An IMO instrument binds a State when that State accepts it, and it reaches a ship only through that State's own legislation and administration;
  • so enforcement rests with the flag State, the port State and the coastal State - which is exactly the allocation UNCLOS Part XII makes, and it is why UNCLOS repeatedly says that rules for ships shall be established "through the competent international organization".

That is why the audit scheme in the second limb exists at all: if the Organization cannot enforce, the only thing it can usefully examine is whether the State is discharging what it accepted.

3. The organs through which the authority is exercised

OrganCompositionWhat it does
AssemblyAll Member States; regular session every two yearsThe governing body. Approves the work programme and the budget, elects the Council, and adopts Assembly resolutions (the A. series)
CouncilForty Members elected by the Assembly, in three categories - the largest providers of international shipping services; the largest interested in seaborne trade; and States with special interests, elected to secure geographical representationThe executive organ. Supervises the work of the Organization between Assemblies, and appoints the Secretary-General subject to Assembly approval
Five main committeesOpen to all Member StatesMSC maritime safety; MEPC marine environment protection; Legal; Technical Cooperation; Facilitation. The MSC and MEPC are where the technical amendments are adopted
Sub-committeesReport to the MSC and MEPCDo the detailed technical work - carriage of cargoes and containers, ship design and construction, ship systems and equipment, navigation and communications, human element and training, pollution prevention and response, and implementation of IMO instruments
SecretariatHeaded by the Secretary-GeneralAdministration, depositary functions, and circulation of instruments

One current point worth a mark at this examination: amendments to the Convention that would enlarge the Council from 40 to 52 Members were adopted by the Assembly in December 2021, but they require acceptance by two thirds of Members and have not entered into force. Adopted is not in force - which is precisely the distinction the third part of this limb is testing.

4. The ratification process - briefly, as the question asks

  1. Adoption. The text is adopted either by the relevant IMO organ - the MSC or MEPC for the technical conventions - or by a diplomatic conference convened for the purpose. Adoption creates no obligation on anybody.
  2. Consent to be bound. A State expresses consent either by signature subject to ratification, acceptance or approval - a two-stage route, where signature indicates intent and the later instrument gives consent - or, where the convention is already closed for signature, by a single act of accession. Ratification and accession have identical legal effect; they differ only in whether the State signed first.
  3. Deposit. The instrument is deposited with the Secretary-General of IMO as depositary, who notifies all States.
  4. Entry into force. Every convention states its own conditions. For a convention of an important technical nature these are deliberately demanding and are set on two axes at once: a minimum number of Contracting States, and a minimum share of world merchant tonnage - and often a further period, typically twelve months, after both are met.
  5. Application. The State then gives the convention effect in national law and administers it. Until that happens the convention binds the State internationally but reaches no ship.

The tonnage condition is the feature to explain rather than merely state: a convention on ship construction is worthless if it binds only States with no ships. Requiring a tonnage share ensures that when the convention enters into force it governs a real proportion of the world fleet. The price is delay - as the Organization's own constitution showed, taking ten years to enter into force.

5. Keeping a convention current - tacit acceptance

Amendment by the same explicit process proved unworkably slow; some early amendments never gathered enough acceptances to enter into force at all. The technical conventions therefore use tacit acceptance: an amendment adopted by the committee is deemed accepted on a stated date and enters into force on a second stated date, unless objections are received from a specified number of Contracting Governments before the first date.

The burden is reversed - silence is consent. This is why SOLAS and MARPOL can be kept current, and it is also why an engineer must always check which amendment set was in force at a given date rather than relying on an edition on the shelf.

6. Short note - the Casualty Investigation Code

  • Full title, the Code of the International Standards and Recommended Practices for a Safety Investigation into a Marine Casualty or Marine Incident. Adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008.
  • It is mandatory, and the route is worth stating because it is the pattern described in limb a): the Code is not binding of itself - SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 requires each Administration to conduct investigations in accordance with the Code, and that made Parts I and II mandatory from 1 January 2010. The underlying duties to investigate are older still - SOLAS regulation I/21, MARPOL Article 12 and UNCLOS Article 94(7).
  • Structure: Part I general provisions and definitions; Part II mandatory standards; Part III recommended practices.
  • The obligation. A marine safety investigation must be conducted into every very serious marine casualty, and into other casualties and incidents where the flag State judges an investigation likely to provide information that could prevent recurrence.
  • A very serious marine casualty is one involving the total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment. That definition is worth carrying verbatim.
  • The defining principle. The sole objective is the prevention of future casualties. A marine safety investigation is separate and independent from, and must not prejudice, any investigation for the purpose of apportioning blame or determining liability - and it is not to be conducted with that purpose. Evidence given is protected accordingly.
  • Who investigates. The flag State is the marine safety investigating State, with provision for a substantially interested State to participate and for States to agree which shall lead. The report is submitted to the Organization.

7. Short note - the IMO Member State Audit Scheme

  • It audits the Member State, not the ship and not the company. That is what makes it unlike every other instrument on this paper, and it follows directly from the limitation described in step 2: since IMO cannot enforce against ships, the only thing it can examine is whether the State is capable of doing so.
  • It began as a voluntary scheme and became mandatory from 1 January 2016, by amendments to the instruments themselves - SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines 1966 and its 1988 Protocol, Tonnage 1969 and COLREG 1972. Every Member State is now subject to audit as a condition of membership of those conventions.
  • The audit standard is the III Code - the IMO Instruments Implementation Code, resolution A.1070(28) of 4 December 2013. The audit does not invent criteria; it measures the State against that Code.
  • What the III Code requires of a State: an overall strategy for discharging its responsibilities, with a methodology to monitor and assess that the strategy is delivering; national legislation giving full and complete effect to the instruments it has accepted; adequate enforcement and administrative arrangements; and a periodic evaluation of its own performance against performance indicators. The Code is organised into the three State roles - flag, coastal and port.
  • What the audit examines is therefore the State's law, its administration, its resourcing, its oversight of any recognized organizations it has delegated to, and its own performance measurement.
  • Outcome. The audit produces findings and observations, against which the audited State prepares a corrective action plan. The Organization uses the aggregated results to identify systemic weaknesses and to direct technical cooperation and capacity building - so the Scheme feeds back into the work programme rather than functioning as a penalty.
  • A supporting instrument at this examination is the Assembly's non-exhaustive list of obligations under instruments relevant to the III Code, which identifies the auditable areas. The list in force at this sitting is resolution A.1157(32).

8. What the two short notes have in common

Do not leave them as two disconnected paragraphs. Both are oversight mechanisms that answer the same structural weakness identified in the first limb - IMO makes rules it cannot enforce - and they attack it from opposite ends:

Casualty Investigation CodeMember State Audit Scheme
LooksBackwards, at a casualty that has happenedForwards, at whether the State can prevent one
SubjectThe ship and the eventThe State and its system
Standard appliedMSC.255(84)The III Code, A.1070(28)
Made mandatory bySOLAS XI-1/6, from 1 January 2010Amendments to SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG, from 1 January 2016
PurposePrevention, never blameCapability, and improvement through corrective action

And both illustrate the pattern of limb a) exactly: a resolution of the Organization acquires binding force not from its own words but from the convention that points at it.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

This is four questions inside one, and the commonest failure is not ignorance but budgeting. Limb a) prints three separate demands - trace the origin, elaborate on authority, explain briefly the ratification process - and limb B prints two short notes. That is five deliverables against sixteen marks, so nothing can be written at length and nothing can be omitted. A candidate who writes four beautiful pages on the ratification process and two lines on the audit scheme will score less than one who writes five adequate sections.

Note the printed command in each part. "Trace" invites a short chronology, so give dates. "Elaborate" is the heaviest verb on the question and is attached to authority, so that is where the argument belongs. "Briefly" is printed against ratification, and it is an instruction - obey it. "Short notes" means bullet-form substance, not essays.

The route also does one thing a list cannot: it makes step 2 - what IMO may and may not do - carry both limbs. The audit scheme in limb B exists because of that limitation, and saying so at step 8 turns five fragments into one answer.

The one idea that unlocks the whole question

IMO regulates States, never ships. It has no inspectors, no power of detention, no power to prosecute. Everything else follows: the ratification process exists because a State must accept an instrument before it binds anything; tacit acceptance exists because that process is too slow for technical amendment; the audit scheme exists because the State is the only thing the Organization can examine; and the Casualty Investigation Code binds only because SOLAS points at it.

If you can state that sentence and then show four consequences of it, you have answered a question most candidates answer as five disconnected recitals.

Dates that are worth carrying

  • 6 March 1948 - convention adopted at Geneva, creating IMCO
  • 17 March 1958 - entry into force, ten years later. Use this as your worked example of why ratification matters
  • January 1959 - first Assembly
  • 22 May 1982 - renamed International Maritime Organization
  • 16 May 2008 - MSC.255(84) adopts the Casualty Investigation Code
  • 1 January 2010 - the Code becomes mandatory through SOLAS XI-1/6
  • 4 December 2013 - A.1070(28) adopts the III Code
  • 1 January 2016 - the audit scheme becomes mandatory

Adopted, in force, and mandatory are three different things

This question rewards precision about status more than almost any other on the paper, and it offers a live example at this sitting. The amendments enlarging the IMO Council from 40 to 52 Members were adopted in December 2021 and require acceptance by two thirds of Members. At this examination they are not in force, so the Council is forty. Writing 52 is a factual error at this date; writing 40 and adding that the enlargement is adopted but awaiting acceptance is worth an extra mark and demonstrates the very distinction the ratification limb is testing.

The same discipline applies to the audit limb. The Assembly's non-exhaustive list of obligations at this sitting is A.1157(32). It was replaced by A.1187(33) on 6 December 2023 - six months after this examination. A candidate revising from a later paper would carry the wrong number. The III Code itself, A.1070(28), is unaffected: it was the lists that were replaced, not the Code.

The one line that must appear in the casualty note

That a marine safety investigation is separate and independent from, and must not prejudice, any investigation for the purpose of apportioning blame or determining liability. Its sole object is prevention. Examiners ask this because it is counter-intuitive to anyone who thinks of an investigation as finding fault, and because it explains why evidence given to a safety investigation is protected. If you carry only one sentence from the Code, carry that one - and pair it with the definition of a very serious marine casualty: total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment.

Common mistakes

  • Answering "authority vested in IMO" by listing the organs. Structure is not authority; the organs are how the authority is exercised.
  • Saying or implying that IMO enforces its conventions, inspects ships or detains them. It does none of these.
  • Writing that the Council has 52 Members. The enlargement was adopted in December 2021 and is not in force at this sitting.
  • Confusing ratification with accession - or treating them as legally different in effect. They differ only in whether the State signed first.
  • Saying entry into force depends only on a number of States. For a technical convention it also depends on a share of world tonnage.
  • Describing tacit acceptance as applying to the entry into force of a convention. It is the amendment procedure.
  • Writing the two short notes at essay length and running out of time before limb a) is finished - or the reverse.
  • Saying the audit scheme audits ships or companies. It audits the Member State.
  • Saying the audit scheme is still voluntary. It has been mandatory since 1 January 2016.
  • Citing A.1187(33) for the list of obligations. That is six months future at this sitting; the list here is A.1157(32).
  • Presenting the Casualty Investigation Code as a blame-finding instrument.

Examiner traps

  • "What did the C in IMCO stand for, and why was it dropped?" Consultative. Its removal in 1982 marks the shift from advisory forum to rule-making body.
  • "Can IMO detain a ship?" No. It has no enforcement power over any ship whatever.
  • "Why did the IMO Convention take ten years to enter into force?" Because it had to be ratified by a set number of States including several with substantial tonnage - the same mechanism the limb asks you to explain.
  • "Difference between ratification and accession?" Only whether the State signed first. Legal effect is identical.
  • "Who is the depositary?" The Secretary-General of IMO.
  • "Under tacit acceptance, what does silence mean?" Consent. The burden is reversed.
  • "What is the audit standard?" The III Code, A.1070(28) - not the conventions themselves.
  • "Is a safety investigation report admissible to establish liability?" The investigation is separate and independent from any liability proceeding and must not prejudice it; that separation is the Code's governing principle.
  • "Which State investigates?" The flag State as marine safety investigating State, with provision for substantially interested States to participate.

Likely oral follow-up

  • When and where was IMO founded, and when did the convention enter into force? Geneva, 6 March 1948; in force 17 March 1958.
  • What was it originally called and when did the name change? IMCO; renamed 22 May 1982.
  • State two purposes of the Organization. Machinery for cooperation on technical matters affecting international shipping; adoption of the highest practicable standards for safety, efficiency of navigation and prevention of pollution from ships.
  • How many Members has the Council and in what categories? Forty, in three categories; the enlargement to 52 is adopted and not in force.
  • Name the five main committees. MSC, MEPC, Legal, Technical Cooperation, Facilitation.
  • How does a State become bound? Signature subject to ratification, acceptance or approval, or accession; instrument deposited with the Secretary-General.
  • On what two axes are entry-into-force conditions set for a technical convention? Number of States and share of world merchant tonnage.
  • What is the Casualty Investigation Code and how is it mandatory? MSC.255(84) of 16 May 2008, made mandatory by SOLAS XI-1/6 from 1 January 2010.
  • Define a very serious marine casualty. Total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment.
  • What does the Member State Audit Scheme audit, and against what? The Member State's implementation of the instruments it is party to, against the III Code, A.1070(28); mandatory since 1 January 2016.

Memory framework

Limb a) is three beats: BORN - POWERS - BINDING. Born gives you 1948, 1958, 1982. Powers gives you the one sentence that carries the question - the Organization makes rules for States and cannot touch a ship. Binding gives you adopt, consent, deposit, enter into force, legislate.

For the ratification chain itself: ADOPT - ACCEPT - DEPOSIT - FORCE - LAW. For the entry-into-force test, COUNT AND TONNAGE. For amendment, SILENCE IS CONSENT.

For limb B, remember the two notes as a pair pulling in opposite directions: BACK versus FORWARD - the Code looks back at a casualty on a ship, the Scheme looks forward at the capability of a State. And each is mandatory only because a convention points at it.

Regulation and source map

  • Convention on the International Maritime Organization - adopted Geneva 6 March 1948 as the IMCO Convention; in force 17 March 1958; renamed IMO 22 May 1982
  • Resolution A.1152(32), December 2021 - Council enlargement from 40 to 52; requires acceptance by two thirds of Members; NOT in force at this sitting
  • MSC.255(84), 16 May 2008 - the Casualty Investigation Code; Parts I, II and III
  • SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 - makes the Code mandatory from 1 January 2010; SOLAS regulation I/21; MARPOL Article 12; UNCLOS Article 94(7)
  • Resolution A.1070(28), 4 December 2013 - the III Code, revoking A.1054(27); the audit standard
  • A.1083(28), A.1084(28), A.1085(28) - Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG amendments making the audit mandatory
  • MSC.366(93), MSC.373(93), MSC.374(93), MSC.375(93) - SOLAS, STCW, STCW Code and the 1988 Load Line Protocol
  • MEPC.246(66), MEPC.247(66) - MARPOL and its 1997 Protocol
  • Resolution A.1157(32) - the non-exhaustive list of obligations, operative at this sitting
  • IMO Member State Audit Scheme - mandatory from 1 January 2016

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The two short notes rest on the stronger evidence. The Casualty Investigation Code was consulted in the held resolution MSC.255(84), and the SOLAS, MARPOL and UNCLOS bases and the Code's definitions come from MIW's own verification record for a question where they were read. The III Code, A.1070(28), and the whole mandating chain come from MIW verification records for two sittings of the III Code question, one of them August 2023, two months after this examination and therefore in the same regulatory position.

Limb a) is weaker and says so. MIW holds no copy of the Convention on the International Maritime Organization. The 1948 adoption, the 1958 entry into force, the 1982 renaming, the purposes of the Organization and the composition of the organs are stated from authoritative secondary description. No article of the IMO Convention is cited by number and no text of it is quoted. Likewise MIW holds no resolution setting out the Framework and Procedures for the Audit Scheme, so the Scheme is described from what is verified - the III Code as its standard and the mandating chain - and no resolution number is asserted for the Framework, and no audit periodicity is claimed.

Two currency instructions for a later editor. Do not update the Council to 52 Members unless it is established that the A.1152(32) amendments had entered into force by June 2023 - they had not. And do not replace A.1157(32) with A.1187(33): the later list was adopted on 6 December 2023, six months after this sitting. Note carefully that only the lists were replaced; the III Code itself is unaffected, and an editor who reverses the Code's own number has misunderstood the change.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a The origin - from a 1948 conference to the Organization of today
  2. __________a The authority vested in IMO - and the decisive limitation
  3. __________a The organs through which the authority is exercised
  4. __________a The ratification process - briefly, as the question asks
  5. __________a Keeping a convention current - tacit acceptance
  6. __________B Short note - the Casualty Investigation Code
  7. __________B Short note - the IMO Member State Audit Scheme
  8. __________B What the two short notes have in common
Flashcards 12

1 The origin, 1948 to 1982. 2 The authority, and the limitation that IMO cannot enforce against ships. 3 The organs. 4 The ratification process, briefly. 5 Tacit acceptance. 6 Short note: Casualty Investigation Code. 7 Short note: Member State Audit Scheme. 8 What the two notes have in common.

Five printed deliverables; the route exists to stop one of them eating the others.

6 March 1948 - the convention establishing IMCO is adopted at the UN Maritime Conference in Geneva. 17 March 1958 - it enters into force, ten years later. January 1959 - the first Assembly meets. 22 May 1982 - the Organization is renamed the International Maritime Organization.

'Trace' invites a chronology, and the ten-year gap is the worked example the ratification limb needs.

Authority to provide machinery for cooperation among Governments on technical matters affecting international shipping, and to encourage and facilitate the adoption of the highest practicable standards for maritime safety, efficiency of navigation and prevention of pollution from ships, with the related administrative and legal matters. It is legislative in form and wholly indirect in effect: IMO adopts instruments but cannot enforce any of them against any ship. It regulates States, and enforcement rests with flag, port and coastal States.

Answering this with a list of organs is the commonest way to lose the heaviest command verb on the question.

Forty, elected in three categories. Resolution A.1152(32) of December 2021 adopted amendments enlarging it to 52, but they require acceptance by two thirds of Members and had not entered into force at this examination. Adopted is not in force.

It is a factual error at this date and it is also the exact distinction the ratification limb is testing.

The text is adopted by the relevant IMO organ or by a diplomatic conference, which binds nobody. A State then consents to be bound by signature subject to ratification, acceptance or approval, or by accession where signature is closed - both have identical legal effect. The instrument is deposited with the Secretary-General. The convention enters into force on its own conditions, which for a technical convention require both a number of Contracting States and a share of world merchant tonnage. The State then legislates.

The stem prints 'briefly', so this is the whole of it - five beats, no more.

Because a convention regulating ship construction or operation is worthless if it binds only States with few or no ships. Requiring a share of world merchant tonnage ensures that when it enters into force it governs a real proportion of the world fleet. The price is delay - the IMO Convention itself took ten years.

Explaining the reason rather than reciting the condition is what lifts this limb.

An amendment adopted by the committee is deemed accepted on a stated date and enters into force on a second stated date, unless objections are received from a specified number of Contracting Governments before the first. It was introduced because explicit amendment was so slow that some amendments never gathered enough acceptances to enter into force at all. The burden is reversed - silence is consent.

It is the mechanism that keeps SOLAS and MARPOL current, and it explains why the amendment set in force at a date must always be checked.

The Code of the International Standards and Recommended Practices for a Safety Investigation into a Marine Casualty or Marine Incident, adopted by resolution MSC.255(84) on 16 May 2008. It is not binding of itself: SOLAS regulation XI-1/6 requires each Administration to conduct investigations in accordance with it, which made Parts I and II mandatory from 1 January 2010. The older underlying duties are SOLAS I/21, MARPOL Article 12 and UNCLOS Article 94(7).

It is the worked example of the pattern in limb a) - a resolution binds because a convention points at it.

Its sole objective is the prevention of future casualties. It is separate and independent from, and must not prejudice, any investigation for the purpose of apportioning blame or determining liability, and it is not conducted for that purpose. A very serious marine casualty is one involving the total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment.

Counter-intuitive to anyone who thinks an investigation finds fault, and it explains why evidence to a safety investigation is protected.

It audits the Member State - its law, administration, resourcing, oversight of recognized organizations and its own performance measurement - and not ships or companies. The standard applied is the IMO Instruments Implementation Code, resolution A.1070(28) of 4 December 2013. It began voluntary and became mandatory on 1 January 2016 through amendments to SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines and its 1988 Protocol, Tonnage and COLREG.

Saying it audits ships, or that it is still voluntary, are the two standard errors.

A.1157(32). The later list, A.1187(33), was adopted on 6 December 2023 - six months after this examination - and must not be cited here. Note that only the lists were replaced: the III Code itself, A.1070(28), stands unrevoked, so reversing the Code's own number is an error in the opposite direction.

Material revised from a 2024 or later paper carries the 33rd Assembly numbers throughout.

Both are oversight mechanisms answering the same weakness - IMO makes rules it cannot enforce. The Code looks backwards at a casualty on a ship, for prevention and never for blame; the Scheme looks forwards at whether a State is capable of preventing one, and produces corrective action. Both are mandatory only because conventions point at them.

It converts two disconnected short notes into a single argument and ties limb B back to limb a).

Quick revision
15-second recall

In force 17 March 1958 (ten years - your own worked example of ratification). First Assembly January 1959. Renamed International Maritime Organization, 22 May 1982 - 'Consultative' dropped. Amendment by tacit acceptance: deemed accepted unless enough object - silence is consent. CASUALTY INVESTIGATION CODE. Investigation mandatory for a very serious marine casualty = total loss, a death, or severe damage to the environment. Sole object is PREVENTION; separate and independent from any blame or liability proceeding. Flag State investigates; substantially interested States may join. MEMBER STATE AUDIT SCHEME. Audits the STATE. Voluntary, then mandatory 1 January 2016 via amendments to SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG. Audit standard = III Code, A.1070(28) of 4 Dec 2013. Produces findings → corrective action plan. Auditable-obligations list at this sitting: A.1157(32).
