Critical regulation
The Casualty Investigation Code, resolution MSC.255(84) of 16 May 2008, is made mandatory by SOLAS regulation XI-1/6, which requires each Administration to conduct investigations of marine casualties and marine incidents in accordance with the Code, with effect from 1 January 2010. The IMO Member State Audit Scheme became mandatory on 1 January 2016 through amendments to SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, the Load Lines Convention and its 1988 Protocol, the Tonnage Convention and COLREG, and applies the IMO Instruments Implementation Code, resolution A.1070(28) of 4 December 2013, as its audit standard.
Major trap
Budgeting. Five printed deliverables against sixteen marks - trace the origin, elaborate on authority, explain the ratification process briefly, and two short notes - and the usual failure is writing two of them well and running out of time. The substantive traps are: answering "authority" with a description of the organs, when the answer is that IMO regulates States and cannot enforce anything against a ship; writing the Council as 52 Members, when the enlargement adopted in December 2021 is not in force at this sitting; saying the audit scheme is voluntary or that it audits ships, when it has audited States compulsorily since 1 January 2016 against the III Code; citing A.1187(33) for the list of obligations, which is six months future here - the list is A.1157(32); and presenting the Casualty Investigation Code as a blame-finding instrument when its governing principle is that it is separate from and must not prejudice any liability proceeding.

Also on the platform: QP2306 Q1 - UNCLOS article 211, which is the provision that makes IMO the 'competent international organization' · QP2306 Q4 - the ISM Code, a worked example of an instrument made mandatory by the route described here · QP2306 Q7 - the SEEMP guidelines, a worked example of a resolution that is binding only because a convention points at it

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

Both halves of this question are really about one boundary: where the cover on the ship herself ends and the cover on the owner's liabilities to other people begins. A hull policy is fundamentally a policy on a piece of property. It makes one deliberate exception, taking on the owner's liability when his ship hits another ship - and even then it pays only three quarters of it, because the underwriters wanted the owner to keep some of his own money at stake so he would fight the claim properly. Everything the clause leaves out - striking a pier rather than a ship, wreck removal, injury to people, oil in the water, the cargo in his own holds - belongs to the mutual association that covers the residue of his liabilities. General average is a different animal that ends up at the same boundary: an old rule that when something is deliberately given up or spent to save everybody's property from a common danger, everybody whose property was saved shares the cost. The hull policy pays the ship's own share of that; the club steps in where the sharing breaks down, typically because the owner's own failings have given the cargo owners a good reason not to pay. The second half then asks a question about time and about what a loss is actually worth. Damage belongs to the year it happened in, not the year somebody noticed it. And if the owner decides to live with it because nothing important is affected, he is not paid what the repair would have cost - he is paid what the damage has knocked off the value of the ship, which may be very much less, and which may be nothing at all if a buyer would not care.

Knowledge map
Running Down Clause, General Average Expenses, and a Claim for Unrepaired Damage
  1. a
    1. 1Where these two clauses live, and why the question pairs them
      • the RUNNING DOWN CLAUSE is a clause in a HULL AND MACHINERY POLICY - the Institute clauses head it '3/4ths Collision Liability'
      • THE RUNNING DOWN CLAUSE AND THE 3/4THS COLLISION LIABILITY CLAUSE ARE THE SAME PROVISION - old market name, modern policy heading
      • GENERAL AVERAGE is NOT a policy clause - it is a rule of maritime law given effect by the York-Antwerp Rules, a CONTRACTUAL code
    2. 2The Running Down Clause - what the hull policy covers
      • the hull policy is primarily a PROPERTY policy; this is the one place it takes on third-party LIABILITY
      • on collision WITH ANOTHER VESSEL, underwriters indemnify THREE FOURTHS of sums paid for:
      • loss of or damage to the OTHER vessel or property on board her
    3. 3What the Running Down Clause excludes, and where the rest goes
      • the exclusions are almost item for item THE P&I BOOK
      • EXCLUDED: removal of obstructions and wreck
      • EXCLUDED: FIXED AND FLOATING OBJECTS and any property other than the other vessel
    4. 4General Average Expenses - what they are
      • Rule A - an EXTRAORDINARY SACRIFICE OR EXPENDITURE, INTENTIONALLY AND REASONABLY made or incurred FOR THE COMMON SAFETY, to preserve from peril the property in a common maritime adventure
      • SACRIFICE is property given up - jettison, machinery damaged refloating, fire-fighting damage
      • EXPENDITURE is money spent - salvage or towage for the common safety, and above all PORT OF REFUGE EXPENSES
    5. 5How H&M and P&I each treat a general average claim
      • GA SACRIFICE OF THE SHIP - paid IN FULL by hull underwriters as damage to the insured property
      • THE SHIP'S PROPORTION of GA contribution and of salvage charges - paid under the hull policy's general average and salvage clause
      • IF THE SHIP IS UNDER-INSURED the hull policy pays only the proportion the insured value bears to the CONTRIBUTORY value; the balance is the owner's
  2. b
    1. 6Damage found in dry dock a year later - is it admissible?
      • YES IN PRINCIPLE, provided the assured can prove it
      • A CLAIM ATTACHES TO THE POLICY ON RISK WHEN THE DAMAGE WAS SUSTAINED, NOT WHEN IT WAS DISCOVERED
      • the BURDEN OF PROOF is on the assured - an insured peril, during that policy period, on the balance of probabilities
    2. 7The unrepaired damage claim, and how its value is ascertained
      • HE MAY STILL CLAIM - an indemnity policy does not require the assured to spend the money first
      • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 section 69(3): where the ship has NOT been repaired and has not been sold in her damaged state during the risk, the assured is indemnified for the REASONABLE DEPRECIATION arising from the unrepaired damage, NOT EXCEEDING the reasonable cost of repairing it
      • the hull clause restates it as DEPRECIATION IN MARKET VALUE AT THE TIME THE INSURANCE TERMINATES, capped at repair cost and at the INSURED VALUE
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

a
  1. Where these two clauses live, and why the question pairs them
  2. The Running Down Clause - what the hull policy covers
  3. What the Running Down Clause excludes, and where the rest goes
  4. General Average Expenses - what they are
  5. How H&M and P&I each treat a general average claim
b
  1. Damage found in dry dock a year later - is it admissible?
  2. The unrepaired damage claim, and how its value is ascertained

Memory cue. Two lines carry limb a): a policy clause versus a rule of law, and both mark WHERE HULL STOPS AND CLUB BEGINS (1). The clause pays DAMAGE, DELAY, SALVAGE - OF THE OTHER SHIP (2); it refuses WRECK, OBJECTS, OWN CARGO, PEOPLE, POLLUTION (3). General average is SACRIFICE versus EXPENDITURE (4), and the split is SHIP'S SHARE to hull, SHORTFALL and UNRECOVERABLE to club (5). Limb b) is two questions in order: SUSTAINED NOT DISCOVERED (6), then DEPRECIATION CAPPED AT REPAIR COST (7) - and the killer, NO TOTAL LOSS IN THE PERIOD (7).

Remember7 route headings
Cover54 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 54 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Where these two clauses live, and why the question pairs them
  • the RUNNING DOWN CLAUSE is a clause in a HULL AND MACHINERY POLICY - the Institute clauses head it '3/4ths Collision Liability'
  • THE RUNNING DOWN CLAUSE AND THE 3/4THS COLLISION LIABILITY CLAUSE ARE THE SAME PROVISION - old market name, modern policy heading
  • GENERAL AVERAGE is NOT a policy clause - it is a rule of maritime law given effect by the York-Antwerp Rules, a CONTRACTUAL code
  • the policy then decides how much of the resulting liability the insurer pays
  • BOTH ARE PLACES WHERE THE HULL POLICY STOPS AND THE CLUB BEGINS - which is why the examiner pairs them
2. The Running Down Clause - what the hull policy covers
  • the hull policy is primarily a PROPERTY policy; this is the one place it takes on third-party LIABILITY
  • on collision WITH ANOTHER VESSEL, underwriters indemnify THREE FOURTHS of sums paid for:
  • loss of or damage to the OTHER vessel or property on board her
  • DELAY TO OR LOSS OF USE of that other vessel or her property
  • GENERAL AVERAGE, SALVAGE OR SALVAGE UNDER CONTRACT of that other vessel
  • it responds IN ADDITION to any claim for damage to the insured vessel herself
  • limited by reference to the insured value; legal costs of contesting liability with underwriters' consent
  • adjusted on CROSS-LIABILITIES normally; SINGLE LIABILITY where one or both owners limit
  • the quarter is left uninsured DELIBERATELY - co-insurance to keep the owner interested in the defence
3. What the Running Down Clause excludes, and where the rest goes
  • the exclusions are almost item for item THE P&I BOOK
  • EXCLUDED: removal of obstructions and wreck
  • EXCLUDED: FIXED AND FLOATING OBJECTS and any property other than the other vessel
  • EXCLUDED: cargo or other property on the INSURED vessel, and the assured's own engagements
  • EXCLUDED: loss of life, personal injury or illness
  • EXCLUDED: pollution or contamination other than damage to the other vessel or property on her
  • A SHIP THAT STRIKES A JETTY HAS NO CLAIM AT ALL UNDER THIS CLAUSE - it responds only to vessel-to-vessel collision
  • the club therefore has TWO roles - it insures the missing QUARTER and it insures the EXCLUDED HEADS IN FULL
  • some modern forms provide FOUR FOURTHS; the policy wording governs
4. General Average Expenses - what they are
  • Rule A - an EXTRAORDINARY SACRIFICE OR EXPENDITURE, INTENTIONALLY AND REASONABLY made or incurred FOR THE COMMON SAFETY, to preserve from peril the property in a common maritime adventure
  • SACRIFICE is property given up - jettison, machinery damaged refloating, fire-fighting damage
  • EXPENDITURE is money spent - salvage or towage for the common safety, and above all PORT OF REFUGE EXPENSES
  • port of refuge: putting in, discharging, storing and reloading cargo, crew wages and maintenance, fuel and stores during detention
  • shared rateably between SHIP, CARGO AND FREIGHT on CONTRIBUTORY VALUES ascertained by an AVERAGE ADJUSTER - no formula or figure given
  • contrast PARTICULAR AVERAGE - accidental partial loss that lies where it falls
  • which York-Antwerp edition applies - 1994, 2004 or 2016 - is a matter of the CONTRACT, not the date
5. How H&M and P&I each treat a general average claim
  • GA SACRIFICE OF THE SHIP - paid IN FULL by hull underwriters as damage to the insured property
  • THE SHIP'S PROPORTION of GA contribution and of salvage charges - paid under the hull policy's general average and salvage clause
  • IF THE SHIP IS UNDER-INSURED the hull policy pays only the proportion the insured value bears to the CONTRIBUTORY value; the balance is the owner's
  • CARGO'S PROPORTION IRRECOVERABLE because the owner is in breach of the contract of carriage - typically UNSEAWORTHINESS - is UNRECOVERABLE GENERAL AVERAGE and is the CLUB'S classic exposure
  • cargo's own share of a cargo sacrifice falls to cargo's underwriters through the adjustment
  • THE ORGANISING SENTENCE: the hull underwriter pays the ship's own share; the club pays where the general average machinery FAILS the owner
  • Rule D preserves the right to contribution notwithstanding fault but leaves remedies against the party at fault intact - which is the gap the club fills
6. Damage found in dry dock a year later - is it admissible?
  • YES IN PRINCIPLE, provided the assured can prove it
  • A CLAIM ATTACHES TO THE POLICY ON RISK WHEN THE DAMAGE WAS SUSTAINED, NOT WHEN IT WAS DISCOVERED
  • the BURDEN OF PROOF is on the assured - an insured peril, during that policy period, on the balance of probabilities
  • evidence: dry-dock survey, photographs, deck and engine logs, the original incident report, the previous docking or in-water survey, class records
  • NOTICE - hull policies require prompt notice so underwriters may appoint a surveyor; failure attracts a REDUCTION FROM THE ASCERTAINED CLAIM. No percentage asserted
  • this is where a late claim is really prejudiced - not admissibility but the deduction and the lost survey
  • time: one year is ordinarily well within any limitation or contractual suit-time period, but check the policy and the applicable law
  • the DEDUCTIBLE applies per accident or occurrence
7. The unrepaired damage claim, and how its value is ascertained
  • HE MAY STILL CLAIM - an indemnity policy does not require the assured to spend the money first
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 section 69(3): where the ship has NOT been repaired and has not been sold in her damaged state during the risk, the assured is indemnified for the REASONABLE DEPRECIATION arising from the unrepaired damage, NOT EXCEEDING the reasonable cost of repairing it
  • the hull clause restates it as DEPRECIATION IN MARKET VALUE AT THE TIME THE INSURANCE TERMINATES, capped at repair cost and at the INSURED VALUE
  • NO LIABILITY AT ALL for unrepaired damage where a SUBSEQUENT TOTAL LOSS is sustained during the period, whether or not covered
  • ASCERTAINMENT STEP 1 - obtain repair specifications and yard quotations; this fixes the CEILING
  • ASCERTAINMENT STEP 2 - value the vessel SOUND and DAMAGED at the same date, by an independent valuer or broker against comparable sales. The difference is the depreciation
  • ASCERTAINMENT STEP 3 - test it against what a willing BUYER would deduct. CLASS UNAFFECTED AND EARNING CAPACITY UNIMPAIRED MEANS THE DEPRECIATION MAY BE SMALL OR NIL
  • ASCERTAINMENT STEP 4 - apply the caps; STEP 5 - settle by agreement, or repair at the next docking and claim the cost instead
  • two warnings: a total loss in the period destroys the claim; and SUBSEQUENT DETERIORATION is not an insured peril
Model written answer

1. Where these two clauses live, and why the question pairs them

The two are not the same kind of thing, and saying so is the opening mark.

  • The Running Down Clause (RDC) is a clause in a hull and machinery policy. In the Institute Time Clauses - Hulls it appears under the heading "3/4ths Collision Liability"; "Running Down Clause" is the older market name for the same provision. They are the same clause.
  • General average is not a policy clause at all. It is a rule of maritime law, given effect in practice by the York-Antwerp Rules, which are a contractual code incorporated by the contract of affreightment. The policy then decides how much of the resulting liability the insurer pays.

The examiner pairs them because both are places where the hull policy stops and the P&I club begins. The question "how are claims treated by H&M as well as P&I" is therefore one question asked twice, and the answer in each case is about where the line falls.

2. The Running Down Clause - what the hull policy covers

The hull policy is primarily a property policy on the assured's own ship. The collision liability clause is the one place where it also takes on liability to a third party. Where the insured vessel comes into collision with another vessel and the assured becomes liable in damages, underwriters indemnify him for three fourths of the sums paid in respect of:

  1. loss of or damage to the other vessel or property on board her;
  2. delay to or loss of use of that other vessel or her property;
  3. general average, salvage, or salvage under contract of that other vessel or her property.

Four features are worth stating explicitly because each carries a mark:

  • It responds in addition to any claim for damage to the insured vessel herself - the two are separate recoveries under one policy.
  • The clause is limited by reference to the insured value; it is not open-ended.
  • It ordinarily also covers the assured's legal costs of contesting liability, with underwriters' consent.
  • Adjustment is normally on the cross-liabilities principle - each ship's liability to the other is calculated in full and then set off - rather than on the single balance actually paid. The exception is where one or both owners are entitled to limit liability, when the single-liability basis applies.

One historical point explains the fraction. Underwriters deliberately left one quarter uninsured so that the shipowner retained a real financial interest in defending the claim and in navigating prudently. It is the classic co-insurance device.

3. What the Running Down Clause excludes, and where the rest goes

This is the heart of the printed question, because the exclusions are, almost item for item, the P&I book.

Head of liabilityH&M under the collision clauseP&I club
Damage to the other vessel and property on board herThree fourthsThe remaining one fourth
Loss of use / delay of the other vesselThree fourthsThe remaining one fourth
General average and salvage of the other vesselThree fourthsThe remaining one fourth
Removal of obstructions or wreck, and the wreck of the other shipExcludedCovered in full
Damage to fixed and floating objects - piers, jetties, buoys, cables, moorings - and to any property other than the other vesselExcluded (this clause responds only to vessel-to-vessel collision)Covered in full
Cargo or other property on the insured vessel, and the assured's own engagementsExcludedCovered in full
Loss of life, personal injury or illnessExcludedCovered in full
Pollution or contamination of any kind, other than damage to the other vessel or the property on herExcludedCovered in full

Two consequences the examiner is looking for:

  1. A ship that strikes a jetty has no claim at all under the collision liability clause, however large the damage, because the clause responds only to collision with another vessel. The whole liability is the club's. This is the single most common misunderstanding of the RDC.
  2. The P&I club therefore performs two distinct functions here: it insures the one fourth the hull policy does not, and it insures the excluded heads in full. Saying both is worth more than saying either.

Note also that some modern hull forms - and many owners by special agreement - provide four fourths collision liability, in which case the club's first function disappears but the second remains. The policy wording governs.

4. General Average Expenses - what they are

The stem does not ask for a definition of general average, so state it compactly and move to the treatment. Under Rule A of the York-Antwerp Rules there is a general average act when, and only when, any extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure is intentionally and reasonably made or incurred for the common safety, for the purpose of preserving from peril the property involved in a common maritime adventure.

  • A general average sacrifice is property given up - cargo jettisoned, machinery damaged in refloating, fire-fighting damage to sound cargo.
  • General average expenditure, which is what this question names, is money spent - salvage or towage engaged for the common safety, and above all port of refuge expenses: the cost of putting in, discharging, storing and reloading cargo, wages and maintenance of crew and fuel and stores consumed during the detention.
  • The loss is then shared rateably between ship, cargo and freight at risk, in proportion to contributory values ascertained by an average adjuster at the termination of the adventure. No formula or figure is given here - contributory values are case-specific.
  • The critical contrast is with particular average: accidental, uncompensated partial loss that lies where it falls, borne by the owner of the property damaged and by his own insurer alone.

Which edition of the York-Antwerp Rules applies - 1994, 2004 or 2016 - is a matter of the contract of affreightment, not of the date. All three are in use, and an answer that asserts one universally is wrong.

5. How H&M and P&I each treat a general average claim

What arisesHull and machinery underwriterP&I club
General average sacrifice of the ship - machinery damaged in refloating, structure damaged fighting a firePaid in full as damage to the insured property, without waiting for the adjustmentNot engaged
The ship's proportion of general average contribution, and of salvage chargesPaid, under the general average and salvage clause of the hull policyNot engaged, save as below
The ship is under-insured - the insured value is less than the contributory valuePays only the proportion the insured value bears to the contributory value. The balance is the owner'sSome clubs cover the shortfall by rule or by special cover; it is not automatic
Cargo's proportion cannot be recovered because the owner is in breach of the contract of carriage - typically unseaworthiness - so cargo successfully resists contributionNot engaged. The hull policy insures the ship's share, not the owner's failure to recover others'This is the club's classic exposure - unrecoverable general average, together with the owner's liability for the cargo damage that produced the breach
Cargo's proportion of a sacrifice of cargoCargo's own underwriters, through the adjustmentNot engaged

The organising sentence, and it is the answer to the printed question: the hull underwriter pays the ship's own share of general average; the club pays where the general average machinery fails the owner - because he cannot enforce contribution, or because his own breach of contract has destroyed the right to it. Rule D preserves the right to contribution notwithstanding fault, but expressly leaves intact any remedy against the party at fault, which is exactly the gap the club fills.

6. Damage found in dry dock a year later - is it admissible?

Yes, in principle - provided the assured can prove it. The governing point is a distinction the question is built to test:

  • A claim attaches to the policy that was on risk when the damage was SUSTAINED, not to the policy in force when it was DISCOVERED. A marine policy responds to a fortuity occurring during its currency; late discovery does not defeat the claim. So the claim lies against the underwriters on risk at the date of the incident, even though that policy year has since closed.
  • The burden of proof is on the assured, and it is a real burden at this remove. He must show, on the balance of probabilities, that the damage was caused by an insured peril and that it occurred during that policy period. After a year, and after a further year's trading, both are harder to establish - which is a practical answer worth giving.
  • Evidence. The dry-dock survey report, photographs, the deck and engine log entries and any incident report for the original casualty, the previous dry-docking or in-water survey showing the area sound, and class records. The best evidence is a documented incident already notified.
  • Notice. Hull policies require prompt notice so that underwriters may appoint their own surveyor before repairs are put in hand. Where notice is not given in time to allow that survey, the clauses provide for a reduction from the ascertained claim. This is where a late-discovered claim is most often prejudiced - not by admissibility but by the deduction and by the loss of the underwriter's opportunity to survey.
  • Time. A claim identified a year after the incident will ordinarily be well within any applicable limitation or contractual suit-time period, but the assured must check both the policy and the applicable limitation law.
  • Deductible. The policy deductible applies to each accident or occurrence, so the claim is only worth pursuing if the damage exceeds it.

So the answer to the first part of limb b) is: admissible against the policy on risk at the time of the incident, subject to proof of an insured peril within that period, and subject to any deduction for want of notice.

7. The unrepaired damage claim, and how its value is ascertained

The owner now elects not to repair, because Class is unaffected. He may still claim. An indemnity policy does not require the assured to spend the money before he is indemnified - it requires him to have suffered a loss.

The measure is statutory and is restated in the hull clauses. Under the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, section 69 - and the identically worded section of the Marine Insurance Act 1906 from which the Indian Act is drawn:

  • where the ship has been repaired, the assured recovers the reasonable cost of repairs, less the customary deductions;
  • where she has been partially repaired, the cost of those repairs plus depreciation for the unrepaired part;
  • where she has NOT been repaired and has not been sold in her damaged state during the risk, the assured is entitled to be indemnified for the REASONABLE DEPRECIATION arising from the unrepaired damage, BUT NOT EXCEEDING the reasonable cost of repairing such damage.

The Unrepaired Damage clause of the hull policy restates this and adds two limits:

  1. the measure is the reasonable depreciation in the market value of the vessel at the time the insurance terminates, capped at the reasonable cost of repairs;
  2. underwriters are not liable for unrepaired damage at all in the event of a subsequent TOTAL LOSS - whether or not that total loss is covered - sustained during the period of the insurance or any extension of it; and liability is in any event capped at the insured value.

How the value is ascertained in practice:

  1. Establish the repair cost first. Obtain repair specifications and yard quotations for the damage as surveyed. This fixes the ceiling on the claim and is the figure the adjuster starts from.
  2. Then establish depreciation, which is the actual measure. This is a valuation exercise, not an engineering one: the vessel is valued in her sound condition and again with the damage disclosed, at the same date, by an independent valuer or broker with reference to comparable sales in the market for that type, age and trade. The difference is the depreciation.
  3. Test it against a purchaser's view. The question is what a willing buyer would deduct knowing the damage exists - which is why unaffected Class status matters so much to the answer: if Class is unaffected and the damage does not impair earning capacity, a buyer may deduct little or nothing, and the depreciation - and therefore the claim - may be small or even nil.
  4. Apply the caps. The recovery is the depreciation, but never more than the reasonable cost of repair and never more than the insured value.
  5. Settle by agreement or by adjustment. In practice underwriters and the owner very often compromise on an agreed figure, or the owner elects to repair at the next scheduled docking and claim the repair cost instead.

Two warnings to close on, because they are what makes this a decision rather than a formality. If the vessel becomes a total loss within the same policy period the unrepaired damage claim is lost entirely - so deferring the repair transfers a real risk to the owner. And if the damage is left and later worsens, the additional deterioration is not an insured peril; underwriters answer for the damage as it stood at the casualty, not for the consequences of the owner's decision to live with it.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

This is one of the few questions on the paper that prints its own mark split - (8) and (8) - so the budget is fixed and there is no excuse for spending twelve marks' worth of writing on general average. Limb a) contains three demands: define the Running Down Clause, define general average expenses, and say how claims under each are treated by H&M as well as P&I. The third demand is the one that carries the marks, and it is the one most candidates answer in a single sentence after two long definitions.

The route therefore front-loads the definitions to a minimum and gives two tables to the allocation - one for collision liability, one for general average. A table is the right form because the examiner's question is a mapping question: for each head of loss, which market pays? Prose obscures that; a table makes every row a countable proposition.

Limb b) is two legal questions wearing one paragraph, and separating them is the whole skill. First, is it admissible - which turns on when the damage was sustained, not when it was found. Second, what is the measure of indemnity if it is not repaired - which turns on the depreciation rule. A candidate who runs them together answers only the second and loses half the limb.

The name is the trap

Many candidates have learned the modern clause heading, 3/4ths Collision Liability, and have never met the older market name. Others know "Running Down Clause" from an oral and do not recognise it in a policy. They are the same provision. Say so in your first line; it costs six words and it tells the examiner immediately that you know what you are describing.

The exclusion that decides the question

The collision liability clause responds only to collision with ANOTHER VESSEL. Strike a jetty, a buoy, a submarine cable, a mooring dolphin or a rig, and the hull policy's collision clause does not respond at all - not three quarters of it, none of it. The entire liability is the club's, under fixed and floating object cover.

Learn the exclusions as a list, because between them they are almost the whole P&I book: wreck removal · fixed and floating objects and any property other than the other vessel · cargo and property on your own ship · loss of life, injury and illness · pollution. Then state the club's two roles: it insures the missing quarter, and it insures the excluded heads in full.

Why three quarters?

Because underwriters deliberately left the shipowner uninsured for a quarter so that he retained a genuine financial stake in defending the claim and in careful navigation. It is co-insurance in its purest form, and giving the reason rather than the fraction alone is what turns a remembered number into an understood one. Add that some modern forms provide four fourths, so the fraction is a market convention and not a rule of law.

The one distinction limb b) is really testing

SUSTAINED versus DISCOVERED. A marine policy answers for a fortuity occurring during its currency. Damage caused by an insured peril in year one and found in dry dock in year two is a claim on year one's policy, not year two's. Late discovery does not defeat the claim - it makes it harder to prove and it exposes the assured to the deduction for want of notice.

That last point is where the real prejudice lies, and examiners like it: the problem with a late claim is usually not admissibility but that underwriters were denied the chance to survey before repairs, and the clauses provide for a reduction from the ascertained claim in consequence.

The unrepaired damage rule, in one line

Reasonable depreciation arising from the unrepaired damage, not exceeding the reasonable cost of repairing it. That is section 69(3) of the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, and the hull clause restates it as depreciation in market value at the time the insurance terminates, capped at repair cost and at the insured value.

Then the two conditions that make it a commercial decision rather than a formality: no recovery for unrepaired damage if the vessel becomes a total loss within the same policy period - whether or not that loss is covered - and no cover for subsequent deterioration, because underwriters answer for the damage as it stood at the casualty. Deferring the repair moves real risk onto the owner.

Why 'Class unaffected' is in the question

It is not decoration. Depreciation is measured by what a willing buyer would deduct knowing the damage exists. If Class is unaffected and earning capacity is unimpaired, a buyer may deduct very little - so the depreciation, and therefore the claim, may be small or even nil, even though the repair would cost a great deal. Saying that explicitly shows you understand that the measure is a market measure and not an engineering one, and it is the most discriminating single sentence available on this limb.

Common mistakes

  • Not recognising that the Running Down Clause and the 3/4ths Collision Liability clause are the same provision.
  • Saying the collision clause covers striking a jetty, buoy or cable. It responds only to collision with another vessel.
  • Saying P&I covers only the missing quarter. It also covers the excluded heads in full.
  • Treating general average as a policy clause. It is a rule of maritime law given effect by a contractual code.
  • Asserting that a particular York-Antwerp edition applies. 1994, 2004 and 2016 are all current and the contract selects.
  • Confusing general average expenditure with sacrifice, or general with particular average.
  • Saying the hull underwriter pays general average in full regardless of value. If the vessel is under-insured he pays only the proportion the insured value bears to the contributory value.
  • Missing unrecoverable general average - the club's classic general average exposure, arising where the owner's own breach of the contract of carriage defeats his right to contribution.
  • In limb b), answering that the claim fails because it was discovered late. It attaches to the policy on risk when the damage was sustained.
  • Answering limb b) with the cost of repair. Unrepaired, the measure is depreciation, capped at repair cost.
  • Omitting the subsequent total loss rule, which is what makes the decision not to repair a risk.

Examiner traps

  • "Your ship damages a berth. What does the collision clause pay?" Nothing. Not a vessel.
  • "Why three quarters?" Deliberate co-insurance to keep the owner interested in the defence and in prudent navigation.
  • "Cross-liabilities or single liability?" Cross-liabilities normally; single liability where one or both owners limit.
  • "Does the collision clause come out of the sum insured for hull damage?" No - it responds in addition, subject to its own limit.
  • "Who pays cargo's share of general average if cargo successfully resists because the ship was unseaworthy?" Nobody recovers it from cargo; the owner bears it and looks to his club for unrecoverable general average.
  • "The ship is insured for less than her contributory value. What does the hull policy pay on general average?" Only the proportion the insured value bears to the contributory value.
  • "Damage found two years later - which policy?" The one on risk when the damage was sustained.
  • "You do not repair. How much do you get?" The reasonable depreciation, not exceeding the reasonable cost of repair.
  • "The ship is then lost in the same policy year. What happens to the unrepaired damage claim?" It is lost - underwriters are not liable for unrepaired damage where a subsequent total loss is sustained in the period.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What is the Running Down Clause and what is it called in the Institute clauses? The collision liability clause; 3/4ths Collision Liability.
  • Name three heads it indemnifies. Damage to the other vessel and property on her; loss of use or delay of that vessel; general average, salvage or salvage under contract of that vessel.
  • Name three heads it excludes. Wreck removal; fixed and floating objects and any property other than the other vessel; loss of life and personal injury; pollution; cargo on your own ship.
  • Define a general average act. An extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure intentionally and reasonably made or incurred for the common safety, to preserve from peril the property involved in a common maritime adventure.
  • Give three examples of general average expenditure. Salvage or towage engaged for the common safety; port of refuge expenses; crew wages and maintenance and fuel during the detention.
  • Who prepares the adjustment and on what basis? An average adjuster, on contributory values at the termination of the adventure.
  • What is unrecoverable general average? The proportion the owner cannot recover from cargo because his own breach of the contract of carriage - typically unseaworthiness - defeats the right to contribution. It falls to the club.
  • Damage discovered a year later - is it covered? Yes, against the policy on risk when it was sustained, if he can prove an insured peril in that period; subject to any deduction for late notice.
  • How is an unrepaired damage claim valued? Reasonable depreciation in market value, established by valuation of the vessel sound and damaged, capped at the reasonable cost of repair and at the insured value.
  • What defeats it entirely? A subsequent total loss during the same policy period, whether or not covered.

Memory framework

For limb a) hold two lines and two lists. Line one: RDC is a policy clause; general average is a rule of law. Line two: both are where the hull policy stops and the club begins.

For the collision clause, remember what it pays as DAMAGE, DELAY, SALVAGE - of the OTHER SHIP, and what it does not as WRECK, OBJECTS, OWN CARGO, PEOPLE, POLLUTION. For general average, SHIP'S SHARE to the hull; SHORTFALL and UNRECOVERABLE to the club.

For limb b), two questions in order: WHEN WAS IT SUSTAINED, then WHAT IS IT WORTH UNREPAIRED. And for the measure, one sentence: depreciation, capped at repair cost - with the killer condition, no total loss in the period.

Regulation and source map

  • Institute Time Clauses - Hulls, 3/4ths Collision Liability clause - the Running Down Clause. Numbering varies between clause sets
  • Institute Time Clauses - Hulls, General Average and Salvage clause - the ship's proportion of general average and salvage
  • Institute Time Clauses - Hulls, Notice of Claim and Tenders - prompt notice so underwriters may survey; reduction from the ascertained claim if not given
  • Institute Time Clauses - Hulls, Unrepaired Damage clause - depreciation in market value at termination, capped at repair cost and at insured value; no liability where a subsequent total loss occurs in the period
  • York-Antwerp Rules, Rule A - the definition of a general average act; Rule D - fault does not defeat contribution but leaves remedies intact
  • York-Antwerp Rules editions 1994, 2004 and 2016 - all current at this sitting; the contract of affreightment selects
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 (India), section 66 general average loss; section 69 measure of indemnity for partial loss of ship, and section 69(3) unrepaired damage
  • Marine Insurance Act 1906 (United Kingdom) - the corresponding provisions from which the Indian Act is drawn
  • P&I club rules - collision liability not recoverable under the hull policy; fixed and floating objects; wreck removal; cargo; personal injury; pollution; unrecoverable general average

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

This question carries the weakest evidential position on the paper and the answer says so rather than disguising it. The True Source corpus consumed for this paper holds no marine insurance material whatever - no Institute Time Clauses or any other hull clause set, no York-Antwerp Rules text, no Marine Insurance Act 1963 and no adjusting practice material. That is the same position recorded for the April 2023 sitting of the general average question.

Three consequences were applied deliberately. No clause wording is quoted anywhere - every provision is described by effect. Clause numbering is given as the standard Institute Time Clauses - Hulls numbering and is flagged as varying between forms, so that no candidate memorises a number his own policy does not use. And no figure is asserted - no deductible, no contributory-value formula, no percentage for the notice-of-claim reduction, and no limitation period in years - with the single exception of the three-quarters and one-quarter proportions, which are the defining characteristic of the very clause the question names, and which are themselves qualified by the note that some modern forms give four fourths.

Two positions are stated as contract-selected rather than date-selected, and that is a finding rather than an evasion: which York-Antwerp edition applies, and which hull clause set applies. Both depend on the contract in the particular case, and an answer asserting one universally would be wrong at this sitting as at any other.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a Where these two clauses live, and why the question pairs them
  2. __________a The Running Down Clause - what the hull policy covers
  3. __________a What the Running Down Clause excludes, and where the rest goes
  4. __________a General Average Expenses - what they are
  5. __________a How H&M and P&I each treat a general average claim
  6. __________b Damage found in dry dock a year later - is it admissible?
  7. __________b The unrepaired damage claim, and how its value is ascertained
Flashcards 12

1 Where the two sit - policy clause versus rule of law, both at the hull/club boundary. 2 What the collision clause pays. 3 What it excludes, and the club's two roles. 4 General average expenditure, compactly. 5 How H&M and P&I each treat a GA claim. 6 Is late-discovered damage admissible? 7 The unrepaired damage measure and how it is ascertained.

The paper prints 8 and 8, so the budget is fixed; steps 3 and 5 are where limb a)'s marks actually are.

The collision liability clause of a hull and machinery policy - the one place a hull policy takes on liability to a third party. In the Institute Time Clauses - Hulls it appears as the 3/4ths Collision Liability clause. Running Down Clause is the older market name for the same provision.

Candidates often know one name and not the other, and stating the identity immediately shows you know what you are describing.

Three fourths of sums paid for loss of or damage to the other vessel or property on board her; delay to or loss of use of that other vessel or her property; and general average, salvage or salvage under contract of that other vessel. It responds in addition to any claim for damage to the insured vessel, and is limited by reference to the insured value.

Delay and the other ship's general average are the two heads most often omitted.

Nothing. The clause responds only to collision with another vessel. Damage to fixed and floating objects - piers, jetties, buoys, cables, moorings - falls entirely to the P&I club under fixed and floating object cover.

This is the single most common misunderstanding of the clause, and it is the fastest way to show the exclusions are understood.

Excluded: wreck and obstruction removal; fixed and floating objects and any property other than the other vessel; cargo or property on the insured vessel; loss of life, personal injury and illness; and pollution or contamination. The club therefore does two things - it insures the remaining one fourth of the covered heads, and it insures the excluded heads in full.

Saying only 'the club covers the quarter' answers half the printed question.

Under Rule A of the York-Antwerp Rules there is a general average act when, and only when, any extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure is intentionally and reasonably made or incurred for the common safety, for the purpose of preserving from peril the property involved in a common maritime adventure. Sacrifice is property given up - jettison, machinery damaged refloating, fire-fighting damage. Expenditure is money spent - salvage or towage for the common safety, and port of refuge expenses.

The stem names 'General Average Expenses', so the expenditure half must be the one worked out.

It pays a general average sacrifice of the ship in full as damage to the insured property, and it pays the ship's proportion of general average contribution and of salvage charges. If the vessel is under-insured - the insured value being less than the contributory value - underwriters pay only the proportion the insured value bears to the contributory value, and the balance falls on the owner.

The under-insurance proportion is the detail that separates a real answer from a general one.

The proportion of general average that the shipowner cannot recover from cargo or other contributing interests because his own breach of the contract of carriage - typically unseaworthiness - gives them a defence to contribution. Rule D preserves the right to contribution notwithstanding fault but expressly leaves intact remedies against the party at fault, and that gap is the club's classic general average exposure.

It is the clearest illustration of the answer's organising idea - the club pays where the general average machinery fails the owner.

The policy on risk when the damage was sustained, not the one in force when it was discovered. Late discovery does not defeat the claim, but the assured bears the burden of proving that an insured peril caused it within that policy period, and failure to give prompt notice so underwriters could survey attracts a reduction from the ascertained claim.

Sustained versus discovered is the distinction the scenario is built to test.

Yes. Under section 69(3) of the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, where the ship has not been repaired and has not been sold in her damaged state during the risk, the assured is indemnified for the reasonable depreciation arising from the unrepaired damage, but not exceeding the reasonable cost of repairing such damage. The hull clause restates this as depreciation in market value at the time the insurance terminates, capped at repair cost and at the insured value.

Answering with the repair cost is the standard error; repair cost is only the ceiling.

Obtain repair specifications and yard quotations, which fix the ceiling. Then value the vessel in sound condition and with the damage disclosed, at the same date, by an independent valuer or broker against comparable sales for that type, age and trade; the difference is the depreciation. Test it by what a willing buyer would deduct knowing the damage exists. Apply the caps, then settle by agreement - or repair at the next docking and claim the cost instead.

It is a valuation exercise, not an engineering one, and the question asks specifically how the value is ascertained.

Because depreciation is measured by what a buyer would deduct. If Class is unaffected and earning capacity unimpaired, the deduction may be small or nil even though the repair would be expensive. The claim is destroyed altogether if the vessel becomes a total loss during the same policy period, whether or not that loss is covered; and subsequent deterioration is not an insured peril.

The 'Class unaffected' clue is in the stem for a reason, and the total-loss rule is what makes deferring the repair a real risk.

Quick revision
15-second recall
LIMB A. RDC = the collision liability clause in a HULL policy; the Institute clauses head it '3/4ths Collision Liability' - SAME PROVISION. On collision with another VESSEL, underwriters pay 3/4 of: damage to the other vessel and property on her; delay/loss of use of her; GA, salvage or salvage under contract of her. Responds in addition to hull damage; limited by insured value; covers legal costs with consent; adjusted on cross-liabilities (single liability where limitation applies). The 1/4 is left uninsured deliberately - co-insurance. EXCLUDED (and therefore P&I in full): wreck removal · FIXED AND FLOATING OBJECTS and any property other than the other vessel · cargo on your OWN ship · loss of life, injury, illness · pollution. Strike a jetty and the clause pays NOTHING - not a vessel. Club's two roles: the missing quarter, and the excluded heads in full. Some forms give 4/4. GENERAL AVERAGE - not a policy clause but a rule of law via the York-Antwerp Rules, a contractual code (1994/2004/2016 - the CONTRACT selects, not the date). Rule A: extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure, intentionally and reasonably made or incurred for the common safety. Expenditure = salvage/towage for common safety, and above all port of refuge expenses (in, discharge, store, reload, crew wages and maintenance, fuel). Shared on contributory values by an average adjuster. TREATMENT: H&M pays GA sacrifice of the ship in full, and the ship's proportion of GA contribution and salvage - but if under-insured, only the proportion insured value bears to contributory value. P&I pays the 1/4 collision, the excluded heads, and UNRECOVERABLE GENERAL AVERAGE - the share the owner cannot get from cargo because his own breach of the contract of carriage (unseaworthiness) defeated it. Rule D keeps the right to contribution but leaves remedies against the party at fault intact. LIMB B. Admissible? YES - the claim attaches to the policy on risk when the damage was SUSTAINED, not DISCOVERED. Burden on the assured: insured peril, within that period. Evidence: dock survey, photos, logs, incident report, previous docking, class records. Notice must be prompt so underwriters can survey - failure attracts a reduction from the ascertained claim. Deductible per occurrence. Unrepaired? He may still claim. MIA 1963 s.69(3): reasonable DEPRECIATION arising from the unrepaired damage, NOT EXCEEDING the reasonable cost of repairing it. Hull clause: depreciation in market value at termination of the insurance, capped at repair cost and insured value. NO liability if a SUBSEQUENT TOTAL LOSS occurs in the period, covered or not. Ascertained by: repair quotations (the ceiling) → independent valuation sound vs damaged at the same date → what a willing buyer would deduct - Class unaffected means the deduction may be small or nil → apply caps → agree, or repair at the next docking and claim the cost.
Keywords
RDC and 3/4ths Collision Liability are the same clauseresponds only to collision with another VESSELstrike a jetty and it pays nothingexclusions are the P&I bookclub has two roles - the quarter and the excluded headsGA is a rule of law, not a policy clausethe contract selects the York-Antwerp editionunder-insurance reduces the hull share of GA proportionatelyunrecoverable general average is the club's classic GA exposuresustained, not discovereddepreciation capped at reasonable cost of repairsubsequent total loss in the period destroys the claim
Critical numbers
  • Three fourths - the proportion of collision liability indemnified by the hull policy
  • One fourth - the balance, insured by the P&I club
  • Four fourths - available under some modern hull forms; the policy wording governs
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 section 69(3) - the unrepaired damage measure
  • York-Antwerp Rules 1994, 2004 and 2016 - all current at this sitting; contract-selected
  • NO deductible, contributory-value formula, notice-of-claim percentage or limitation period is asserted
Critical regulation
Marine Insurance Act, 1963 section 69(3), reproducing section 69(3) of the Marine Insurance Act 1906: where the ship has not been repaired and has not been sold in her damaged state during the risk, the assured is entitled to be indemnified for the reasonable depreciation arising from the unrepaired damage, but not exceeding the reasonable cost of repairing such damage. The Unrepaired Damage clause of the hull policy restates this as the reasonable depreciation in market value at the time the insurance terminates, capped at the reasonable cost of repairs and at the insured value, and excludes liability altogether where a subsequent total loss is sustained during the period of the insurance.
Major trap
In limb a), treating the Running Down Clause as covering collision with anything. It responds only to collision with another VESSEL - a ship that strikes a jetty, buoy, cable or rig recovers nothing under it and the whole liability is the club's. The related trap is answering "how are claims treated by H&M as well as P&I" with a single sentence after two long definitions, when the allocation IS the question. In limb b), two traps: concluding that a claim discovered a year later fails, when it attaches to the policy on risk at the date the damage was sustained; and answering the valuation with the cost of repair, when the measure for unrepaired damage is the reasonable depreciation, merely capped at repair cost - and is lost entirely if the ship becomes a total loss within the same policy period.

Also on the platform: QP2306 Q6 - ship repair contracts, where the repair the owner declines to carry out here would be specified and priced · QP2306 Q8 - carrier liability and defences, which decide whether general average is recoverable from cargo at all · QP2306 Q1 - compulsory insurance and direct action under the pollution conventions, a third place where liability and insurance meet

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

The examiner has taken his opening sentence straight out of the safety management code, and the sentence is doing more work than it looks. It does not say the ship must be able to cope. It says the shore organisation must be able to respond at any time - meaning that at three in the morning on a public holiday, somebody with authority must pick up the telephone. That is the whole idea, and once you see it the four items the question lists stop being a random collection. Two of them are about getting help to the ship: a reliable way for the Master to reach a decision-maker, and equipment that still works when the power has gone and the fire is in the space where the radio used to be. The other two are about people who are nowhere near the ship. The press will publish something within the hour whether or not the company says anything, and if several people give slightly different accounts the company loses control of the story permanently. And the families will find out somehow - and if they find it out from a news bulletin, or from another seafarer's wife, no amount of competent fire-fighting will repair that. So the thread is this: an emergency is not only a technical event to be managed but a set of relationships to be held together, and every one of those relationships runs down a communication channel that either was set up in advance or was not. Nothing here can be improvised, which is why the code also insists the whole thing be rehearsed.

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.
