Memory cue. The Code first: IDENTIFY, DRILL, RESPOND ANY TIME (1), propped up by DP, MASTER, LANGUAGE, REVIEW (3). Then note the four items are two pairs - INWARD (4, 5) is how the response is delivered, OUTWARD (6, 7) is how the company is judged. Office is WHO, HOW, WHAT, HOW OFTEN (4). Equipment is WORKS, POWERED, PORTABLE, TESTED, MANNED, INTERNAL (5). Press is ONE VOICE, FAST, FACTS ONLY (6). Families are FROM US, FROM A PERSON, FIRST, AND TRUE (7). The hinge between the last two: no names before the families know (6).

Remember7 route headings
Cover64 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 64 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Where the printed words come from, and what is actually being asked
  • THE OPENING SENTENCE OF THE QUESTION IS ISM CODE PARAGRAPH 8.3, ALMOST WORD FOR WORD - say so
  • 8.1 - identify potential emergency shipboard situations, and establish procedures to respond to them
  • 8.2 - establish PROGRAMMES FOR DRILLS AND EXERCISES to prepare for emergency actions
  • 8.3 - the SMS provides measures ensuring THE COMPANY'S ORGANIZATION can respond AT ANY TIME
  • 'the Company's ORGANIZATION' - not the ship. 'AT ANY TIME' - not office hours. The paragraph is about the SHORE side round the clock
  • TWO printed demands: UNDERLINE THE IMPORTANCE, and STATE THE MEASURES for four named items
2. Why communication is the load-bearing element of emergency preparedness
  • communication is not one item among several - IT IS THE MEDIUM THROUGH WHICH EVERY OTHER ELEMENT OPERATES
  • an emergency plan that cannot be ACTIVATED is not a plan; the first act of any response is a communication
  • the ship cannot resolve a serious casualty alone - salvage, class, flag, coastal State, P&I, charterers and families are all reached through the office
  • DELAY IS THE COST - almost every escalation from incident to disaster contains an interval in which the right information had not reached the person who could act
  • information DEGRADES as it is relayed, under stress and often in a second language - hence ISM 6 on effective communication and working language
  • BAD EXTERNAL COMMUNICATION CREATES A SECOND EMERGENCY, and unlike the fire that damage is self-inflicted
  • communication failure is a recurring CAUSAL FACTOR in casualties, which is why it is a safety system requirement and not a courtesy
  • hence the ISM formula - AVAILABILITY, NOT INTENTION, is what is required
3. The ISM framework that supports emergency communication
  • SECTION 3 - responsibility, authority and interrelation of all personnel DEFINED AND DOCUMENTED; adequate resources and shore-based support
  • SECTION 4 - the DESIGNATED PERSON, the link between Company and ship, with DIRECT ACCESS TO THE HIGHEST LEVEL OF MANAGEMENT
  • that is why a Master can reach a decision-maker at 0300 without going through a commercial chain
  • SECTION 5 - the Master's OVERRIDING AUTHORITY, and the express right to REQUEST THE COMPANY'S ASSISTANCE AS MAY BE NECESSARY
  • SECTION 6 - personnel able to COMMUNICATE EFFECTIVELY, and SMS information in a WORKING LANGUAGE THEY UNDERSTAND
  • 8.2 DRILLS AND EXERCISES - the only mechanism that tests whether any of it works before it is needed
  • SECTIONS 9 AND 12 - a communication failure is a NON-CONFORMITY, reported, analysed and corrected
4. Item (i) - contact between ship and the office
  • a WRITTEN emergency contact procedure in the SMS, carried on board and ashore
  • A 24-HOUR NUMBER ANSWERED BY A PERSON, NOT A MACHINE - the direct expression of 'at any time'
  • duty roster and CALL-OUT LIST with a first contact and an alternate for every role
  • the DESIGNATED PERSON as primary route, with authority to escalate; emergency response team convened on a defined trigger
  • a STANDARD INITIAL REPORT FORMAT - ship, position, time, nature, casualties, pollution, damage and stability, weather, action taken, assistance required, caller
  • an AGREED REPORTING INTERVAL thereafter, so silence is recognised as a signal
  • MULTIPLE INDEPENDENT MEANS with a stated order of preference
  • ONE POINT OF CONTACT EACH WAY - multiple simultaneous conversations produce contradictory instructions
  • a TIMED WRITTEN LOG of every communication, kept both ends
  • THE MASTER'S OVERRIDING AUTHORITY IS RESPECTED - the office supports; it does not fight the fire from ashore
  • a maintained shore contact list - flag, coastal State, port, class, salvors, P&I correspondents, charterers, technical support
5. Item (ii) - communication equipment
  • equipment appropriate to the trading area, MAINTAINED IN WORKING ORDER AND AVAILABLE AT ALL TIMES
  • GMDSS functions - distress alerting, SAR coordinating communications, on-scene communications, locating signals, maritime safety information, general communications
  • EMERGENCY POWER AND INDEPENDENCE - reserve source of energy, charged batteries, siting so one fire or flood does not remove every means
  • PORTABLE EQUIPMENT for on-scene use - intrinsically safe portables for fire and enclosed-space teams, survival craft VHF, EPIRB, SART - with a defined allocation to teams
  • SCHEDULED TESTING with results recorded; batteries tested and dated; covered by the planned maintenance system
  • DEFECT REPORTING AND REDUNDANCY - a defective set is a reportable defect, with stated alternative arrangements
  • QUALIFIED OPERATORS, AND ENOUGH OF THEM, so the response does not depend on one person who may be the casualty
  • clear operating instructions POSTED AT THE STATION - under stress people follow what is in front of them
  • INTERNAL COMMUNICATIONS MATTER AS MUCH AS EXTERNAL - general alarm, public address, bridge/engine room/steering gear telephones independent of main power. MOST EMERGENCIES ARE LOST INTERNALLY
  • COMPATIBILITY with rescue coordination centres, assisting ships and helicopters, including on-scene frequencies and language
6. Item (iii) - dealing with the media
  • THIS IS A COMPANY FUNCTION, NOT A SHIPBOARD ONE - say so first
  • A SINGLE AUTHORISED SPOKESPERSON named in the plan; ALL other personnel refer every enquiry to that person
  • THE MASTER AND CREW DO NOT SPEAK TO THE PRESS - partial information, fully occupied, and anything said becomes the company's position
  • A PREPARED HOLDING STATEMENT issued quickly - incident occurred, company responding, safety of those on board is the priority, more to follow. SPEED MATTERS MORE THAN COMPLETENESS
  • FACTS ONLY - no speculation as to cause, no damage or spill estimates before established, NO ADMISSION OF LIABILITY; legal and P&I advice first
  • NAMES ARE NEVER RELEASED BEFORE THE NEXT OF KIN HAVE BEEN INFORMED - the rule that links (iii) and (iv)
  • MEDIA TRAINING for the spokesperson and a single controlled channel of release
  • MONITOR what is published, including social media; correct significant factual errors; assume crew posts will appear and cover it in crew briefing
  • COORDINATE with flag, coastal State, salvors, charterers, class and P&I so statements do not conflict; do not prejudice the safety investigation
  • KEEP THE CREW AND THE OFFICE INFORMED - an organisation that learns of its own casualty from a news bulletin loses confidence in its management
7. Item (iv) - dealing with the next of kin
  • ONE PRINCIPLE GOVERNS: THE FAMILY MUST HEAR IT FROM THE COMPANY FIRST, FROM A PERSON, AND ACCURATELY
  • ACCURATE CURRENT NEXT-OF-KIN RECORDS - name, relationship, address, telephone, language - reviewed at every crew change and IMMEDIATELY RETRIEVABLE OUT OF HOURS
  • NAMED, TRAINED PERSONNEL make contact - never the switchboard - and where possible in the family's own language
  • CONTACT AS SOON AS THE FACTS ARE VERIFIED, and before the names reach the press; verify who you are speaking to before disclosing
  • SAY ONLY WHAT IS KNOWN - no speculation, no minimising, no assurances that may prove false; say when you will call again AND THEN CALL
  • A SINGLE NAMED POINT OF CONTACT for each family, with a direct number
  • PRACTICAL AND WELFARE SUPPORT - travel, accommodation, documents and visas, repatriation, interpreters, counselling - offered rather than waited for
  • CLEAR PROMPT INFORMATION ON ENTITLEMENTS handled with the P&I club, without pressing anyone to sign while in shock
  • PROTECT THE FAMILY FROM THE PRESS - no names before they are informed; warn them they may be approached and offer help
  • KEEP CONTACT UP through the response, the investigation and afterwards; name who is responsible
  • DO NOT MAKE ADMISSIONS - compassion and liability are separate
  • CLOSE: (i) and (ii) are how the response is DELIVERED; (iii) and (iv) are how the company is JUDGED; and only 8.2 drills test whether any of it works
Model written answer

1. Where the printed words come from, and what is actually being asked

The opening sentence of the question is ISM Code paragraph 8.3, almost word for word. Say so - it costs one line and it frames everything that follows. Section 8, Emergency Preparedness, has three paragraphs and they are short enough to carry:

  1. 8.1 - the Company should identify potential emergency shipboard situations, and establish procedures to respond to them;
  2. 8.2 - the Company should establish programmes for drills and exercises to prepare for emergency actions;
  3. 8.3 - the SMS should provide for measures ensuring that the Company's organization can respond AT ANY TIME to hazards, accidents and emergency situations involving its ships.

Two words in 8.3 do the work. "The Company's organization" - not the ship. And "at any time" - not in office hours. The paragraph is about the shore organisation being reachable and capable round the clock, which is precisely why the question then asks about the office, the equipment, the press and the families.

The stem prints two demands and both must be answered: underline the importance of communication in emergency preparedness, and state what measures are required for four named items. The first is an argument; the second is four lists.

2. Why communication is the load-bearing element of emergency preparedness

Communication is not one item in the emergency plan alongside fire-fighting and damage control. It is the medium through which every other element operates. Set out the argument in steps:

  • An emergency plan that cannot be activated is not a plan. Procedures under 8.1 exist on paper until somebody transmits the alarm, so the first act of any response is a communication.
  • The ship cannot resolve a serious casualty alone. Salvage, technical support, class, charterers, cargo interests, flag State, coastal State, P&I and the family of an injured man are all reached through the office. The ship's only route to all of them is the link to shore.
  • Delay is the cost. Almost every escalation from incident to disaster contains an interval in which the right information had not reached the person who could act.
  • Information degrades as it is relayed. Under stress, with an unfamiliar vocabulary and often a second language, detail is lost and inference is added. This is why the ISM Code requires under section 6 that personnel be able to communicate effectively in the execution of their duties and receive SMS information in a working language they understand.
  • Bad external communication creates a second emergency. A well-handled casualty can still destroy a company commercially if the press and the families are handled badly - and unlike the fire, that damage is entirely self-inflicted.
  • Communication failure is a recurring causal factor in casualties and near misses, which is why it is treated as a safety system requirement and not as a courtesy.

Hence the ISM formula: the SMS must ensure the organisation can respond at any time. Availability, not intention, is what is required.

3. The ISM framework that supports emergency communication

Element 8 does not stand alone, and citing the supporting sections shows the Code is understood as a system:

ISM sectionWhat it contributes to emergency communication
3 - Company responsibilities and authorityResponsibility, authority and interrelation of all personnel defined and documented, with adequate resources and shore-based support. In an emergency this is what tells everyone who decides and who reports to whom
4 - Designated Person(s)The link between the Company and those on board, with direct access to the highest level of management. The DP is the reason a Master can reach a decision-maker at 0300 without going through a commercial chain of command
5 - Master's responsibility and authorityThe SMS must contain a clear statement of the Master's overriding authority to make decisions for safety and pollution prevention and to request the Company's assistance as may be necessary. The right to ask for help is written into the Code
6 - Resources and personnelPersonnel able to communicate effectively in their SMS duties, and SMS information supplied in a working language they understand
8.2 - Drills and exercisesThe only mechanism that tests whether any of the above actually works before it is needed
9 and 12 - Reporting, and Company reviewA communication failure in a drill or an incident is a non-conformity, reported, analysed and corrected - so the system improves rather than merely being exercised

4. Item (i) - contact between ship and the office

  • A written emergency contact procedure carried on board and ashore, forming part of the SMS, stating who is called, in what order, by what means and with what information.
  • A 24-hour emergency contact number that is answered by a person, not by a machine - this is the direct expression of "at any time" in 8.3 - backed by a duty roster and a call-out list naming a first contact and at least one alternate for every role, with all numbers.
  • The Designated Person as the primary route, with authority to escalate immediately to senior management, and with the emergency response team convened on a defined trigger.
  • A standard initial report format so that nothing essential is forgotten under stress: ship, position, time, nature of the incident, casualties, pollution, damage and stability, weather, immediate action taken, assistance required, and who is calling.
  • An agreed reporting interval - regular situation reports thereafter, so the office is not obliged to keep interrupting the Master, and so silence is recognised as a signal rather than assumed to be good news.
  • Multiple independent means - satellite voice, e-mail, mobile and the radio installation - with the order of preference stated, so that a single failure does not sever the link.
  • One point of contact each way. The Master, or an officer he nominates, speaks for the ship; a single duty person speaks for the office. Multiple simultaneous conversations produce contradictory instructions and consume the Master's time when he has least of it.
  • A written log of every communication, timed, kept both on board and ashore. It supports the later safety investigation and is the only reliable record of what was known when.
  • The Master's overriding authority is respected. The office supports and advises; it does not navigate the ship or fight the fire from the shore.
  • A shore contact list maintained current for flag State, coastal State and port authorities, class, salvors, P&I correspondents, charterers and technical support - because in an emergency there is no time to look for a number.

5. Item (ii) - communication equipment

  • Equipment appropriate to the ship's trading area, maintained in working order and available at all times, covering the GMDSS functions - distress alerting, search and rescue coordinating communications, on-scene communications, locating signals, maritime safety information and general communications.
  • Emergency power and independence. The installation must work when main power is lost - a dedicated reserve source of energy, charged batteries, and equipment sited so that a fire or flood in one space does not remove every means at once.
  • Portable equipment for on-scene use - intrinsically safe portable radios for fire and enclosed-space teams, portable VHF for survival craft, EPIRB and SART - with a defined allocation of sets to teams so that nobody is searching for a radio while mustering.
  • Routine testing and record. Scheduled testing of all distress and safety equipment with results recorded, batteries tested and dated, and the whole installation covered by the ship's planned maintenance system.
  • Defect reporting and redundancy. A defective set is a reportable defect, not something to be fixed at the next port. The procedure must state what alternative arrangements apply while equipment is out of service and whom to inform.
  • Qualified operators, and enough of them. Certificated radio operators, and enough people who can actually use the equipment that the response does not depend on one individual who may be the casualty.
  • Clear, unambiguous operating instructions posted at the station, with distress procedures displayed at the position from which they would be transmitted - because under stress people follow what is in front of them.
  • Internal communications matter as much as external. Public address and general alarm, telephones between bridge, engine room and steering gear, sound-powered or otherwise independent of main power, and portable sets for teams. Most emergencies are lost internally, not externally.
  • Compatibility with rescue services. Equipment and procedures must allow direct working with rescue coordination centres, assisting ships and helicopters, including the on-scene frequencies and the language to be used.

6. Item (iii) - dealing with the media

This is a company function, not a shipboard one, and the first measure is to say so.

  • A single authorised spokesperson, named in the plan, is the only person who speaks publicly. All other personnel, ashore and afloat, are instructed to make no statement and to refer every enquiry to that person. This is not secrecy; it is the only way to prevent contradictory accounts.
  • The Master and crew do not speak to the press. They are fully occupied, they have partial information, and anything said will be treated as the company's position. The plan must say this explicitly, because a reporter will reach the ship.
  • A prepared holding statement, drafted in advance and issued quickly, confirming only that an incident has occurred, that the company is responding, that the safety of those on board is the priority and that further information will follow. Speed matters more than completeness: a vacuum is filled by speculation and by others' accounts.
  • Facts only, and only confirmed facts. No speculation as to cause, no estimate of damage or quantity spilled before it is established, and no admission or attribution of liability. Legal and P&I advice is taken before anything of substance is released.
  • Names are never released before the next of kin have been informed. This is an absolute rule and it links items (iii) and (iv).
  • Media training for the spokesperson, and a single controlled channel of release - a company statement and a monitored press contact - rather than answering individual approaches ad hoc.
  • Monitor what is being published, including social media, and correct significant factual errors promptly through the same channel. Assume crew members' own posts and photographs will appear, and cover that in the plan and in crew briefing.
  • Coordinate with the other parties - flag State, coastal State, salvors, charterers, class and P&I - so that statements do not conflict, and remember that a marine safety investigation is being conducted separately and must not be prejudiced.
  • Keep the crew and the office informed too. An organisation that learns about its own casualty from a news bulletin loses confidence in its management at exactly the moment it needs it.

7. Item (iv) - dealing with the next of kin

One principle governs everything here: the family must hear it from the company first, from a person, and accurately.

  • Accurate, current next-of-kin records for every person on board - name, relationship, address, telephone, language - maintained by the crewing department, reviewed at every crew change, and immediately retrievable out of hours. A plan that depends on files nobody can open at 0300 has failed.
  • Named, trained personnel to make contact. This is done by designated and briefed people - never by the switchboard, never by the person who happens to answer - and where possible by someone who can speak the family's own language.
  • Contact is made as soon as the facts are verified. Verified, but fast: the family must not learn of it from the news or from another seafarer's family. Verify the identity of the person you are speaking to before disclosing anything.
  • Say only what is known. Do not speculate, do not minimise, and do not give assurances that may prove false. Where information is not yet available, say so, and say when you will call again - and then call.
  • A single named point of contact for each family, with a direct number, so that they are not passed between departments and so that the company hears their questions consistently.
  • Practical and welfare support: travel and accommodation, assistance with documents and visas, help with the repatriation of an injured or deceased person, interpreters, and access to counselling. Say clearly what the company will do rather than leaving it to be asked for.
  • Clear, prompt information on entitlements - wages, contractual compensation, insurance and any statutory entitlement - handled with the P&I club, without pressing anyone to sign anything while in shock.
  • Protect the family from the press. Do not release names before they are informed, advise them that they may be approached, and offer the company's assistance in handling it.
  • Keep contact up. Communication does not end with the first call - the family needs updates during the response, during any investigation and afterwards, and the plan should say who is responsible for maintaining that contact.
  • Do not make admissions. Compassion and liability are separate; the plan should make clear that being humane costs nothing legally, but that statements about cause and fault are not for this conversation.

Close by tying it back. The ISM Code requires that the Company's organisation can respond at any time. Items (i) and (ii) are how the response is delivered; items (iii) and (iv) are how it is judged. A company that fights the fire well and handles the families badly will still be remembered for the second - and only element 8.2, the programme of drills and exercises, tests whether any of it works before the day it is needed.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks are printed on the question and the four items carry no split, so the paper is silent on weighting and the candidate must decide it. The stem prints two demands: "underline the Importance of communication in emergency preparedness" and "what measures are required for the following". A candidate who answers only the second has answered perhaps three quarters of the question; one who writes a general essay on the importance of communication and gives four thin lists has answered less.

The route therefore spends one step making the argument properly, one step showing that element 8 is supported by the rest of the Code, and then four substantial steps of measures. The four measure sets are where most of the marks are, and they are written as lists of measures because the printed word is "measures" - each bullet is a countable proposition.

The opening line that changes the answer

The first sentence of the question is ISM paragraph 8.3. Recognising and saying so does three things: it proves you know the Code rather than the topic, it tells you the examiner wants an ISM-based answer rather than a general management essay, and it gives you 8.1 and 8.2 free of charge as the surrounding structure.

Two words in 8.3 carry the whole question. "The Company's organization" - the paragraph is about the shore side. And "at any time" - which is why a 24-hour contact answered by a person is the single most important measure under item (i). If you quote nothing else, quote those.

Section 8 in full - it is three sentences

  • 8.1 - identify potential emergency shipboard situations and establish procedures to respond to them
  • 8.2 - establish programmes for drills and exercises
  • 8.3 - the SMS provides measures ensuring the Company's organization can respond at any time

Three sentences is a very small amount of Code to memorise for a sixteen-mark question, and almost nobody does it. Note also, if it comes up in an oral, that 8.1 is the amended wording - the original 1993 text read "identify, describe and respond to" and was replaced by the 2008 amendment set. The current form is shorter.

Why the supporting sections matter

The Code is a system and element 8 leans on four other sections. Section 4 gives the Designated Person direct access to the highest level of management - that is what makes a 0300 call to a decision-maker possible. Section 5 gives the Master overriding authority and the express right to request the Company's assistance as may be necessary. Section 6 requires that people can communicate effectively and get SMS information in a working language they understand. Sections 9 and 12 turn a failure in a drill into a non-conformity that must be analysed and corrected.

Citing those four alongside element 8 is the difference between an answer about communication and an answer about the ISM Code.

What the Code does NOT say - and why saying so earns credit

The ISM Code requires that procedures exist. It says nothing whatever about press statements, next-of-kin contact or radio equipment specification. Those measures are good management practice, consistent with the Code but not prescribed by it. Making that distinction explicitly - "the Code requires the Company to establish procedures; what follows is what a competent procedure contains" - shows judgement, and it protects you from asserting as regulation something the examiner knows is not in the Code.

The two rules that connect items (iii) and (iv)

First: names are never released to the media before the next of kin have been informed. Second: one authorised spokesperson, and nobody else speaks. If you carry only two propositions across the external-communication half of this question, carry those - they are the two failures that turn a well-handled casualty into a reputational disaster, and both are entirely within the company's control.

The parallel rule inside item (iv) is that the family must hear it from the company, from a person, first, and accurately. Everything else in that list is a way of making that possible.

Common mistakes

  • Not recognising that the stem is ISM 8.3, and answering with a general essay on communication.
  • Answering only the measures and never underlining the importance, when the stem prints both.
  • Giving four thin lists. The four items carry most of the marks and each needs real content.
  • Treating media handling as a job for the Master. It is a company function with a single authorised spokesperson.
  • Releasing names before the next of kin are informed - the one unforgivable error on this question.
  • Forgetting internal communications under item (ii). General alarm, public address, engine room telephones and portable sets are communication equipment, and most emergencies are lost internally.
  • Quoting GMDSS carriage requirements and sea areas instead of answering what measures are required - the question asks about management of the equipment, not the SOLAS table.
  • Presenting media and next-of-kin procedures as ISM requirements. They are not in the Code.
  • Omitting drills and exercises under 8.2, which are the only test that any of this works.
  • Forgetting the Master's overriding authority - the office supports; it does not command the response from ashore.
  • Making admissions of liability, to the press or to a family. Compassion and liability are separate.

Examiner traps

  • "Which paragraph of the ISM Code is the question quoting?" 8.3.
  • "What are the three paragraphs of element 8?" Identify situations and establish procedures; drills and exercises; the organisation can respond at any time.
  • "Whose organisation must respond, and when?" The Company's, at any time - it is about the shore side, round the clock.
  • "Who does the Master call, and why can he reach the top?" The Designated Person, who under section 4 has direct access to the highest level of management.
  • "Can the office order the Master to keep going?" No. Section 5 requires a clear statement of the Master's overriding authority for safety and pollution prevention.
  • "A reporter telephones the ship. What does the Master say?" Nothing. He refers them to the company spokesperson.
  • "When may a casualty's name be released?" Only after the next of kin have been informed.
  • "Is next-of-kin procedure required by the ISM Code?" Not expressly. The Code requires response procedures; this is what a competent procedure contains.
  • "What tests all of it?" The programme of drills and exercises under 8.2 - and a failure found in a drill is a non-conformity under section 9.

Likely oral follow-up

  • State ISM paragraph 8.3. The SMS should provide for measures ensuring that the Company's organization can respond at any time to hazards, accidents and emergency situations involving its ships.
  • What must the initial report from the ship contain? Ship, position, time, nature of incident, casualties, pollution, damage and stability, weather, action taken, assistance required, and who is calling.
  • Why an agreed reporting interval? So the office need not interrupt the Master, and so silence is recognised as a signal.
  • Name the GMDSS functions. Distress alerting; SAR coordinating communications; on-scene communications; locating signals; maritime safety information; general communications.
  • What internal communications matter in an emergency? General alarm and public address; bridge to engine room and steering gear telephones independent of main power; portable intrinsically safe radios for teams.
  • Who speaks to the press? One named authorised spokesperson, and nobody else.
  • What goes in a holding statement? That an incident has occurred, that the company is responding, that the safety of those on board is the priority, and that more will follow. No cause, no quantities, no liability.
  • Who contacts the next of kin, and how quickly? Named, trained personnel, in the family's own language where possible, as soon as the facts are verified and before the names reach the press.
  • What support should the family be offered? Travel and accommodation, documents and visas, repatriation assistance, interpreters, counselling, and clear information on entitlements.
  • Where does the Designated Person fit? Section 4 - the link between Company and ship, with direct access to the highest level of management.

Memory framework

The Code first: IDENTIFY, DRILL, RESPOND ANY TIME - that is 8.1, 8.2, 8.3. Then the four supports: DP, MASTER, LANGUAGE, REVIEW - sections 4, 5, 6 and 12.

For the four printed items, note that they fall into two natural pairs. INWARD - the office and the equipment; OUTWARD - the press and the families. Inward is how the response is delivered; outward is how the company is judged.

Inside item (i): WHO, HOW, WHAT, HOW OFTEN - call-out list, multiple means, standard report format, agreed interval. Inside item (ii): WORKS, POWERED, PORTABLE, TESTED, MANNED, INTERNAL. Inside item (iii): ONE VOICE, FAST, FACTS ONLY, NO NAMES BEFORE FAMILIES. Inside item (iv): FROM US, FROM A PERSON, FIRST, AND TRUE.

Regulation and source map

  • ISM Code section 8 - Emergency Preparedness. 8.1 identify situations and establish procedures; 8.2 programmes for drills and exercises; 8.3 the SMS provides measures ensuring the Company's organization can respond at any time
  • ISM Code section 3 - responsibility, authority and interrelation of all personnel defined and documented; adequate resources and shore-based support
  • ISM Code section 4 - Designated Person(s); the link between Company and ship; direct access to the highest level of management
  • ISM Code section 5 - the Master's responsibility and his overriding authority, and the express right to request the Company's assistance
  • ISM Code section 6 - personnel able to communicate effectively; SMS information in a working language understood by them
  • ISM Code sections 9 and 12 - reporting and analysis of non-conformities; Company internal audit, review and evaluation
  • ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92) - the operative Code at this sitting. Paragraph 8.1 is in its amended form; 8.2 and 8.3 stand as originally adopted
  • GMDSS - cited by function only: distress alerting, SAR coordinating communications, on-scene communications, locating signals, maritime safety information, general communications

Uncertainty and the boundary between Code and practice

The Code half of this answer is primary verified. Section 8 was read directly in the ISM Code itself, paragraph 8.3 was confirmed word by word against the printed stem, and the amendment chain was checked rather than assumed: the 2008 amendment set replaces paragraph 8.1 and does not touch 8.2 or 8.3, and no later amendment set alters section 8 at all. Sections 3, 4, 5, 6, 9 and 12 were read in the same session.

The practice half is deliberately labelled as such. The ISM Code requires that procedures exist; it does not prescribe their content, and it says nothing whatever about media handling, next-of-kin contact or the specification of communication equipment. The four measure sets are therefore good management practice consistent with the Code, and the answer states that distinction openly rather than presenting management practice as regulation.

Three things are deliberately not asserted. No GMDSS carriage requirement, sea area or SOLAS chapter IV regulation number, because the equipment limb is answered by function and by management measure, which is what the question asks. The SOLAS edition held post-dates this sitting and no carriage requirement is taken from it. No drill frequency or response-time target, because those come from SOLAS and company policy rather than from the ISM Code. And no legal advice on what may be said to press or family: the answer requires that legal and P&I advice be taken and that no admission be made, and goes no further.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing Where the printed words come from, and what is actually being asked
  2. __________importance Why communication is the load-bearing element of emergency preparedness
  3. __________framework The ISM framework that supports emergency communication
  4. __________(i) Item (i) - contact between ship and the office
  5. __________(ii) Item (ii) - communication equipment
  6. __________(iii) Item (iii) - dealing with the media
  7. __________(iv) Item (iv) - dealing with the next of kin
Flashcards 12

1 The stem is ISM 8.3, and section 8 in full. 2 Why communication is the load-bearing element. 3 The supporting ISM sections - 3, 4, 5, 6, 9, 12. 4 Ship to office. 5 Communication equipment. 6 Media. 7 Next of kin.

Steps 1 to 3 answer the 'underline the importance' half; steps 4 to 7 carry most of the marks.

8.1 - the Company should identify potential emergency shipboard situations, and establish procedures to respond to them. 8.2 - the Company should establish programmes for drills and exercises to prepare for emergency actions. 8.3 - the safety management system should provide for measures ensuring that the Company's organization can respond at any time to hazards, accidents and emergency situations involving its ships.

Three sentences for a sixteen-mark question, and the opening line of the stem is the third of them.

'The Company's organization' - the paragraph is about the shore side, not the ship. And 'at any time' - not office hours. Together they mean somebody with authority must be reachable round the clock, which is why the answer's first measure is a 24-hour number answered by a person.

It converts a quoted sentence into an argument and explains why the question then asks about the office and the families.

Section 3 - responsibility, authority and interrelation of all personnel defined and documented, with adequate resources and shore-based support. Section 4 - the Designated Person, the link between Company and ship, with direct access to the highest level of management. Section 5 - the Master's overriding authority and his express right to request the Company's assistance. Section 6 - effective communication and a working language understood by personnel. Sections 9 and 12 - non-conformity reporting and Company review.

Citing these is the difference between an answer about communication and an answer about the ISM Code.

A written procedure in the SMS held both ends; a 24-hour number answered by a person; a duty roster and call-out list with alternates; the Designated Person as primary route with authority to escalate; a standard initial report format; an agreed reporting interval thereafter; multiple independent means in a stated order; one point of contact each way; a timed log kept both ends; respect for the Master's overriding authority; and a maintained shore contact list.

The printed word is 'measures', so each item is a countable proposition.

Ship's name, position, time, the nature of the incident, casualties, pollution, damage and stability condition, weather, immediate action taken, assistance required, and who is calling. An agreed interval for situation reports follows, so the office need not interrupt the Master and so silence is recognised as a signal.

A standard format is what stops essential information being forgotten under stress.

Internal communications - the general alarm and public address, telephones between bridge, engine room and steering gear independent of main power, and portable intrinsically safe radios allocated to fire and enclosed-space teams. Most emergencies are lost internally rather than externally. Also emergency power and dispersed siting, so that one fire or flood does not remove every means at once.

Candidates reach for GMDSS and stop, when the question asks about measures rather than carriage requirements.

Distress alerting; search and rescue coordinating communications; on-scene communications; locating signals; maritime safety information; and general communications.

Answering by function rather than by sea area is what the question asks for and avoids quoting a carriage table.

A single named authorised spokesperson and an instruction that nobody else speaks; the Master and crew refer all enquiries to him; a prepared holding statement issued quickly, saying only that an incident has occurred and the company is responding; facts only, with no speculation as to cause and no admission of liability, on legal and P&I advice; no names released before the next of kin are informed; a single channel of release; monitoring of coverage including social media; and coordination with flag State, salvors, class and P&I.

It is a company function, not a shipboard one, and saying so is the first mark.

Accurate current records retrievable out of hours; named trained personnel to make contact, in the family's language where possible; contact as soon as facts are verified and before names reach the press; say only what is known and call back when promised; a single named point of contact per family; practical support - travel, accommodation, documents, repatriation, interpreters, counselling; clear information on entitlements handled with the P&I club without pressing anyone to sign; protection from the press; continued contact through the investigation; and no admissions of liability.

The governing principle is that the family hears it from the company, from a person, first, and accurately.

Not expressly. Section 8 requires the Company to identify potential emergency situations, establish procedures to respond to them, run drills and exercises, and ensure the organisation can respond at any time. It says nothing about media handling, next-of-kin contact or equipment specification. Those are good management practice consistent with the Code, and what a competent procedure contains.

Stating the boundary between Code and practice shows judgement and avoids asserting as regulation something that is not in the Code.

Into two pairs. Ship-to-office contact and communication equipment are inward-facing - how the response is delivered. Media and next of kin are outward-facing - how the company is judged. Only paragraph 8.2, the programme of drills and exercises, tests whether any of it works before the day it is needed, and a failure found in a drill is a non-conformity under section 9.

It gives the answer a closing argument instead of stopping after the fourth list.

Quick revision
15-second recall
THE STEM IS ISM 8.3. Section 8: 8.1 identify potential emergency shipboard situations and establish procedures to respond; 8.2 establish programmes for drills and exercises; 8.3 the SMS provides measures ensuring the Company's ORGANIZATION can respond AT ANY TIME to hazards, accidents and emergencies involving its ships. Two words carry it: the Company's organization (shore, not ship) and at any time (round the clock). SUPPORTING SECTIONS: 3 responsibility, authority and interrelation defined and documented, adequate shore-based support; 4 Designated Person - link between Company and ship, direct access to the highest level of management; 5 Master's overriding authority and right to request the Company's assistance; 6 able to communicate effectively, SMS information in a working language understood; 9 and 12 non-conformity reporting and Company review. IMPORTANCE: a plan that cannot be activated is not a plan; the ship cannot resolve a serious casualty alone; delay is the cost; information degrades under stress and in a second language; bad external communication is a second, self-inflicted emergency; communication failure is a recurring causal factor. (i) SHIP-OFFICE: written procedure in the SMS · 24-hour number answered by a PERSON · duty roster and call-out list with alternates · DP as primary route, ERT on a defined trigger · standard initial report (ship, position, time, nature, casualties, pollution, damage/stability, weather, action taken, assistance required, caller) · agreed reporting interval so silence is a signal · multiple independent means in stated order · one point of contact each way · timed log both ends · Master's overriding authority respected · maintained shore contact list. (ii) EQUIPMENT: appropriate to trade, working, available at all times · GMDSS functions - alerting, SAR coordinating, on-scene, locating, MSI, general · emergency power, charged batteries, dispersed siting · portables - intrinsically safe for teams, survival craft VHF, EPIRB, SART, allocated in advance · scheduled testing recorded, in the PMS · defect reporting with stated alternatives · enough qualified operators · instructions posted at the station · INTERNAL - general alarm, PA, bridge/ER/steering telephones independent of main power. Most emergencies are lost internally · compatibility with RCC, assisting ships, helicopters. (iii) MEDIA - a COMPANY function: ONE authorised spokesperson, everyone else refers · Master and crew say nothing · prepared holding statement, fast - incident, responding, safety is the priority, more to follow · facts only, no cause, no quantities, NO ADMISSION, legal and P&I advice first · NO NAMES BEFORE NEXT OF KIN ARE INFORMED · media training, single channel · monitor including social media, assume crew posts · coordinate with flag, coastal State, salvors, class, P&I; do not prejudice the safety investigation · keep crew and office informed. (iv) NEXT OF KIN - from us, from a person, first, and true: accurate records retrievable out of hours · named trained callers, family's language · as soon as verified, before the press · say only what is known, say when you will call again and do · one named contact per family · travel, documents, repatriation, interpreters, counselling · clear entitlements via P&I, no signing under shock · shield them from the press · keep contact up · no admissions. The Code requires procedures to EXIST; it does not prescribe their content - say so.

Critical regulation
ISM Code paragraph 8.3 - the safety management system should provide for measures ensuring that the Company's organization can respond at any time to hazards, accidents and emergency situations involving its ships. Read with paragraph 8.1, requiring the Company to identify potential emergency shipboard situations and establish procedures to respond to them, and paragraph 8.2, requiring programmes for drills and exercises. Support comes from section 4, under which the Designated Person is the link between the Company and those on board with direct access to the highest level of management, and section 5, which requires a clear statement of the Master's overriding authority and his right to request the Company's assistance as may be necessary.
Major trap
Answering only half the question. The stem prints two demands - underline the importance of communication in emergency preparedness, and state the measures required for four named items - and a candidate who gives four lists without the argument, or the argument with four thin lists, has answered part of it. The substantive traps are: not recognising that the opening sentence is ISM paragraph 8.3 and writing a general management essay instead; treating media handling as the Master's job when it is a company function with a single authorised spokesperson; releasing names before the next of kin are informed; forgetting internal communications under the equipment limb when most emergencies are lost internally; answering the equipment limb with GMDSS carriage requirements instead of the measures asked for; and presenting media and next-of-kin procedures as ISM requirements when the Code requires only that procedures exist and says nothing about their content.

Also on the platform: QP2306 Q2 - the Casualty Investigation Code, which governs what happens after the emergency this question prepares for · QP2306 Q7 - the SEEMP, another management cycle that the ISM safety management system may absorb · QP2306 Q6 - ship repair contracts, where the same discipline of defined responsibility and defined communication lines applies commercially

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

The whole subject exists because the best antifouling ever invented turned out to be poisoning everything around the ship at concentrations so small that nobody had thought to look. Once it was outlawed, the industry had to reproduce what made it so good - not the poison itself, but the trick of releasing a poison at a steady, controlled rate for years - and the four things this question names are four answers to that problem, of which only three are really the same kind of answer. Two of them work by wearing away. In the cheaper one the paint film simply dissolves in seawater, uncovering more of the poison mixed into it as it goes; because it leaves behind a spongy skeleton of what would not dissolve, the hull grows rougher as the years pass and the release tails off. In the better one the poison is chemically built into the binder itself, and a chemical reaction with seawater loosens the outermost skin so that the passing water carries it away, exposing a fresh face beneath; the release stays even and the hull actually gets smoother. The third is simply a mixture of the two recipes in whatever proportion suits the price and the docking interval an owner wants. The fourth is a complete change of thinking: instead of poisoning what lands on the hull, make the hull so slippery that nothing can hold on, and let the ship's own speed wash it off. That last idea escapes the regulatory problem entirely, which matters more each time another biocide is outlawed - but it only works if the ship actually goes fast, and it is expensive and fragile. Underneath all of it is a newer commercial fact: a rough or fouled hull now costs measurable fuel and shows up in the ship's efficiency figures, so choosing a coating is no longer just about protecting the sea from the paint.

Knowledge map
Alternatives to Tin-Based Antifouling Paints
  1. framing
    1. 1Why there are alternatives at all
      • TBT self-polishing copolymers were the most effective antifoulings ever produced - and PERSISTENT, BIOACCUMULATIVE AND TOXIC AT VERY LOW CONCENTRATION
      • IMPOSEX in marine gastropods; SHELL DEFORMATION in oysters, far below concentrations affecting the target organisms
      • AFS CONVENTION 2001, IN FORCE 17 SEPTEMBER 2008 - organotin compounds acting as biocides not applied or re-applied; otherwise a BARRIER COATING
    2. 2The second ban, and why it bites on this question now
      • MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, IN FORCE 1 JANUARY 2023 - adds CYBUTRYNE to the controlled list
      • may not be APPLIED OR RE-APPLIED from that date
      • existing systems REMOVED OR OVERCOATED WITH A SEALER COAT at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system, and within 60 MONTHS of last application
  2. (i)
    1. 3(i) Controlled Depletion Polymer (CDP)
      • PRINCIPLE - a SOLUBLE MATRIX. A rosin-based binder DISSOLVES in seawater, exposing and releasing the biocide dispersed in it
      • the modern descendant of the traditional soluble-matrix paint, with depletion controlled by the polymer chemistry
      • BEHAVIOUR - leaves a LEACHED POROUS SKELETON; release is NOT LINEAR, highest when fresh and falling as the leached layer thickens
  3. (ii)
    1. 4(ii) Self-Polishing Copolymer (SPC)
      • PRINCIPLE - CHEMICAL, NOT PHYSICAL. The biocide or pendant group is CHEMICALLY BONDED into an acrylate copolymer backbone
      • in slightly alkaline seawater the side group HYDROLYSES, the surface layer becomes soluble and is WASHED AWAY BY THE PASSING WATER, exposing fresh binder and biocide
      • the tin-free descendant of TBT-SPC using SILYL, COPPER OR ZINC ACRYLATE chemistry
  4. (Ill)
    1. 5(Ill) Hybrid TBT free paint
      • IT IS NOT A PAINT CONTAINING SOME TIN - it is a COMMERCIAL BLEND of the two chemistries above
      • binder combines ROSIN OR ROSIN DERIVATIVE with a SELF-POLISHING COPOLYMER component, in varying proportions
      • PRINCIPLE - both mechanisms operate together; adjusting the ratio moves the product along a scale between CDP and full SPC
  5. (iv)
    1. 6(iv) Fouling Release paint
      • THE ONE GENUINE DEPARTURE - IT IS NOT A BETTER BIOCIDE, IT CONTAINS NO BIOCIDE AT ALL
      • PRINCIPLE - a SILICONE ELASTOMER or fluoropolymer giving VERY LOW SURFACE ENERGY, very smooth, slightly flexible, non-stick
      • organisms that settle attach WEAKLY and are SHEARED OFF BY THE WATER FLOW once the ship is up to speed
  6. comparison
    1. 7Choosing between them
      • THE FOUR ARE NOT FOUR GRADES OF THE SAME PRODUCT - they are TWO BIOCIDAL FAMILIES, ONE BLEND OF THEM, AND ONE NON-BIOCIDAL PRINCIPLE
      • MECHANISM - dissolves / hydrolyses and polishes / both / non-stick and sheared off
      • SURFACE WITH AGE - roughens / smooths / intermediate / smoothest throughout
  7. wider field
    1. 8What else is in the field, and why this is now a fuel question
      • COPPER-BASED AND COPPER-FREE biocidal systems with organic booster biocides, and increasingly ZINC chemistry
      • HYDROGEL AND HYDROPHILIC-MODIFIED coatings combining fouling release with an enhanced water layer
      • HARD, INERT, NON-TOXIC coatings used with PLANNED IN-WATER CLEANING - no biocide and no depletion, but a scheduled cleaning regime, and a growing regulatory issue in itself

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. Why there are alternatives at all
