  1. What else is in the field, and why this is now a fuel question

Memory cue. Group first: TWO BIOCIDAL FAMILIES, ONE BLEND, ONE NON-STICK (7). Four verbs for the mechanisms - DISSOLVES (3), POLISHES (4), BOTH (5), RELEASES (6). Three words for the surface with age - ROUGHENS, SMOOTHS, SMOOTHEST. Cost and life run together: CHEAP AND SHORT, DEAR AND LONG, MIDDLE, DEAREST AND LONGEST (7). Treat every type under the same four headings, PRINCIPLE, BEHAVIOUR, MERITS, LIMITS. And carry the current fact: CYBUTRYNE, IN FORCE THIS JANUARY (2).

Remember8 route headings
Cover60 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 60 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Why there are alternatives at all
  • TBT self-polishing copolymers were the most effective antifoulings ever produced - and PERSISTENT, BIOACCUMULATIVE AND TOXIC AT VERY LOW CONCENTRATION
  • IMPOSEX in marine gastropods; SHELL DEFORMATION in oysters, far below concentrations affecting the target organisms
  • AFS CONVENTION 2001, IN FORCE 17 SEPTEMBER 2008 - organotin compounds acting as biocides not applied or re-applied; otherwise a BARRIER COATING
  • the commercial problem that generated all four alternatives: REPLACE A BIOCIDE THAT WORKED VERY WELL WITHOUT LOSING THE CONTROLLED RELEASE THAT MADE IT WORK
  • KEEP THIS SHORT - the stem asks about the alternatives, not about the Convention
2. The second ban, and why it bites on this question now
  • MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, IN FORCE 1 JANUARY 2023 - adds CYBUTRYNE to the controlled list
  • may not be APPLIED OR RE-APPLIED from that date
  • existing systems REMOVED OR OVERCOATED WITH A SEALER COAT at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system, and within 60 MONTHS of last application
  • AT THIS SITTING THE CONTROLS ARE ABOUT FIVE MONTHS OLD
  • cybutryne was a BOOSTER BIOCIDE in some SPC and HYBRID products - two of the four types named
  • THE REGULATORY PRESSURE IS NOW ON THE BIOCIDAL ALTERNATIVES, NOT ON THE TIN THEY REPLACED
  • sets up the closing argument - every biocidal system carries the risk that its biocide is next
3. (i) Controlled Depletion Polymer (CDP)
  • PRINCIPLE - a SOLUBLE MATRIX. A rosin-based binder DISSOLVES in seawater, exposing and releasing the biocide dispersed in it
  • the modern descendant of the traditional soluble-matrix paint, with depletion controlled by the polymer chemistry
  • BEHAVIOUR - leaves a LEACHED POROUS SKELETON; release is NOT LINEAR, highest when fresh and falling as the leached layer thickens
  • MERITS - CHEAPEST; tolerant of poor surface preparation; easy to apply and overcoat; COMPATIBLE WITH MOST EXISTING SCHEMES
  • LIMITS - SHORTEST LIFE; performance DEGRADES progressively; the leached layer INCREASES HULL ROUGHNESS so the fuel penalty grows
  • LIMITS - POOR AT REST; depends on water movement; rosin is brittle and water-sensitive
  • WHERE IT FITS - coastal and short-sea trades, short docking cycles, first cost dominant
4. (ii) Self-Polishing Copolymer (SPC)
  • PRINCIPLE - CHEMICAL, NOT PHYSICAL. The biocide or pendant group is CHEMICALLY BONDED into an acrylate copolymer backbone
  • in slightly alkaline seawater the side group HYDROLYSES, the surface layer becomes soluble and is WASHED AWAY BY THE PASSING WATER, exposing fresh binder and biocide
  • the tin-free descendant of TBT-SPC using SILYL, COPPER OR ZINC ACRYLATE chemistry
  • BEHAVIOUR - a CONTROLLED, NEAR-LINEAR POLISHING RATE; steady release; NO THICK LEACHED LAYER; THE HULL GETS SMOOTHER IN SERVICE
  • MERITS - LONG PREDICTABLE LIFE, supports the longest docking intervals of the biocidal three; consistent performance to the end of the cycle; smoothing gives a real fuel benefit
  • LIMITS - DEARER and demanding of surface preparation and application control
  • LIMITS - polishing depends on WATER FLOW, so poor when idle; rate varies with SPEED, TEMPERATURE AND SALINITY; STILL BIOCIDAL, with the regulatory exposure cybutryne has just demonstrated
  • THE KEY SENTENCE: CDP DISSOLVES; SPC HYDROLYSES AND IS POLISHED AWAY BY THE WATER
5. (Ill) Hybrid TBT free paint
  • IT IS NOT A PAINT CONTAINING SOME TIN - it is a COMMERCIAL BLEND of the two chemistries above
  • binder combines ROSIN OR ROSIN DERIVATIVE with a SELF-POLISHING COPOLYMER component, in varying proportions
  • PRINCIPLE - both mechanisms operate together; adjusting the ratio moves the product along a scale between CDP and full SPC
  • MERITS - INTERMEDIATE COST AND LIFE, which is the point: buy the docking interval you actually need
  • MERITS - more tolerant of application conditions than a full SPC; easier to overcoat and more compatible at a repair yard; lets a manufacturer offer a graded range on one platform
  • LIMITS - a COMPROMISE THAT BEHAVES LIKE ONE; neither polishes as cleanly nor costs as little; LESS PREDICTABLE because it depends on the blend
  • LIMITS - still leaches and can build a partial leached layer; STILL BIOCIDAL - hybrids were among the products using CYBUTRYNE as a booster
  • WHERE IT FITS - mixed trading, moderate docking intervals, where the full SPC premium is not justified
6. (iv) Fouling Release paint
  • THE ONE GENUINE DEPARTURE - IT IS NOT A BETTER BIOCIDE, IT CONTAINS NO BIOCIDE AT ALL
  • PRINCIPLE - a SILICONE ELASTOMER or fluoropolymer giving VERY LOW SURFACE ENERGY, very smooth, slightly flexible, non-stick
  • organisms that settle attach WEAKLY and are SHEARED OFF BY THE WATER FLOW once the ship is up to speed
  • MERITS - NO BIOCIDE, so outside biocidal regulatory pressure entirely - the strongest argument in a year when a second biocide has just been banned
  • MERITS - SMOOTHEST SURFACE OF THE FOUR, lowest frictional resistance, a direct fuel and carbon-intensity benefit
  • MERITS - LONG LIFE; it is not consumed and does not deplete; can last several docking cycles; easier, less damaging cleaning
  • LIMITS - HIGHEST FIRST COST; requires a FULL MULTI-COAT SCHEME with a tie coat and exacting preparation; essentially a newbuilding or major-refit decision
  • LIMITS - MECHANICALLY SOFT and easily damaged; LOCAL REPAIR IS DIFFICULT because nothing adheres well to it, including new paint
  • LIMITS - IT DEPENDS ON SPEED. Below a threshold service speed there is insufficient shear, so POOR FOR SLOW SHIPS AND SHIPS THAT LIE IDLE
  • WHERE IT FITS - fast, high-utilisation, deep-sea ships with long docking intervals
7. Choosing between them
  • THE FOUR ARE NOT FOUR GRADES OF THE SAME PRODUCT - they are TWO BIOCIDAL FAMILIES, ONE BLEND OF THEM, AND ONE NON-BIOCIDAL PRINCIPLE
  • MECHANISM - dissolves / hydrolyses and polishes / both / non-stick and sheared off
  • SURFACE WITH AGE - roughens / smooths / intermediate / smoothest throughout
  • LIFE AND COST run together - shortest and cheapest / long and dear / intermediate / longest and dearest
  • ALL FOUR ARE FLOW-DEPENDENT AND ALL PERFORM POORLY AT REST; fouling release is the worst of them for a slow or idle ship
  • REGULATORY EXPOSURE - biocidal, biocidal, biocidal, effectively none
  • SELECTION FACTORS - trading pattern and service speed; proportion of idle and port time; water temperature and fouling severity; INTERVAL TO NEXT DOCKING; newbuilding versus in-service repaint and overcoat compatibility; the surface preparation the yard can deliver
  • there is no merit in buying coating life you will not use
8. What else is in the field, and why this is now a fuel question
  • COPPER-BASED AND COPPER-FREE biocidal systems with organic booster biocides, and increasingly ZINC chemistry
  • HYDROGEL AND HYDROPHILIC-MODIFIED coatings combining fouling release with an enhanced water layer
  • HARD, INERT, NON-TOXIC coatings used with PLANNED IN-WATER CLEANING - no biocide and no depletion, but a scheduled cleaning regime, and a growing regulatory issue in itself
  • NON-COATING approaches - ultrasonic and UV systems; hull-form and niche-area design to reduce sheltered fouling sites
  • FOULING IS NOW A FUEL AND EMISSIONS QUESTION AS MUCH AS AN ENVIRONMENTAL ONE
  • fouling and roughness raise frictional resistance, consumption and carbon intensity; the operational carbon-intensity regime came into force at the start of this year
  • so coating choice is now about MEASURED OPERATING EFFICIENCY ACROSS THE DOCKING CYCLE - which is why SPC smoothing and fouling-release friction are selection criteria and not curiosities
Model written answer

1. Why there are alternatives at all

The stem asks about alternatives to tin-based paints, so the ban is framing and not the answer. Keep it to four lines and move on.

  • Tributyl tin (TBT) self-polishing copolymers were the most effective anti-foulings ever produced. They were also persistent, bioaccumulative and toxic at vanishingly low concentration - causing imposex in marine gastropods and shell deformation in oysters at levels far below those affecting the organisms they were meant to control.
  • The AFS Convention 2001, in force 17 September 2008, prohibits the application or re-application of organotin compounds acting as biocides in anti-fouling systems, and requires that a ship either does not bear such a system or carries a coating forming a barrier to its leaching.
  • The commercial problem the industry then had to solve is worth one sentence, because it explains all four alternatives: replace a biocide that worked extremely well, without losing the controlled, predictable release that made it work.

2. The second ban, and why it bites on this question now

This is what makes the question current rather than historical, and it is the fact most candidates will not have. Resolution MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, entered into force on 1 January 2023 and added cybutryne to the controlled list. From that date:

  • anti-fouling systems containing cybutryne may not be applied or re-applied; and
  • a ship already bearing such a system must have it removed, or overcoated with a sealer coat that prevents leaching, at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system and in any event within 60 months of the last application.

The controls have been in force for about five months at this examination. Why it matters here: cybutryne was used as a booster biocide in some tin-free products, including self-polishing copolymer and hybrid formulations - two of the four types the examiner has named. The regulatory pressure on this question is on the biocidal alternatives, not on the tin they replaced.

It also sets up the comparison at the end: every biocidal system carries the risk that its biocide is next, and that risk is the strongest argument for the non-biocidal option.

3. (i) Controlled Depletion Polymer (CDP)

Controlled Depletion Polymer
PrincipleA soluble-matrix coating. The binder is a rosin-based or rosin-modified system which dissolves slowly in seawater, progressively exposing and releasing the biocide dispersed in it. The modern descendant of the traditional soluble-matrix paint, with the depletion rate controlled by the polymer chemistry rather than left to the rosin alone
Behaviour in serviceThe dissolved layer leaves a leached, porous skeleton at the surface. Release rate is not linear - highest when fresh, falling away as the leached layer thickens and diffusion through it becomes the limiting step
MeritsCheapest of the four. Tolerant of poor surface preparation and of over-application. Easy to apply and to overcoat, and compatible with most existing schemes, which matters at a repair dock. Low capital cost suits short docking intervals and older or lower-value tonnage
LimitsShortest effective life of the four, so it is not suited to long docking intervals. Performance degrades progressively rather than holding. The leached layer increases hull roughness, so the fuel penalty grows through the cycle. Poor at rest - it depends on water movement to erode and does not defend a ship lying idle. Rosin is somewhat brittle and water-sensitive
Where it fitsCoastal and short-sea trades, short docking cycles, vessels where first cost dominates

4. (ii) Self-Polishing Copolymer (SPC)

Self-Polishing Copolymer
PrincipleChemical, not physical. The biocide - or a pendant group - is chemically bonded into an acrylate copolymer backbone. In slightly alkaline seawater the side group hydrolyses, making the surface layer soluble so it is washed away by the passing water, exposing fresh binder and fresh biocide beneath. This is the tin-free descendant of the TBT-SPC principle, using silyl, copper or zinc acrylate chemistry instead of tributyl tin
Behaviour in serviceThe defining feature is a controlled, near-linear polishing rate. The surface is continuously renewed, so release stays steady and no thick leached layer builds up - and the hull actually gets smoother in service rather than rougher
MeritsLong, predictable service life - the type that supports the longest docking intervals of the biocidal three. Consistent antifouling performance to the end of the cycle. Smoothing effect gives a real fuel benefit over the cycle. Predictable enough that the coating life can be matched to the docking plan
LimitsDearer than CDP and demanding of surface preparation and application control. Polishing depends on water flow, so like CDP it performs poorly on a ship lying idle, and the rate varies with speed, temperature and salinity. It is still a biocidal system, so it carries regulatory exposure - which is exactly what the cybutryne control has just demonstrated
Where it fitsThe workhorse of deep-sea trading - long voyages, steady speeds, long docking intervals

If one distinction is to be made clearly in this answer, make this one. CDP dissolves; SPC hydrolyses and is polished away by the water. That is why CDP roughens and SPC smooths, and it is the single sentence the examiner is looking for.

5. (Ill) Hybrid TBT free paint

Note first what this category is not. It is not a paint containing some tin. It is a commercial blend of the two chemistries above - a binder combining rosin or rosin-derivative with a self-polishing copolymer component, in varying proportions.

  • Principle. Both mechanisms operate together: some dissolution of the rosin fraction, some hydrolysis and polishing of the copolymer fraction. Adjusting the ratio moves the product along a continuous scale between CDP and full SPC.
  • Merits. Intermediate cost and intermediate life, which is exactly the point - it lets an owner buy a docking interval he actually needs instead of the one the product offers. More tolerant of application conditions than a full SPC, and generally easier to overcoat and more compatible with existing schemes at a repair yard. Commercially it lets a manufacturer offer a graded range on one platform.
  • Limits. It is a compromise and behaves like one: it neither polishes as cleanly as a true SPC nor costs as little as a CDP, and performance is less predictable because it depends on the blend. It still leaches to some extent and can still build a partial leached layer. And it remains biocidal, with the same regulatory exposure - hybrids were among the products that used cybutryne as a booster, so this category was directly affected by the control that came into force at the start of this year.
  • Where it fits. Mixed trading patterns, moderate docking intervals, and vessels where the full SPC premium is not justified by the operating profile.

6. (iv) Fouling Release paint

This is the one genuine departure in the list. It is not a better biocide - it contains no biocide at all. It does not kill or poison fouling; it makes the surface one that fouling cannot adhere strongly to.

  • Principle. A silicone elastomer - or a fluoropolymer - giving a very low surface energy, very smooth, slightly flexible, non-stick surface. Organisms that do settle attach weakly and are sheared off by the water flow once the ship is up to speed, or removed by gentle cleaning.
  • Merits. No biocide, so it sits outside the biocidal regulatory pressure entirely - the strongest single argument in its favour in a year in which a second biocide has just been banned. The smoothest surface of the four, giving the lowest frictional resistance, which is a direct fuel and carbon-intensity benefit. Long life - it is not consumed, so it does not deplete, and it can last several docking cycles. Cleaning is easier and less damaging.
  • Limits. Highest first cost, often by a wide margin. It requires a full multi-coat scheme - tie coat over the anticorrosive, then the topcoat - and exacting surface preparation and application; it is essentially a newbuilding or major-refit decision rather than something applied at any dock. It is mechanically soft and easily damaged by ice, contact and careless brush cleaning, and local repair is difficult because nothing adheres well to it, including new paint. Above all, it depends on speed: below a threshold service speed there is insufficient shear to release the fouling, so it is poor for slow ships and for ships that lie idle for long periods.
  • Where it fits. Fast, high-utilisation, deep-sea ships with long docking intervals - container ships, car carriers, LNG carriers - and increasingly any owner for whom hull smoothness is now a carbon-intensity question.

7. Choosing between them

The four are not four grades of the same product. They are two biocidal families, one commercial blend of them, and one non-biocidal principle - and once that grouping is stated the comparison writes itself.

CDPSPCHybridFouling release
MechanismMatrix dissolvesBinder hydrolyses and polishesBoth, in proportionNon-stick; fouling is sheared off
BiocideYesYesYesNone
Surface with ageRoughens (leached layer)SmoothsIntermediateSmoothest throughout
Effective lifeShortestLongIntermediateLongest
First costLowestHighIntermediateHighest
At rest / low speedPoorPoorPoorPoorest - needs speed to work
Application demandTolerantDemandingModerateMost demanding; hard to repair
Regulatory exposureBiocidalBiocidalBiocidalEffectively none

The selection factors that actually decide it in practice are the trading pattern and the docking interval: service speed and how much of the time the ship is actually making it; the proportion of idle and port time, since three of the four need water flow and the fourth needs speed; water temperature and fouling severity on the route; the interval to the next docking, because there is no merit in buying coating life you will not use; whether this is a newbuilding or an in-service repaint, since the compatibility of an overcoat with the existing scheme may exclude an option outright; and the surface preparation the yard can actually deliver.

8. What else is in the field, and why this is now a fuel question

  • Copper-based and copper-free biocidal systems using organic booster biocides, and increasingly zinc chemistry - the mainstream tin-free biocidal market from which the four named types are drawn.
  • Hydrogel and hydrophilic-modified coatings, which combine a fouling-release surface with an enhanced water layer.
  • Hard, inert, non-toxic coatings used with planned in-water cleaning - a different trade: no biocide and no depletion, but a scheduled cleaning regime instead, and a growing regulatory issue in its own right because cleaning releases what was on the hull.
  • Non-coating approaches - ultrasonic and UV systems, and hull-form or niche-area design to reduce sheltered fouling sites.

Close on the point that gives the question its present importance. Fouling is now a fuel and emissions question as much as an environmental one. Fouling and roughness increase frictional resistance and therefore consumption and carbon intensity, and the operational carbon-intensity regime entered into force at the beginning of this year. So the coating decision is no longer only about protecting the sea from the paint - it is about the ship's measured operating efficiency across the whole docking cycle. That is why the smoothing behaviour of a self-polishing copolymer, and the low friction of a fouling release surface, are now selection criteria and not merely technical curiosities.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

This question prints no marks, four named types and a single command verb, "discuss". That looks generous and is actually a trap: with sixteen marks and four named limbs there is room for real depth on each, and a candidate who writes four short paragraphs of general description will use half the available marks. The route therefore gives each type its own step with a consistent four-heading treatment - principle, behaviour, merits, limits - and then adds a comparison step and a wider-field step, which are where the discriminating marks are.

Note what the stem does not ask. It does not ask about the Anti-Fouling Convention, the certificate, the declaration or the records. Those belong to a different printing of this material. Here the Convention is framing only, worth four lines, because the words "alternatives to tin-based" require you to say what was banned and why - and nothing more.

The one sentence that separates the top answers

CDP dissolves; SPC hydrolyses and is polished away by the moving water. Everything else about the two follows from it - why CDP roughens as its leached layer builds and SPC smooths, why CDP's release rate falls away and SPC's stays steady, why CDP is cheap and short-lived and SPC is dear and long-lived. If you can write that one line and then derive the consequences, you have shown mechanism rather than memory.

What 'hybrid TBT free' actually means

Almost every weak answer to this question gets the third type wrong, usually by implying it contains some reduced amount of tin. It does not. A hybrid is a blend of the CDP and SPC binder chemistries - rosin or rosin derivative plus a self-polishing copolymer component - in a chosen ratio, so the product sits at a chosen point on the scale between them. "TBT free" is in the name for historical and marketing reasons, distinguishing it from the tin-based hybrids of the past.

Say that explicitly, and add the commercial reason the category exists: it lets an owner buy the docking interval he needs rather than the one the product offers.

Fouling release is a different principle, not a better paint

The fourth type is the only one that is not a biocidal system at all, and treating it as "the expensive one" misses the point. It kills nothing. It presents a low-surface-energy, non-stick, elastomeric surface to which fouling adheres so weakly that water flow shears it off.

That single fact generates all its merits and all its limits at once: no biocide means no regulatory exposure and no depletion, hence long life and the smoothest hull; but non-stick also means nothing else sticks to it either, hence difficult repair and demanding application, and shear-dependence means it is poor for slow ships and ships that lie idle. Deriving both halves from one property is what a "discuss" answer is for.

The current fact almost nobody will have

Cybutryne was added to the controlled list by MEPC.331(76) and the control entered into force on 1 January 2023 - about five months before this examination. From that date it may not be applied or re-applied, and an existing system containing it must be removed or sealed over at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system and in any event within 60 months of the last application.

It is worth a mark twice over. First, because it is genuinely current at this sitting. Second, because it lands on the biocidal types - cybutryne was used as a booster biocide in some SPC and hybrid products - which means the regulatory pressure in this question is now on the alternatives, not on the tin they replaced. That is the observation that makes the whole answer feel contemporary, and it is what justifies the closing argument for the non-biocidal option.

Why this is also a fuel question

Fouling and roughness raise frictional resistance, which raises consumption, which raises carbon intensity - and the operational carbon-intensity requirements came into force at the beginning of this same year. So coating selection is now an energy-efficiency decision measured across the docking cycle, which is why the smoothing behaviour of an SPC and the low friction of a fouling release surface have become selection criteria rather than technical footnotes. One paragraph on this at the end lifts the answer out of the paint shop.

Common mistakes

  • Writing a long answer on the Anti-Fouling Convention, the certificate and the records. Not asked here.
  • Describing CDP and SPC as the same mechanism at different speeds. One dissolves, the other hydrolyses and polishes.
  • Saying CDP gets smoother with age. It roughens - the leached layer is the reason.
  • Implying a hybrid contains some tin. It is a blend of the two tin-free binder chemistries.
  • Calling fouling release a biocidal paint, or a "very good antifouling". It contains no biocide and kills nothing.
  • Omitting that fouling release is speed-dependent and therefore poor for slow or idle ships - the limitation examiners ask about most.
  • Not knowing about cybutryne, which is the one genuinely current regulatory fact available here.
  • Quoting service lives in months or fuel savings as percentages. These are product-specific, commercial and easy to get wrong.
  • Naming manufacturers or products.
  • Giving four descriptions and no comparison. With sixteen marks and no printed split, the comparison is where the top band is.

Examiner traps

  • "Does a hybrid contain tin?" No. It is a blend of rosin-based and self-polishing copolymer chemistries.
  • "Which coating gets smoother in service, and why?" SPC - the surface is continuously renewed by hydrolysis and shear, so no leached layer accumulates.
  • "Your ship lies at anchor for two months. Which coating suffers most?" All the flow-dependent ones suffer, but fouling release suffers most - it needs speed to shear the fouling off.
  • "How does fouling release work?" Low surface energy and elasticity; weak adhesion; fouling is sheared off by flow. No biocide.
  • "Why is fouling release hard to repair?" Because nothing adheres well to it - including new paint.
  • "What changed in anti-fouling regulation this year?" Cybutryne controls entered into force on 1 January 2023.
  • "What must a ship do if her existing system contains cybutryne?" Remove it or apply a sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system, and within 60 months of the last application.
  • "Which of the four is outside biocidal regulation?" Fouling release - and that is now a commercial argument, not just an environmental one.
  • "Which would you choose for a short-sea vessel with a two-year docking cycle?" CDP or a rosin-rich hybrid - there is no merit in buying coating life you will not use.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Why were tin-based antifoulings banned? Persistence, bioaccumulation, imposex in gastropods and shell deformation in oysters at very low concentrations.
  • When did the AFS Convention enter into force? 17 September 2008.
  • How does a CDP work? A rosin-based soluble matrix dissolves in seawater, exposing and releasing the biocide dispersed in it.
  • How does an SPC work? The biocide or a pendant group is chemically bonded into an acrylate copolymer; hydrolysis in seawater makes the surface layer soluble and it is polished away by the flow.
  • Which chemistries replaced tributyl tin in SPCs? Silyl, copper and zinc acrylates.
  • What is a hybrid? A blend of rosin and self-polishing copolymer binders, giving intermediate cost, life and predictability.
  • How does fouling release differ in principle? It contains no biocide; low surface energy and elasticity make adhesion weak so fouling is sheared off by flow.
  • What are its two main practical drawbacks? Speed dependence, and mechanical vulnerability with difficult local repair.
  • What is cybutryne and what happened to it? A booster biocide, added to the controlled list by MEPC.331(76), controls in force 1 January 2023.
  • How does coating choice affect the ship's carbon intensity? Through hull roughness and fouling, which raise frictional resistance and consumption across the docking cycle.

Memory framework

Group them before you describe them: TWO BIOCIDAL FAMILIES, ONE BLEND, ONE NON-STICK. That is the whole taxonomy and it stops the four blurring together.

For the mechanisms, four verbs: DISSOLVES · POLISHES · BOTH · RELEASES. For the surface with age, three words: ROUGHENS · SMOOTHS · SMOOTHEST. For cost and life, remember they run together: CHEAP AND SHORT · DEAR AND LONG · MIDDLE · DEAREST AND LONGEST.

Treat every type under the same four headings - PRINCIPLE, BEHAVIOUR, MERITS, LIMITS - so the answer is visibly systematic and nothing is forgotten under one type that was remembered under another.

Regulation and source map

  • AFS Convention 2001 - adopted October 2001, in force 17 September 2008
  • AFS Annex 1 - organotin compounds acting as biocides: not applied or re-applied; otherwise a barrier coating
  • AFS Annex 4 - survey and certification; International Anti-Fouling System Certificate for 400 GT and above on international voyages; Declaration for 24 m and above but under 400 GT. Framing only - not asked on this paper
  • Resolution MEPC.331(76) - adopted 17 June 2021, in force 1 January 2023; adds cybutryne. Removal or sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system, and within 60 months of last application
  • Resolution MEPC.358(78) - 2022 Guidelines for survey and certification of anti-fouling systems; the operative guidance at this sitting
  • Coating chemistry and behaviour - industry guidance, stated qualitatively; no service life, polishing rate, saving percentage or price asserted

Uncertainty and the boundary between regulation and industry practice

The regulatory half of this answer is verified. The AFS Convention position and the cybutryne chain come from MIW's own verification record for a sitting at which the Convention itself was read, and the elapsed interval was recalculated for this examination rather than carried across - about five months, not the days that were correct for a January 2023 sitting and not the fifteen months that will be correct for an April 2024 one. The boundary is day-independent here: the controls took effect on the first day of January and this sitting is in June, so no reasoning about the examination day is needed.

The coating technology itself is industry guidance and the answer says so. Release mechanisms, polishing behaviour, relative service life and surface-energy behaviour are drawn from coating-manufacturer technical literature and are stated qualitatively. Accordingly no service life in months, no polishing or leaching rate, no dry-film thickness, no fuel-saving percentage and no price comparison is asserted anywhere, and no product or manufacturer is named. Relative behaviour - which type holds performance longer, which roughens, which is dearest - is what the question asks for, and it is what is given.

One instruction for a later editor: the Convention-and-documents material must not be added back into this answer. It belongs to a different printing of the same limb, which prints a second limb this paper does not. Adding it would spend half a sixteen-mark question on something the examiner did not ask.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing Why there are alternatives at all
  2. __________framing The second ban, and why it bites on this question now
  3. __________(i) (i) Controlled Depletion Polymer (CDP)
  4. __________(ii) (ii) Self-Polishing Copolymer (SPC)
  5. __________(Ill) (Ill) Hybrid TBT free paint
  6. __________(iv) (iv) Fouling Release paint
  7. __________comparison Choosing between them
  8. __________wider field What else is in the field, and why this is now a fuel question
Flashcards 12

1 Why alternatives exist - the tin ban, briefly. 2 The cybutryne control, in force this January. 3 CDP. 4 SPC. 5 Hybrid. 6 Fouling release. 7 Comparison and selection. 8 The wider field and the fuel link. Each type gets the same four headings - principle, behaviour, merits, limits.

Sixteen marks with no printed split; the comparison at step 7 is where the top band is, and step 1 must stay short.

A CDP is a soluble matrix: a rosin-based binder dissolves in seawater, progressively exposing and releasing the biocide dispersed in it, leaving a leached porous layer behind. An SPC is chemical: the biocide or a pendant group is chemically bonded into an acrylate copolymer, and hydrolysis in seawater makes the surface layer soluble so it is polished away by the passing water, exposing fresh binder beneath.

This one distinction generates every difference between the two, including roughness and service life.

CDP roughens, because the undissolved fraction remains as a leached porous skeleton that accumulates. SPC smooths, because the surface is continuously renewed by hydrolysis and shear so no leached layer builds up. Fouling release is the smoothest of all throughout, being neither consumed nor depleted.

It converts a chemistry point into a fuel-consumption consequence, which is where the marks now are.

No. It is a blend of the two tin-free binder chemistries - rosin or a rosin derivative together with a self-polishing copolymer component - in a chosen ratio, so the product sits at a chosen point on the scale between CDP and full SPC. 'TBT free' is in the name to distinguish it from the tin-based hybrids of the past.

Almost every weak answer implies the hybrid retains some tin.

It contains no biocide. A silicone elastomer or fluoropolymer presents a very low surface energy, very smooth, slightly flexible non-stick surface, so organisms that settle adhere weakly and are sheared off by the water flow once the ship is up to speed, or removed by gentle cleaning.

It is the only one of the four that is a different principle rather than a different chemistry.

All three follow from its non-stick, soft, low-energy surface. It is speed-dependent - below a threshold service speed there is insufficient shear, so it is poor for slow ships and ships lying idle. It is mechanically soft and easily damaged. And local repair is difficult because nothing adheres well to it, including new paint. Add the high first cost and the need for a full multi-coat scheme with exacting preparation.

Deriving merits and limits from one property is what the command verb 'discuss' is asking for.

A booster biocide used in some tin-free anti-fouling products. Resolution MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, added it to the AFS Convention controlled list with effect from 1 January 2023. From that date it may not be applied or re-applied, and an existing system containing it must be removed or overcoated with a sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system and within 60 months of the last application.

It is the one genuinely current regulatory fact available on this question at this sitting.

Because it falls on the biocidal alternatives, not on the tin they replaced. Cybutryne was used as a booster biocide in some self-polishing copolymer and hybrid products - two of the four types the examiner names - so the regulatory pressure has moved onto the substitutes. It is also the strongest argument for the non-biocidal option: every biocidal system carries the risk that its biocide is next.

It turns a list of paints into an argument about where the technology is heading.

The Convention was adopted in October 2001 and entered into force on 17 September 2008. Organotin compounds acting as biocides in anti-fouling systems shall not be applied or re-applied, and a ship shall either not bear such a system or shall carry a coating forming a barrier to its leaching.

Framing only on this paper - the certificate and records limb is not printed here.

Two biocidal families - CDP and SPC - one commercial blend of them, and one non-biocidal principle. The choice is decided by trading pattern and service speed, the proportion of idle and port time, water temperature and fouling severity on the route, the interval to the next docking, whether it is a newbuilding or an in-service repaint with overcoat compatibility to consider, and the surface preparation the yard can actually deliver.

Stating the grouping first is what makes the comparison answerable in the space available.

A CDP or a rosin-rich hybrid. There is no merit in buying coating life that will not be used, the first cost is lowest, application is tolerant, and overcoating at a repair yard is easy. A fouling release scheme would be the wrong choice on cost, on the difficulty of application in a repair dock, and above all on speed - short-sea trading with much manoeuvring and port time gives too little shear to release the fouling.

The examiner wants a selection judgement, not four descriptions.

Because fouling and hull roughness increase frictional resistance, and therefore fuel consumption and carbon intensity, across the whole docking cycle - and the operational carbon-intensity requirements entered into force at the beginning of this same year. So the smoothing behaviour of a self-polishing copolymer and the low friction of a fouling release surface have become selection criteria rather than technical footnotes.

It lifts the answer out of the paint shop and connects it to the rest of the paper.

Quick revision
15-second recall
FRAME (keep short). TBT-SPC was the best antifouling ever made, and persistent, bioaccumulative and toxic at tiny concentrations - imposex in gastropods, shell deformation in oysters. AFS Convention 2001, in force 17 September 2008: organotin biocides not applied or re-applied, else a barrier coating. CURRENT FACT: MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, in force 1 January 2023 - adds cybutryne; no application or re-application; existing systems removed or sealer-coated at the next scheduled renewal and within 60 months of last application. Five months old at this sitting, and it lands on SPC and hybrid products that used it as a booster - so the pressure is now on the alternatives. (i) CDP - soluble matrix; rosin-based binder DISSOLVES, exposing biocide. Leaves a leached porous layer; release non-linear, falling with age. Cheapest, tolerant of poor preparation, easy to overcoat, compatible with most schemes. Shortest life, roughens with age so the fuel penalty grows, poor at rest, rosin brittle. Short-sea, short docking cycles. (ii) SPC - chemical: biocide/pendant group bonded into an acrylate copolymer; seawater hydrolyses the side group, the surface layer dissolves and is polished away by the flow. Silyl, copper, zinc acrylate. Near-linear polishing rate, steady release, no leached layer - the hull SMOOTHS. Long, predictable life; best fuel behaviour of the biocidal three. Dearer; demanding application; flow-dependent, varies with speed, temperature, salinity; still biocidal. Deep-sea workhorse. (Ill) HYBRID - NOT tin-containing: a blend of rosin and SPC binders. Both mechanisms; the ratio sets the position between CDP and SPC. Intermediate cost and life - buy the docking interval you need; more application-tolerant; easier to overcoat. Compromise: less predictable, still leaches, still biocidal - hybrids used cybutryne. (iv) FOULING RELEASE - NO BIOCIDE. Silicone elastomer or fluoropolymer, very low surface energy, smooth, slightly flexible; fouling adheres weakly and is sheared off by flow. No regulatory exposure; smoothest surface, lowest friction; longest life, not consumed. Dearest; full multi-coat scheme with tie coat; exacting preparation; soft, easily damaged, hard to repair because nothing sticks to it; SPEED-DEPENDENT - poor for slow or idle ships. Fast, high-utilisation deep-sea. COMPARE: two biocidal families, one blend, one non-stick. Dissolves / polishes / both / releases. Roughens / smooths / intermediate / smoothest. Cheap-short / dear-long / middle / dearest-longest. Select on trading pattern and speed, idle time, water temperature, interval to next docking, newbuild versus repaint and overcoat compatibility, achievable surface preparation. ALSO IN THE FIELD: copper and copper-free biocidal systems, zinc chemistry, hydrogel, hard inert coatings with planned in-water cleaning, ultrasonic and UV. CLOSE: fouling and roughness raise resistance, consumption and carbon intensity, and the operational carbon-intensity regime came into force this January - so coating choice is now an efficiency decision across the docking cycle.
Keywords

Critical numbers
  • AFS Convention 2001 - in force 17 September 2008
  • MEPC.331(76) - adopted 17 June 2021, in force 1 January 2023, adding cybutryne
  • Cybutryne: removal or sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system and within 60 months of the last application
  • MEPC.358(78) - 2022 Guidelines for survey and certification, operative at this sitting
  • NO service life, polishing rate, dry-film thickness, fuel-saving percentage or price is asserted
Critical regulation
The AFS Convention 2001, in force 17 September 2008, prohibits the application or re-application of organotin compounds acting as biocides in anti-fouling systems, requiring instead that a ship either bears no such system or carries a coating forming a barrier to leaching. Resolution MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021 and in force 1 January 2023, extends the controlled list to cybutryne: it may not be applied or re-applied, and an existing system containing it must be removed or overcoated with a sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system and in any event within 60 months of the last application.
Major trap
Two traps, and the first is about marks rather than knowledge. This stem asks only about the four coating types - it does not ask about the Anti-Fouling Convention, the certificate or the records - so a candidate who has revised the two-limb version of this question will spend half a sixteen-mark answer on material nobody asked for. The technical traps are: describing CDP and SPC as the same mechanism at different speeds, when one dissolves and the other hydrolyses and is polished away - which is why CDP roughens and SPC smooths; implying a hybrid contains some tin when it is a blend of the two tin-free binder chemistries; treating fouling release as a very good antifouling paint when it contains no biocide and kills nothing; and omitting that fouling release is speed-dependent and therefore poor for slow or idle ships. Finally, not knowing about cybutryne - in force since 1 January 2023 - forfeits the one genuinely current fact on the question.

Also on the platform: QP2306 Q1 - UNCLOS article 194(3)(b), the treaty obligation that anti-fouling control implements · QP2306 Q9 - skin friction reduction, where hull surface condition is attacked from the other direction · QP2306 Q7 - the SEEMP, whose planning stage is where a coating decision is actually taken

Repeated — reworded Returns reworded from January 2023 Q2. Also set at April 2024 Q5, September 2024 Q8.

Understand this first

A repair contract looks like five separate commercial topics and is really one. Somebody is going to spend a large sum of money on work whose full extent nobody can know until the ship is opened up, in a yard that may be on the other side of the world, while the ship earns nothing. Every clause in the agreement is an answer to the same question - if this goes wrong, whose problem is it? Who pays if the job costs more than expected; who pays if it turns out to include something the parties never wrote down; who pays if it takes longer; who pays if it fails afterwards; and what happens if the whole arrangement collapses. Two practical realities sit underneath all of it. The first is that once the ship is in the dock, the yard is holding the thing the owner needs back, so every term worth having must be settled before she arrives - including, specifically, the terms on which she is allowed to leave if there is an argument about the bill. The second is that for the owner the expensive risk is almost never the price of the work; it is the days. A ship in dock is earning nothing, so an extra week can cost more than the item being argued over, which is why the machinery for ordering extra work and for handling delay deserves more attention than the headline figure. Read the five headings that way and they stop being five topics: they are cost, scope, time, defect, and what happens when the arrangement fails.

Knowledge map
Key Issues in Ship Repair Contracts
  1. framing
    1. 1What a repair contract is, and the one idea behind all five limbs
      • defined work, on a defined vessel, in a defined time, for a defined price
      • contract documents - agreement, general conditions, SPECIFICATION, quotation, clarifications, plus VARIATION ORDERS, with an ORDER OF PRECEDENCE clause
      • THE FIVE PRINTED ISSUES ARE FIVE ALLOCATIONS OF THE SAME FOUR RISKS - COST, SCOPE, TIME, DEFECT
  2. A
    1. 2A. Payment terms
      • LUMP SUM for fully specified work - price certainty, productivity risk on the yard
      • SCHEDULE OF RATES for quantum-uncertain work - steel by weight, pipe by length, coating by area, staging, gas-freeing, day rates by trade; PROVISIONAL SUMS for open-and-inspect
      • ILLUSTRATION - a tank top 'open, inspect, renew wasted plate'. Agree the rate per tonne BEFORE the tender is accepted, or negotiate it with the tank open and the ship immobilised
  3. B
    1. 3B. Ambit of the specification and additional work
      • THE SPECIFICATION IS THE CONTRACT'S DEFINITION OF THE WORK AND IS WHERE COST IS ACTUALLY CONTROLLED
      • EVERY AMBIGUITY IS RESOLVED ON THE DAY, IN THE YARD'S FAVOUR, AT THE YARD'S PRICE
      • draft to close gaps - workmanship standard and class rules; staging, gas-freeing, cleaning, protection, debris removal, reinstatement, access work; who supplies what
  4. C
    1. 4C. Contract period, liquidated damages and force majeure
      • the CONTRACT PERIOD runs from arrival or docking to redelivery; it should also fix a date by which the OWNER must deliver the vessel
      • LIQUIDATED DAMAGES - a pre-agreed sum PER DAY, usually after a GRACE PERIOD
      • CAPPED at a proportion of the contract price - the cap matters as much as the rate
  5. D
    1. 5D. Guarantees and insurance
      • GUARANTEE OR DEFECTS LIABILITY PERIOD - the yard makes good defects due to DEFECTIVE WORKMANSHIP OR MATERIALS appearing within the period from redelivery; commonly about a year, NEGOTIATED, not a rule
      • THE REMEDY IS DEFINED - the yard's option to repair at its own yard OR to bear the REASONABLE COST of repair elsewhere on a pre-approved quotation
      • notice of the defect within a stated period after discovery, and access for the yard to inspect
  6. E
    1. 6E. Termination events
      • termination rights must be DEFINED, MUTUAL AND CONSEQUENTIAL
      • OWNER MAY TERMINATE - yard's INSOLVENCY; DELAY BEYOND THE LIQUIDATED DAMAGES CAP or a long-stop date; ABANDONMENT or failure to proceed with due diligence; unremedied MATERIAL BREACH after notice; PROLONGED FORCE MAJEURE
      • YARD MAY TERMINATE - owner's FAILURE TO PAY after notice; owner's INSOLVENCY; failure to DELIVER THE VESSEL by the agreed date or removal of her; the same prolonged force majeure
  7. closing
    1. 7The two clauses the question does not name, but which decide all five
      • GOVERNING LAW AND DISPUTE RESOLUTION - yard, owner and ship are usually in three different countries
      • every right in limbs A to E is worth what it costs to enforce, so a clear governing law and a workable ARBITRATION provision - seat, rules, language - is what gives the others value
      • consider EXPERT DETERMINATION or a short-form procedure for the small urgent technical dispute that actually arises mid-docking, when arbitration is far too slow
