Memory cue. Map the five limbs to four risks and an exit: COST (2), SCOPE (3), TIME (4), DEFECT (5), EXIT (6). One hook inside each: payment is THE YARD HAS THE SHIP (2); specification is EVERY GAP IS AN EXTRA (3); delay is THE VARIATION MUST BUY THE TIME TOO (4); guarantees are THEY FIX THE PART, YOU PAY THE OFF-HIRE (5); termination is SEAWORTHY AND TOWABLE (6). Close on the two unnamed clauses - WHICH LAW, AND WHICH DOCUMENT WINS (7).

Remember7 route headings
Cover67 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 67 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What a repair contract is, and the one idea behind all five limbs
  • defined work, on a defined vessel, in a defined time, for a defined price
  • contract documents - agreement, general conditions, SPECIFICATION, quotation, clarifications, plus VARIATION ORDERS, with an ORDER OF PRECEDENCE clause
  • THE FIVE PRINTED ISSUES ARE FIVE ALLOCATIONS OF THE SAME FOUR RISKS - COST, SCOPE, TIME, DEFECT
  • every clause answers one question: WHO CARRIES THIS IF IT GOES WRONG?
  • FACT ONE - THE YARD HAS THE SHIP. Once docked, the owner's leverage is gone; agree everything commercially important BEFORE she arrives
  • FACT TWO - THE OWNER'S EXPOSURE IS TIME, NOT PRICE. A dispute that adds a week can cost more than the item in dispute
  • the command verb is ILLUSTRATE - each limb must be shown working
2. A. Payment terms
  • LUMP SUM for fully specified work - price certainty, productivity risk on the yard
  • SCHEDULE OF RATES for quantum-uncertain work - steel by weight, pipe by length, coating by area, staging, gas-freeing, day rates by trade; PROVISIONAL SUMS for open-and-inspect
  • ILLUSTRATION - a tank top 'open, inspect, renew wasted plate'. Agree the rate per tonne BEFORE the tender is accepted, or negotiate it with the tank open and the ship immobilised
  • STAGE PAYMENTS on docking, undocking and redelivery; RETENTION held until the guarantee expires so there is money behind the guarantee
  • invoices supported by measured quantities and SIGNED VARIATION ORDERS, certified by the owner's superintendent; disputed items severable - but yards commonly EXCLUDE SET-OFF, so pay first and argue after
  • currency and exchange risk, escalation, taxes, port and dock dues - where headline price and final invoice diverge
  • SECURITY BOTH WAYS - owner's payment security; yard's REFUND OR PERFORMANCE GUARANTEE from a bank of standing
  • THE LIEN DOMINATES THE LIMB - a repairer in possession will assert a right to RETAIN THE SHIP until paid
  • so the contract must fix what is payable on redelivery and WHAT SECURITY may be offered instead of cash for a disputed sum. NEGOTIATE THE EXIT BEFORE YOU DOCK
3. B. Ambit of the specification and additional work
  • THE SPECIFICATION IS THE CONTRACT'S DEFINITION OF THE WORK AND IS WHERE COST IS ACTUALLY CONTROLLED
  • EVERY AMBIGUITY IS RESOLVED ON THE DAY, IN THE YARD'S FAVOUR, AT THE YARD'S PRICE
  • draft to close gaps - workmanship standard and class rules; staging, gas-freeing, cleaning, protection, debris removal, reinstatement, access work; who supplies what
  • 'renew valve' without saying who provides, lands, tests and re-lags it has left FOUR items to be charged as extras
  • OPEN-AND-INSPECT items handled by RATE, with a defined inspection standard and who determines it - usually class, attending
  • THE VARIATION PROCEDURE IS THE HEART OF THE LIMB - ordered IN WRITING by a NAMED owner's representative BEFORE the work is done
  • the variation order records the work, THE PRICE, and - most often omitted - THE EFFECT ON THE DELIVERY DATE
  • ILLUSTRATION - stern tube bearing wear found on opening. Record only the price and the yard will say the extra work extended the docking and liquidated damages are disapplied. AGREE TIME AND MONEY IN THE SAME DOCUMENT
  • owner-supplied items and OWNER'S OWN SUBCONTRACTORS - access, hours, yard's facility charge, safety regime, responsibility split
  • REWORK - the yard bears it where its workmanship or material is at fault; a change of requirement is a variation
4. C. Contract period, liquidated damages and force majeure
  • the CONTRACT PERIOD runs from arrival or docking to redelivery; it should also fix a date by which the OWNER must deliver the vessel
  • LIQUIDATED DAMAGES - a pre-agreed sum PER DAY, usually after a GRACE PERIOD
  • CAPPED at a proportion of the contract price - the cap matters as much as the rate
  • normally the owner's SOLE AND EXCLUSIVE REMEDY for delay, excluding any claim for actual lost earnings
  • with a RIGHT TO TERMINATE once the cap is reached - what stops an indefinite delay being merely an expensive one
  • PERMISSIBLE DELAYS extend the date - additional work ordered, owner default, late owner-supplied items, force majeure
  • JURISDICTION-DEPENDENT CAUTION - the sum must be a GENUINE PRE-ESTIMATE OF LOSS; a figure fixed to punish may be attacked as a PENALTY and be unenforceable
  • FORCE MAJEURE IS A CREATURE OF CONTRACT - in common-law systems there is NO GENERAL DOCTRINE; without a clause there is only the narrow doctrine of FRUSTRATION
  • DEFINED EVENTS - act of God, war and civil commotion, strikes, fire, flood and severe weather, epidemic, government or port authority action, unavailability of essential materials; beyond reasonable control and not self-caused
  • PROCEDURE - prompt written notice within a stated time, duty to MITIGATE and resume, notice when it ends. Notice provisions are conditions and are commonly missed
  • EFFECT - AN EXTENSION OF TIME, NOT MONEY; each side bears its own costs; termination by EITHER party if it persists beyond a stated period
  • ILLUSTRATION - a three-week yard strike moves the date three weeks, suspends liquidated damages, and the owner recovers nothing for lost earnings
5. D. Guarantees and insurance
  • GUARANTEE OR DEFECTS LIABILITY PERIOD - the yard makes good defects due to DEFECTIVE WORKMANSHIP OR MATERIALS appearing within the period from redelivery; commonly about a year, NEGOTIATED, not a rule
  • THE REMEDY IS DEFINED - the yard's option to repair at its own yard OR to bear the REASONABLE COST of repair elsewhere on a pre-approved quotation
  • notice of the defect within a stated period after discovery, and access for the yard to inspect
  • EXCLUSIONS ARE WHAT THE ARGUMENT IS ABOUT - fair wear and tear, owner's misuse or neglect, owner-supplied materials, and ALL CONSEQUENTIAL LOSS INCLUDING LOSS OF TIME, HIRE AND PROFIT
  • SO THE YARD REPLACES THE COMPONENT AND THE OWNER BEARS THE OFF-HIRE
  • supported by the RETENTION and, on larger contracts, a bank or parent company guarantee
  • INSURANCE - owner's H&M continues but underwriters must be NOTIFIED of port risk, immobilisation, hot work and layup
  • YARD'S SHIP REPAIRERS' LIABILITY for damage to the vessel in its CARE, CUSTODY AND CONTROL - agree a minimum limit and require the certificate BEFORE docking
  • yard's third-party and employer's liability; owner's P&I notified where crew remain on board
  • WAIVER OF SUBROGATION or knock-for-knock, so the two insurers do not sue each other
  • ILLUSTRATION - FIRE DURING HOT WORK is the classic repair-yard loss; who pays turns on care, custody and control, the permit regime and the subrogation waiver
6. E. Termination events
  • termination rights must be DEFINED, MUTUAL AND CONSEQUENTIAL
  • OWNER MAY TERMINATE - yard's INSOLVENCY; DELAY BEYOND THE LIQUIDATED DAMAGES CAP or a long-stop date; ABANDONMENT or failure to proceed with due diligence; unremedied MATERIAL BREACH after notice; PROLONGED FORCE MAJEURE
  • YARD MAY TERMINATE - owner's FAILURE TO PAY after notice; owner's INSOLVENCY; failure to DELIVER THE VESSEL by the agreed date or removal of her; the same prolonged force majeure
  • CONSEQUENCES ARE WHERE THE VALUE LIES - a clause that stops at 'either party may terminate' is nearly useless
  • VALUATION AND PAYMENT for work done and materials ordered or fabricated to the ship's specification
  • RELEASE OF THE VESSEL AND SECURITY FOR THE LIEN - on what terms she may leave and what security covers a disputed balance. AGREE THIS BEFORE DOCKING
  • CONDITION ON REDELIVERY - SEAWORTHY AND TOWABLE, openings closed and systems reinstated so far as practicable
  • RETURN of owner-supplied materials, equipment, drawings and technical information
  • REPAYMENT OF ADVANCES called under the refund guarantee where the yard has failed
  • REMOVAL AND TOW - who arranges and who pays
  • SURVIVAL of confidentiality, guarantee, insurance and DISPUTE clauses
7. The two clauses the question does not name, but which decide all five
  • GOVERNING LAW AND DISPUTE RESOLUTION - yard, owner and ship are usually in three different countries
  • every right in limbs A to E is worth what it costs to enforce, so a clear governing law and a workable ARBITRATION provision - seat, rules, language - is what gives the others value
  • consider EXPERT DETERMINATION or a short-form procedure for the small urgent technical dispute that actually arises mid-docking, when arbitration is far too slow
  • ORDER OF PRECEDENCE - the specification, general conditions, quotation and clarifications WILL conflict; say which prevails
  • without it the parties argue about which document governs before they can argue about the point in dispute
  • RECOGNISED INDUSTRY STANDARD FORMS EXIST FOR SHIP REPAIR and their value is that these five issues are already allocated in a way both sides recognise
  • starting from a known form and negotiating the schedule is faster, cheaper and safer than drafting each docking from scratch
Model written answer

1. What a repair contract is, and the one idea behind all five limbs

A ship repair contract is an agreement to carry out defined work on a defined vessel, in a defined time, for a defined price. The contract documents normally comprise the agreement, the general conditions, the repair specification, the yard's quotation, and any agreed clarifications - with an order of precedence clause saying which prevails if they conflict - together with the variation orders issued during the docking.

The command verb is illustrate, so each limb below is shown working. The organising idea is simple: the five printed issues are five allocations of the same four risks - cost, scope, time and defect - and each clause exists to answer one question: who carries this if it goes wrong? Two facts colour everything:

  • The yard has the ship. Once she is docked, the owner's leverage is largely gone and the yard's is at its maximum. Every commercially important term must therefore be agreed before she arrives.
  • The owner's real exposure is time, not price. A ship in dock earns nothing. A dispute that adds a week may cost more than the item in dispute is worth - which is why the delay and variation machinery matters more than the headline price.

2. A. Payment terms

  • The pricing basis. A lump sum for the specified work gives price certainty and puts the productivity risk on the yard, but only for work that is fully specified. Because much dry-dock work is not, it is combined with a schedule of rates - steel renewal by weight, pipe by length, tank cleaning and coating by area, staging, gas-freeing, and day rates by trade - and provisional sums for open-and-inspect items.
  • Illustration. A tank top is specified as "open, inspect, renew wasted plate". Nobody knows the tonnage until it is opened. If a rate per tonne of renewed steel, fitted and coated, is agreed before the tender is accepted, the owner has price control over an unknown quantity. If it is not, he is negotiating that rate on the day, with the tank open and the ship immobilised - and he will pay a great deal more.
  • Stage payments tied to identifiable events - on arrival or docking, on undocking, and the balance on redelivery - so that neither side is far out of pocket at any point, with a retention held until the guarantee period expires so that there is money behind the guarantee.
  • Certification and disputed items. Invoices supported by measured quantities and signed variation orders, certified by the owner's superintendent. Disputed items should be severable, so that a disagreement over one item does not stop the whole payment - though note that yards commonly exclude the owner's right of set-off, which means paying first and arguing afterwards.
  • Currency, escalation, taxes and dues. The currency of account and who bears exchange movement; whether rates are firm for the contract period; and the allocation of port, dock and berth dues, tugs, waste disposal and local taxes - which is where a headline price and a final invoice most often diverge.
  • Security both ways. The yard wants payment security or an advance; the owner wants a refund or performance guarantee from a bank of standing, so that an advance is recoverable if the yard fails.
  • The lien, which dominates the limb. A repairer in possession will in practice assert a right to retain the ship until his account is settled. Whatever the position in the applicable law, the commercial reality is the same and the contract must deal with it: what is payable on redelivery, what security - a bank guarantee or a P&I letter of undertaking - the owner may offer instead of cash for a disputed sum, and on what terms the ship sails. Negotiate the exit before you dock, not after.

3. B. Ambit of the specification and additional work

  • The specification is the contract's definition of the work, and it is where cost is actually controlled. Everything the yard is obliged to do, and everything the price covers, is fixed by it. Every ambiguity is resolved on the day, in the yard's favour, at the yard's price.
  • Draft it to close gaps. State the standard of workmanship and the applicable class rules; state what is included by implication - staging, gas-freeing, cleaning, protection, removal of debris, reinstatement, cropping and refitting of insulation and pipework to get access; and state what the yard supplies as against what the owner supplies. A specification that says "renew valve" without saying who provides the valve, who lands it, who tests it and who reinstates the lagging has left four items to be charged as extras.
  • Open-and-inspect items are the standing problem of ship repair. Handle them by rate, as in limb A, and by defining the inspection standard and who determines it - usually class, attending.
  • The variation procedure is the heart of this limb. Additional work must be ordered in writing by a named owner's representative before it is performed, on a numbered variation order recording the work, the price, and - the part most often omitted - the effect on the delivery date.
  • Illustration. The ship is docked for a fifteen-day period. Opening a stern tube reveals bearing wear that must be dealt with. If the variation order records only the price, the owner has bought the work and inadvertently surrendered his delay remedy: the yard will say the extra work extended the docking and the liquidated damages clause is disapplied for that period. Agree the time and the money in the same document, or the delay clause in limb C is worth nothing.
  • Owner-supplied items and the owner's own subcontractors. Specialists - class-approved service suppliers, coating inspectors, makers' technicians - frequently work in the yard on the owner's account. Agree access, working hours, the yard's charge for facilities, the safety regime and the split of responsibility, or the two sides will each blame the other for delay.
  • Rework. State who bears the cost if class or the flag surveyor rejects work - the yard, where the workmanship or material is at fault - and distinguish that from a change of requirement, which is a variation.

4. C. Contract period, liquidated damages and force majeure

The contract period runs from an agreed start - commonly arrival at the yard or docking - to redelivery, and is the yard's time to perform. It should also fix a date by which the owner must deliver the vessel, because a berth held empty is the yard's loss.

Liquidated damages are the agreed remedy for late redelivery:

  • a pre-agreed sum per day of delay, usually after a short grace period;
  • capped at a proportion of the contract price - and the cap matters as much as the rate, because it is the yard's worst case;
  • normally the owner's sole and exclusive remedy for delay, excluding any claim for actual loss of earnings;
  • with a right to terminate once the cap is reached, which is what stops an indefinite delay becoming merely an expensive one.
  • Permissible delays must be listed and they extend the date rather than trigger damages: additional work ordered under limb B, the owner's own default, late owner-supplied items, and force majeure.
  • The legal caution, which is jurisdiction-dependent: the sum should be a genuine pre-estimate of the loss the owner would suffer. A figure fixed to punish rather than to compensate may be attacked as a penalty and be unenforceable, leaving the owner to prove his actual loss - so a rate loosely tied to prevailing charter earnings is defensible, and an arbitrary large number is not.

Force majeure relieves a party of liability for failure caused by events beyond its reasonable control. Four points, and the first is the one candidates miss:

  1. In common-law systems force majeure is a creature of contract. There is no general doctrine to fall back on; a party with no force majeure clause has only the very narrow doctrine of frustration. If it is not drafted, it does not exist.
  2. The events must be defined, usually as a list - act of God, war and civil commotion, strikes and labour disputes, fire, flood and severe weather, epidemic, government or port authority action, and unavailability of essential materials - with the qualification that they be beyond the reasonable control of the affected party and not caused by its own default.
  3. Procedure. Prompt written notice within a stated time, with particulars; a duty to mitigate and to resume as soon as possible; and notice when the event ends. Notice provisions are conditions and are commonly missed in practice.
  4. Effect. An extension of time, not money - each side generally bears its own costs - and, if the event persists beyond a stated period, a right for either party to terminate.

Illustration. A yard-wide strike stops work for three weeks. Under the clause the yard gives notice, the redelivery date moves by three weeks, liquidated damages do not run for that period, and the owner recovers nothing for the lost earnings. If the strike ran for months and passed the stated period, either party could walk away - which is why the length of that period is negotiated as carefully as the daily rate.

5. D. Guarantees and insurance

The guarantee, or defects liability period, is the owner's protection after redelivery:

  • the yard makes good, at its own cost, defects due to defective workmanship or materials that appear within the period - commonly about a year from redelivery, though the length is negotiated and is not a rule;
  • the remedy is defined: usually the yard's option either to repair at its own yard, or to bear the reasonable cost of repair elsewhere on a quotation approved beforehand - because a ship that fails in another continent cannot practicably return;
  • notice of the defect within a stated period after discovery, and access for the yard to inspect;
  • exclusions, which are what the argument is always about: fair wear and tear, the owner's misuse, neglect or failure to maintain, owner-supplied materials and equipment, and - almost invariably - all consequential loss, including loss of time, hire and profit. So the yard replaces the failed component and the owner bears the off-hire, and an owner who has not understood that has misjudged his exposure;
  • supported commercially by the retention from limb A and, on larger contracts, a bank or parent company guarantee.

Insurance must be arranged on both sides, and the clause should say who carries what and require evidence:

CoverWhoseWhat it must address
Hull and machineryOwnerCover continues during the docking, but underwriters must be notified of the port risk, the immobilisation, hot work and any layup - a docking is not the risk the policy was written for
Ship repairers' liabilityYardDamage to the vessel while in the yard's care, custody and control - the yard's principal exposure. Agree a minimum limit adequate to the value of the vessel and require the certificate before docking
Third party and employer's liabilityYardInjury to yard workers and damage to other property; port and dock risks
P&IOwnerCrew remaining on board, and pollution from the vessel. Notify the club of the docking
Cargo or bunker exposuresOwnerWhere any remain on board, and the tank condition required for hot work
  • Include a waiver of subrogation between the two sides' insurers, or a knock-for-knock allocation, so that the yard's insurer does not sue the owner's and vice versa - circular claims consume the premium and delay the settlement.
  • Illustration. A fire starts during hot work in a pump room. Whether the owner's H&M or the yard's ship repairers' liability insurer ultimately pays turns on the care, custody and control wording, on the hot-work permit regime and on whether the parties waived subrogation. Fire during hot work is the classic repair-yard loss and it is worth naming as the illustration of why this limb exists.

6. E. Termination events

Termination rights should be defined, mutual and consequential - that is, the contract must say not only when each party may terminate but what then happens to the ship, the money and the materials.

Terminating partyTypical events
Owner may terminateThe yard's insolvency, receivership, administration or winding-up · delay beyond the liquidated damages cap or beyond a long-stop date · abandonment of the work, or persistent failure to proceed with due diligence · material breach not remedied after written notice · a force majeure event continuing beyond the stated period
Yard may terminateThe owner's failure to pay a sum due after notice · the owner's insolvency · failure to deliver the vessel by the agreed date, or removal of her from the yard · the same prolonged force majeure

The consequences are where the value lies, and a clause that stops at "either party may terminate" is nearly useless:

  • Valuation and payment for work actually done to the date of termination, and for materials already ordered or fabricated to the ship's specification.
  • Release of the vessel and security for the lien - on what terms she may leave, and what security the owner must provide for a disputed balance. This is the practical crux and it should be agreed before docking.
  • Condition on redelivery. A part-completed docking must be left in a seaworthy and towable state, with openings closed and systems reinstated so far as practicable - otherwise the owner terminates and finds he cannot move her.
  • Return of owner-supplied materials and equipment, and of drawings and technical information.
  • Repayment of advances, called under the refund guarantee where the yard has failed.
  • Removal and tow - who arranges and who pays.
  • Survival of the confidentiality, guarantee, insurance and dispute clauses, so that terminating the contract does not destroy the machinery for resolving the dispute that caused it.

7. The two clauses the question does not name, but which decide all five

  1. Governing law and dispute resolution. The yard and the owner are usually in different countries, and the ship in a third. Every right in limbs A to E is worth exactly what it costs to enforce, so a clear governing law and a workable arbitration provision - seat, rules and language - is the clause that gives the others value. Consider also whether an expert determination or a short-form procedure is available for the sort of small, urgent technical dispute that actually arises mid-docking, when arbitration is far too slow to help.
  2. Order of precedence. The specification, the general conditions, the quotation and the clarifications will conflict. Say which prevails. Without it the parties argue about which document governs before they can argue about the point in dispute.

Finally, note that widely used industry standard forms exist for ship repair, and their commercial value is precisely that these five issues are already allocated in a way both sides recognise. Starting from a known form and negotiating the schedule - rates, period, liquidated damages, guarantee and limits - is faster, cheaper and far safer than drafting each docking from scratch.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Five printed limbs, sixteen marks, no split anywhere, and the command verb is "illustrate". Two consequences follow and both are about discipline rather than knowledge. First, five does not divide into sixteen, so nothing can be written at length - roughly three marks a limb, and the candidate who writes a page on payment terms will not finish. Second, "illustrate" is not "list": each limb needs something shown working - a worked consequence, a scenario, a named failure mode - or it has not been illustrated.

The route therefore gives one step per printed limb, opens each with the commercial risk it allocates, and hangs a worked illustration inside four of the five. Step 1 supplies the organising idea in three lines, and step 7 adds the two clauses the examiner did not name - which is the cheapest way to show that the subject is understood as a whole rather than as five headings.

The idea that makes all five one subject

Every one of the five printed issues is an allocation of the same four risks - cost, scope, time and defect - and every clause answers one question: who carries this if it goes wrong? Payment allocates cost. Specification allocates scope. Period and liquidated damages allocate time. Guarantees and insurance allocate defect and damage. Termination decides what happens when the allocation fails altogether.

State that in your opening and the answer stops being five disconnected headings.

The two commercial facts that colour everything

First: the yard has the ship. Once she is docked the owner's leverage is gone and the yard's is at its maximum. Everything commercially important must be agreed before she arrives - which is why the exit terms, the rates for unknown quantities and the security for a disputed balance are all pre-docking negotiations, not mid-docking ones.

Second: the owner's exposure is time, not price. A ship in dock earns nothing, so a dispute that adds a week can cost more than the item in dispute. That is why the variation and delay machinery matters more than the headline figure, and it is the sentence that shows an examiner you have thought about this from the owner's side.

The single most valuable practical point on the question

A variation order must capture the TIME as well as the MONEY. If it records only the price, the owner has bought the extra work and inadvertently surrendered his delay remedy - the yard will say the additional work extended the docking, and liquidated damages are disapplied for that period.

This is the classic failure in real dockings, it links limb B directly to limb C, and it is exactly the sort of thing a Chief Engineer knows and a lawyer does not. Say it, and illustrate it with an open-and-inspect item such as a stern tube.

Force majeure is a creature of contract

The point most candidates miss entirely. In common-law systems there is no general doctrine of force majeure. If the clause is not drafted, the party has only the doctrine of frustration, which is narrow and rarely available. So force majeure is not a background legal protection - it is a negotiated list, with defined events, a notice obligation, a duty to mitigate, and a stated effect.

And note its effect precisely: an extension of time, not money. Each side generally bears its own costs. A candidate who says force majeure entitles the owner to compensation has it backwards.

Liquidated damages: the cap and the exclusivity matter more than the rate

Three features are usually more important than the daily figure. It is normally the owner's sole and exclusive remedy for delay, so he cannot claim his actual lost earnings instead. It is capped, so the yard's worst case is known. And reaching the cap should trigger a right to terminate - which is what prevents an indefinite delay from becoming merely an expensive one.

Add the jurisdiction-dependent legal caution: the sum should be a genuine pre-estimate of loss, or it may be attacked as a penalty and be unenforceable, leaving the owner to prove actual loss.

The exclusion that surprises owners

A repair guarantee almost always excludes consequential loss, including loss of time, hire and profit. So when a guarantee defect stops the ship, the yard replaces the component and the owner bears the off-hire. Knowing and stating that is worth a mark on its own, because it shows you understand what the guarantee is actually worth rather than merely that one exists.

Common mistakes

  • Writing at length on one or two limbs and running out of time. Five limbs, sixteen marks - budget first.
  • Listing headings without illustrating anything, when the printed command verb is "illustrate".
  • Omitting the lien from the payment limb. It is the commercial fact that dominates the whole contract.
  • Treating the specification as a technical document rather than as the contract's definition of the work and the place cost is controlled.
  • Agreeing a variation on price alone and losing the delay remedy.
  • Saying force majeure is a general legal principle. In common-law systems it exists only if drafted.
  • Saying force majeure entitles a party to money. It gives an extension of time.
  • Giving liquidated damages figures or caps as percentages. They are negotiated and vary widely - quote none.
  • Forgetting that liquidated damages are usually exclusive and capped, and that the cap should trigger termination.
  • Omitting the consequential loss exclusion from the guarantee limb.
  • Forgetting ship repairers' liability insurance, which is the yard's central cover and the one to demand evidence of.
  • Writing termination as "either party may terminate" with no consequences - the consequences are the substance.
  • Omitting governing law and arbitration when the parties are in different countries.

Examiner traps

  • "How do you price work whose quantity is unknown?" By an agreed schedule of rates and provisional sums, fixed before the tender is accepted - not negotiated with the tank open.
  • "The yard will not let the ship sail. What is your position?" He is asserting a repairer's possessory lien. Offer security - a bank guarantee or a club letter of undertaking - for the disputed sum, on terms agreed before docking.
  • "You order extra work. What have you just done to your delivery date?" Extended it, unless the variation order fixed the time impact.
  • "Is force majeure implied?" Not in common-law systems. If it is not drafted, it does not exist.
  • "What does force majeure give you?" Time, not money - and a right to terminate if it persists beyond the stated period.
  • "Why might your liquidated damages clause fail?" If it is a penalty rather than a genuine pre-estimate of loss.
  • "A guarantee defect puts the ship off hire for ten days. Who pays the hire?" The owner. Consequential loss is excluded.
  • "Whose insurance covers a fire started by the yard's hot work?" Turns on care, custody and control, on the ship repairers' liability wording, on the permit regime, and on whether subrogation was waived.
  • "You terminate mid-docking. What must the yard leave you?" A seaworthy and towable vessel, with openings closed and systems reinstated so far as practicable.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Name the contract documents and why an order of precedence clause is needed. Agreement, general conditions, specification, quotation, clarifications, variation orders - they will conflict.
  • How is a repair contract priced? Lump sum for specified work, schedule of rates for quantum-uncertain items, provisional sums for open-and-inspect.
  • What is retention for? To keep money behind the guarantee obligation until the guarantee period expires.
  • What must a variation order record? The work, the price, and the effect on the delivery date.
  • Who bears rework if class rejects the work? The yard, where its workmanship or material is at fault; a change of requirement is a variation.
  • What are permissible delays? Additional work ordered, owner default, late owner-supplied items, force majeure.
  • Name five force majeure events. Act of God; war or civil commotion; strikes; fire or flood; government or port authority action.
  • How long is a repair guarantee and what does it exclude? Commonly about a year, negotiated; excludes wear and tear, owner misuse, owner-supplied materials and all consequential loss.
  • What insurance must the yard carry? Ship repairers' liability for vessels in its care, custody and control, plus third-party and employer's liability.
  • Give three owner termination events. Yard insolvency; delay beyond the liquidated damages cap; abandonment or failure to proceed with due diligence.

Memory framework

The five limbs map onto four risks: COST · SCOPE · TIME · DEFECT, and then EXIT. Payment is cost, specification is scope, period and liquidated damages are time, guarantees and insurance are defect, termination is exit.

Inside each limb one hook. Payment - THE YARD HAS THE SHIP. Specification - EVERY GAP IS AN EXTRA. Time - THE VARIATION MUST BUY THE TIME TOO. Guarantees - THEY FIX THE PART, YOU PAY THE OFF-HIRE. Termination - SEAWORTHY AND TOWABLE.

And close with the two unnamed clauses: WHICH LAW, AND WHICH DOCUMENT WINS.

Regulation and source map

  • There is no regulation on this question. A ship repair contract is a commercial contract and is governed by the law the parties choose, not by any IMO instrument or national maritime statute
  • Contract documents - agreement, general conditions, specification, quotation, clarifications, variation orders, with an order of precedence clause
  • Pricing - lump sum, schedule of rates, provisional sums, stage payments, retention
  • Security - owner's payment security; yard's refund or performance guarantee; the repairer's possessory lien
  • Delay - contract period, grace period, liquidated damages, cap, exclusivity, permissible delays, long-stop date
  • Force majeure - defined events, notice, mitigation, extension of time, termination on prolonged event
  • Guarantee - defects liability period, remedy and its location, notice of defect, exclusions including consequential loss
  • Insurance - owner's H&M and P&I; yard's ship repairers' liability, third party and employer's liability; waiver of subrogation
  • Termination - events for each party; valuation, release and security, seaworthy and towable condition, return of materials, repayment of advances, survival clauses
  • Governing law and arbitration; and the commercial value of a recognised industry standard form

Uncertainty and the evidential position on this question

This question is stated plainly to be the weakest-sourced on the paper alongside the marine insurance question, and for the same reason. The True Source corpus holds no ship repair contract standard form, no industry form of any kind, no shipbuilding or repair commentary and no contract law material, and no solved MIW question anywhere addresses this subject. This question therefore carries no primary source and none is claimed. Every proposition is industry guidance or commercial and engineering judgement.

Three disciplines follow, and they are what make the answer safe. No standard form is quoted and no clause of any form is reproduced; the existence of recognised industry forms is noted by identity only, to make a point about negotiation cost. No figure is asserted anywhere - no liquidated damages rate, no cap percentage, no retention percentage, no grace period, no guarantee period in months, no insurance limit - because every one of them is negotiated and varies by yard, region, vessel and market; where a common position is described, such as a guarantee of about a year, it is expressly stated as typical and negotiable. And no proposition of law is asserted for any named jurisdiction: the two legal points that do appear - that liquidated damages must be a genuine pre-estimate rather than a penalty, and that force majeure is a creature of contract - are both flagged as jurisdiction-dependent, and the repairer's lien is described as a commercial reality rather than as a rule of any stated law.

That is a deliberate choice rather than a gap. What the examiner asks for - the key issues, illustrated - is entirely answerable at the level of allocation and consequence, and an answer that quoted figures or asserted the law of an unnamed jurisdiction would be less reliable, not more.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing What a repair contract is, and the one idea behind all five limbs
  2. __________A A. Payment terms
  3. __________B B. Ambit of the specification and additional work
  4. __________C C. Contract period, liquidated damages and force majeure
  5. __________D D. Guarantees and insurance
  6. __________E E. Termination events
  7. __________closing The two clauses the question does not name, but which decide all five
Flashcards 12

1 What the contract is and the one idea - five allocations of cost, scope, time and defect. 2 A payment terms. 3 B specification and additional work. 4 C period, liquidated damages and force majeure. 5 D guarantees and insurance. 6 E termination. 7 The two unnamed clauses - governing law and arbitration, and order of precedence.

Five limbs into sixteen marks with no split; the route fixes the budget and step 7 shows the subject is understood as a whole.

Each is an allocation of the same four risks - cost, scope, time and defect - and every clause answers one question: who carries this if it goes wrong? Payment allocates cost, the specification allocates scope, period and liquidated damages allocate time, guarantees and insurance allocate defect, and termination decides what happens when the allocation fails altogether.

Stating it in the opening turns five disconnected headings into one argument.

Because the yard has the ship. Once she is docked the owner's leverage is largely gone and the yard's is at its maximum, and a repairer in possession will in practice assert a right to retain her until his account is settled. So the rates for unknown quantities, the exit terms and the security that may be offered instead of cash for a disputed sum must all be settled in advance.

It is the commercial fact that dominates the whole contract and the payment limb in particular.

By an agreed schedule of rates - steel renewal by weight, pipework by length, tank cleaning and coating by area, staging, gas-freeing and day rates by trade - together with provisional sums for open-and-inspect items, all fixed before the tender is accepted. The alternative is negotiating the rate on the day with the tank open and the ship immobilised.

It is where an owner actually keeps control of cost on a docking.

The work, the price, and the effect on the delivery date. If it records only the price, the owner has bought the extra work and inadvertently surrendered his delay remedy - the yard will say the additional work extended the docking and the liquidated damages clause is disapplied for that period.

The classic real-world failure, and the point that links the specification limb to the delay limb.

A pre-agreed daily sum after a grace period; a cap expressed as a proportion of the contract price; exclusivity, so it is normally the owner's sole remedy for delay in place of actual lost earnings; and a right to terminate once the cap is reached. Permissible delays - extra work ordered, owner default, late owner-supplied items and force majeure - extend the date instead of triggering damages.

The cap and the exclusivity are usually more important commercially than the daily rate.

Because the sum stipulated must be a genuine pre-estimate of the loss the owner would suffer. A figure fixed to punish rather than to compensate may be attacked as a penalty and struck down, leaving the owner to prove his actual loss. This is jurisdiction-dependent.

It explains why the rate is tied to prevailing earnings rather than set arbitrarily high.

In common-law systems it is not implied. Force majeure is a creature of contract - if it is not drafted, it does not exist, and the party is left with the narrow doctrine of frustration. Where drafted, it gives an extension of time and not money, each side generally bearing its own costs, with a right for either party to terminate if the event persists beyond a stated period.

Both halves are commonly stated backwards, and both are worth a mark.

That the events be beyond the reasonable control of the affected party and not caused by its own default; prompt written notice within a stated time with particulars; a duty to mitigate and to resume as soon as possible; notice when the event ends; and a stated period after which either party may terminate. Notice provisions are conditions and are commonly missed in practice.

The procedure is where force majeure claims actually fail.

The owner. A repair guarantee almost always excludes consequential loss, including loss of time, hire and profit. The yard makes good the defect due to its defective workmanship or materials - at its own yard, or by bearing the reasonable cost of repair elsewhere on a pre-approved quotation - but the commercial loss stays with the owner.

It shows what the guarantee is actually worth rather than merely that one exists.

The owner's hull and machinery cover continuing, with underwriters notified of the port risk, immobilisation and hot work; the owner's P&I where crew remain on board. The yard must carry ship repairers' liability cover for damage to vessels in its care, custody and control, with an agreed minimum limit and a certificate produced before docking, plus third-party and employer's liability. A waiver of subrogation between the two sides' insurers avoids circular claims.

Ship repairers' liability is the yard's central cover and the one an owner should demand evidence of.

Valuation and payment for work actually done and materials ordered or fabricated to the ship's specification; release of the vessel and what security must be given for a disputed balance; a seaworthy and towable condition with openings closed and systems reinstated so far as practicable; return of owner-supplied materials, drawings and technical information; repayment of advances under the refund guarantee; who arranges and pays for removal and tow; and survival of the confidentiality, guarantee, insurance and dispute clauses.

A clause that stops at 'either party may terminate' is nearly useless - the consequences are the substance.

Quick revision
15-second recall
FRAME. Defined work, defined ship, defined time, defined price. Documents: agreement, general conditions, specification, quotation, clarifications, variation orders, with an order of precedence clause. The five limbs are five allocations of four risks - COST, SCOPE, TIME, DEFECT - plus EXIT. Two facts: the yard has the ship (agree everything before docking), and the owner's exposure is time, not price. Command verb is illustrate. A. PAYMENT. Lump sum for specified work; schedule of rates for quantum-uncertain (steel by weight, pipe by length, coating by area, staging, gas-freeing, day rates); provisional sums for open-and-inspect. Stage payments on docking / undocking / redelivery; retention until guarantee expires. Invoices on measured quantities and signed variation orders; yards usually exclude set-off. Currency, escalation, taxes, dock dues. Security both ways - owner's payment security, yard's refund/performance guarantee. THE LIEN: a repairer in possession will retain the ship - so fix what is payable on redelivery and what security may be given instead of cash, before docking. B. SPECIFICATION. It is the contract's definition of the work and where cost is controlled - every ambiguity is resolved on the day, in the yard's favour. Close gaps: workmanship standard, class rules, staging, gas-freeing, cleaning, protection, reinstatement, access work, who supplies what. Open-and-inspect by rate, with a defined inspection standard. VARIATIONS: in writing, by a named representative, BEFORE the work - recording the work, THE PRICE AND THE TIME EFFECT. Record price alone and you have surrendered the delay remedy. Owner's subcontractors: access, hours, facility charge, safety, responsibility. Rework at the yard's cost where its workmanship is at fault. C. TIME. Period from arrival/docking to redelivery; fix the owner's delivery date too. Liquidated damages: per day, after a grace period, capped, normally the sole and exclusive remedy, with a right to terminate at the cap. Permissible delays: extra work, owner default, late owner items, force majeure. Must be a genuine pre-estimate of loss or attacked as a penalty (jurisdiction-dependent). FORCE MAJEURE IS A CREATURE OF CONTRACT - no general doctrine; without it, only frustration. Defined events; notice, mitigation; effect is time, not money; termination if prolonged. D. GUARANTEE AND INSURANCE. Defects due to defective workmanship or materials within the period from redelivery (commonly about a year, negotiated); remedy at the yard or the reasonable cost elsewhere; notice and access; excludes wear and tear, owner misuse, owner-supplied materials and ALL CONSEQUENTIAL LOSS - they fix the part, you pay the off-hire. Insurance: owner's H&M notified of port risk, immobilisation and hot work; yard's ship repairers' liability for the vessel in its care, custody and control with an agreed limit and certificate before docking; yard's third-party and employer's liability; P&I for crew aboard; waiver of subrogation. Classic loss: fire during hot work. E. TERMINATION. Owner: yard insolvency, delay beyond the cap or long-stop, abandonment or lack of due diligence, unremedied material breach, prolonged force majeure. Yard: non-payment after notice, owner insolvency, failure to deliver the vessel, prolonged force majeure. Consequences: valuation and payment for work done and materials, release of the vessel and security for the lien, seaworthy and towable condition, return of owner materials and drawings, repayment of advances under the refund guarantee, removal and tow, survival of dispute clauses. CLOSE: governing law and arbitration, and order of precedence.
Keywords

Critical numbers
  • NO figure is asserted anywhere on this question - no liquidated damages rate, no cap percentage, no retention percentage, no grace period, no guarantee period in months, no insurance limit, no interest rate. All are negotiated and vary by yard, region, vessel and market
  • A guarantee period of about a year from redelivery is described as TYPICAL AND NEGOTIABLE, not as a rule
  • The five printed limbs do not divide evenly into sixteen marks; the paper does not divide them and no split is invented
Critical regulation
There is no regulation on this question, and saying so is itself a point. A ship repair contract is a commercial contract governed by the law the parties choose; no IMO instrument and no national maritime statute determines its content. Two general propositions apply and both are jurisdiction-dependent: a stipulated sum for delay is enforceable as liquidated damages where it is a genuine pre-estimate of the loss and may be unenforceable as a penalty where it is not; and in common-law systems force majeure is a creature of contract, with only the narrow doctrine of frustration behind it if no clause is drafted.
Major trap
Budgeting, first of all: five printed limbs against sixteen marks with no split, so nothing can be written at length and nothing may be omitted - and the command verb is illustrate, so a bare list of headings has not answered the question. The substantive traps are: omitting the repairer's lien from the payment limb, when it is the commercial fact that dominates the whole contract; agreeing a variation on price alone and thereby surrendering the delay remedy, which is the classic real-world failure and the point that links limbs B and C; asserting that force majeure is a general legal principle when in common-law systems it exists only if drafted, and that it gives money when it gives an extension of time; forgetting that the guarantee excludes consequential loss, so the yard replaces the part and the owner bears the off-hire; and writing termination as a bare right with no consequences, when the consequences - valuation, release of the ship against security, seaworthy and towable condition, return of materials - are the substance of the limb.

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

This question hands you its own structure, which is why it is easier to answer adequately than to answer well. The four steps are printed in the stem, so describing them earns only part of the marks; the printed command asks for the critical role of each, with examples. So the real task is to show that the four steps form a closed loop in which each stage is load-bearing - plan without monitoring and you cannot prove a saving; monitor without evaluating and the loop never closes; implement without naming who does what by when and the plan becomes a document carried for inspection rather than a way the ship is run. Two framing facts separate a current answer from a stale one. The four steps are the framework of one part of the plan and not of the whole thing, because the plan also carries a data-collection part and a carbon-intensity part that apply to bigger ships. And the requirement moved recently to a different regulation number, so a great deal of circulating material cites the old one. The timing also gives the cycle unusual force just now: the carbon-intensity rules took effect at the start of this year, so every ship is in the middle of the first period of data collection, with no ratings issued to anybody yet - which means the planning and implementing that is being done right now is what will determine the first result anyone sees.

Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

framing
  1. What the SEEMP is, and where the four steps live
main
  1. Step 1 - Planning
  2. Step 2 - Implementation
  3. Step 3 - Monitoring
  4. Step 4 - Self-evaluation and improvement
  5. Why the cycle is critical, not merely administrative

Memory cue. PIMS - Plan, Implement, Monitor, Self-evaluate. It is Plan-Do-Check-Act in IMO clothes. Inside planning: SHIP, COMPANY, PEOPLE, GOAL (2). Implementation is WHO, WHAT, BY WHEN (3). Monitoring is QUANTITATIVE, CONTINUOUS, SHORE-SIDE (4). Self-evaluation asks did it work, why, what trend, what next (5). Then close with BREAK ONE LINK AND THE LOOP FAILS (6).

Remember6 route headings
Cover50 core points beneath them

Memorise the 6 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 50 separate facts to learn individually.

Model written answer

1. What the SEEMP is, and where the four steps live

The Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan (SEEMP) is required by regulation 26 of MARPOL Annex VI. It is a ship-specific, operational management document kept on board, developed and reviewed taking into account the guidelines adopted by the Organization. The guidelines operative at this examination are the 2022 Guidelines, resolution MEPC.346(78), adopted on 10 June 2022, which revoked the 2016 Guidelines, MEPC.282(70).

Note the numbering, because it is where most marks are quietly lost. The SEEMP sits at regulation 26, not regulation 22: chapter 4 of Annex VI was renumbered by MEPC.328(76), which entered into force on 1 November 2022 - seven months before this examination. Within chapter 4, the goal is stated at regulation 20, the fuel oil data collection system at regulation 27, and the operational carbon intensity indicator at regulation 28.

The plan has three Parts, and it matters for this question that the four steps belong to Part I:

  • Part I - Ship Management Plan to Improve Energy Efficiency, required by regulation 26.1, applying to any ship of 400 GT and above. This is the part built on the four-step cycle.
  • Part II - Ship Fuel Oil Consumption Data Collection Plan, required by regulation 26.2, applying to any ship of 5,000 GT and above. It describes the methodology for collecting the data required by regulation 27 and reporting it to the Administration.
  • Part III - Ship Operational Carbon Intensity Plan, required by regulations 26.3 and 28.8 for ships of 5,000 GT and above in the listed categories. It carries the attained annual operational CII methodology, the required CII for the next three years, a three-year implementation plan, a procedure for self-evaluation, and - for a ship rated D for three consecutive years or E in a single year - a plan of corrective actions.

Section 3.6 states the cycle directly: Part I should be developed as a ship-specific plan by the company and should reflect efforts to improve energy efficiency and reduce carbon intensity through four steps - planning, implementation, monitoring, and self-evaluation and improvement - and "these components play a critical role in the continuous cycle". It adds that with each iteration some elements of Part I will necessarily change while others may remain. Section 3.7 carries the standing qualification that safety considerations are paramount at all times, and that the trade a ship is engaged in may determine which measures are feasible.

Two further permissions are worth a line each, because they show the plan is meant to be absorbed rather than added: a company already running an ISO 14001 environmental management system should treat efficiency monitoring within it (3.3), and Part I may form part of the ship's safety management system (3.4).

2. Step 1 - Planning

The Guidelines are unusually emphatic here. Section 4.1.1: planning is the most crucial stage of Part I, because it primarily determines both the ship's current status of energy usage and carbon intensity and the expected improvement - so sufficient time should be devoted to it. Planning has four components:

  1. Ship-specific measures. Establish the ship's present status; identify measures already undertaken and how effective they have actually been; identify what further can be adopted. The Guidelines warn (4.1.3) that not all measures can be applied to all ships, or even to the same ship under different operating conditions, and that SOME OF THEM ARE MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE - and add the practical point that initial savings can be reinvested in more difficult or expensive upgrades.
  2. Company-specific measures. Efficiency does not depend on single-ship management alone (4.1.5): repair yards, owners, operators, charterers, cargo owners, fuel suppliers, ports and traffic management services all bear on it. The Guidelines' own example is "just in time" arrival, which requires good early communication among operators, ports and traffic management services. A company-level plan and coordination is therefore recommended.
  3. Human resource development. Awareness-raising and training for personnel ashore and on board (4.1.6) - which the Guidelines call both a planning component and a critical element of implementation.
  4. Goal setting (4.1.7). For a ship subject to regulation 28, the goal should be consistent with the continuous CII improvement that regulation requires. For a ship or company not subject to regulation 28 the Guidelines say expressly that there is no requirement to define a goal, to communicate it publicly, or to be subject to external inspection, survey or audit in respect of the SEEMP - though a meaningful goal should still be set as a signal of commitment, using indicators such as annual fuel consumption, AER, cgDIST, EEOI or another CII. In all cases the goal should be measurable and easy to understand.

Examples. Ship-specific: speed optimisation; weather routeing; hull and propeller condition management with a coating and polishing regime; trim optimisation; and confirming berth availability and arrival time with the destination port, so the ship is not steaming hard in order to wait at anchor. Company-specific: negotiating just-in-time arrival with terminals, which cannot be delivered from the bridge and needs the operator, the port and traffic management talking early. Mutually exclusive: a fixed-pitch propeller re-pitched for a lower service speed forecloses later gains from increasing speed, and slow steaming interacts with waste-heat recovery output. Goal: "reduce AER by a stated percentage year on year, consistent with the required CII" - measurable, and tied to the regulation.

3. Step 2 - Implementation

Section 4.2.1: once measures are identified it is essential to establish a system for implementing them. That means developing procedures for energy management, defining the tasks associated with them and assigning those tasks to responsible personnel, with defined levels of authority and lines of communication, and procedures for internal audit and management review where relevant. Part I should state how each measure is to be implemented, who is responsible, and the start and end dates of each. The Guidelines note that building this system can be treated as part of planning and completed there.

Section 4.2.2 adds record-keeping, and two duties that are easy marks because they are counter-intuitive:

  • if an identified measure cannot be implemented for any reason, the reason should be recorded for internal use - a plan that quietly drops a measure has destroyed the evidence its own next self-evaluation needs;
  • events and operational conditions outside the control of the ship's crew - the Guidelines instance waiting for berths, extended port dwell times and operation in severe adverse weather - should be documented, because they may affect the ship's rating and cannot be reconstructed afterwards.

Examples. The Chief Engineer is named as responsible for the hull and propeller condition regime, with inspection at each dry-docking and in-water polishing at defined fouling thresholds; the Master for trim and speed compliance against the passage plan; the shore superintendent for retrofit scheduling. A shaft generator retrofit deferred because the yard slot moved is recorded with the reason, so that the next evaluation does not read it as a failure of the measure itself.

4. Step 3 - Monitoring

Section 4.3.1: energy efficiency should be monitored quantitatively, by an established method and preferably by an international standard. The monitoring tool should target the goal indicator set at planning - AER, cgDIST, EEOI or another CII - and a ship subject to regulation 28 is likely to use the CII itself, calculated in accordance with the guidelines developed by the Organization.

  • 4.3.4 - monitor at regular intervals, for consistency of data and to assist verification; fuel oil consumption monitored using daily reporting, such as noon reports, or at higher frequency.
  • 4.3.5 - continuous, consistent and reliable data collection is the foundation of monitoring, so a monitoring system with data-collection procedures and assigned personnel must be developed - and, again, completed at the planning stage.
  • 4.3.6 - to avoid unnecessary administrative burden on ships' staff, monitoring should be carried out as much as possible by shore staff where data can be transferred automatically, using existing required records such as the official and engineering logbooks and the oil record books.
  • 4.3.7 - where a ship diverts to engage in search and rescue, the data obtained during those operations should not be used in energy efficiency monitoring and should be recorded separately.

Examples. Noon reports feeding a performance system that separates hull-fouling degradation from weather effect; a shaft power meter and mass flow meters giving consumption independent of tank soundings; the attained CII tracked monthly against the required value, so that corrective action is taken in month seven rather than discovered in the annual return.

5. Step 4 - Self-evaluation and improvement

Section 4.4.1: this is the final phase of the management cycle, and its purpose is to produce meaningful feedback for the coming first stage - the planning stage of the next improvement cycle. Section 4.4.2 gives four purposes:

  1. evaluate the effectiveness of the planned measures and of their implementation;
  2. deepen the understanding of the overall characteristics of the ship's operation - what types of measure can or cannot function effectively, and how and why;
  3. comprehend the trend of the efficiency improvement of that ship; and
  4. develop the improved management plan for the next cycle by identifying further opportunities.

Note the second and third. The Guidelines do not ask only "did it work?" but "why did it work, and what is the trend" - and section 4.4.3 makes that explicit by requiring that time be invested in identifying the cause and effect of the performance during the evaluated period, so lessons learned are taken into account in revising the plan. That is what converts a year of data into a better plan rather than into a score.

Examples. Evaluation shows weather routeing delivered most of its claimed saving on ocean legs and almost none on short coastal legs, so the next cycle applies it selectively and reinvests the effort in trim optimisation. Hull coating performance is found to degrade faster on a trade with long idle periods in warm water, so the next cycle shortens the polishing interval rather than blaming the coating. And for a ship subject to regulation 28, Part III carries this further with a statutory consequence: rated D for three consecutive years or E in a single year, a plan of corrective actions must be developed and included in the SEEMP.

6. Why the cycle is critical, not merely administrative

The printed command asks for the critical role of each component, so the answer must show why removing any one of them breaks the whole:

StepWhat it contributesWhat happens without it
PlanningEstablishes the baseline and selects measures that suit this ship and this tradeMeasures are adopted by fashion rather than by fit, and no saving can be claimed because there is no baseline to claim it against
ImplementationConverts intention into assigned, dated, auditable actionThe plan becomes a document carried for inspection rather than a way the ship is run
MonitoringSupplies quantitative evidence, continuously and consistentlyEffectiveness becomes a matter of opinion; the return is unverifiable, and adverse weather cannot be distinguished from poor operation
Self-evaluationTurns the data into a better plan, and explains why measures workedThe cycle does not close - the same plan is re-issued each year and improvement stops

The cycle is also what keeps the plan ship-specific and living rather than generic: section 3.6 says that with each iteration some elements of Part I will necessarily change while others may remain. And it is what connects an operational management document to a regulatory outcome, because the same four beats reappear in Part III - planning becomes the required CII for the next three years and the three-year implementation plan, monitoring becomes the attained annual operational CII, and self-evaluation becomes the plan of corrective actions.

Two closing observations fix the answer to this examination. First, note what the SEEMP is not: it is operational, not technical. The EEDI and EEXI fix what the ship is, once; the SEEMP governs how she is operated, continuously - which is why it is a cycle and they are not. Second, the timing gives the cycle unusual force just now: the CII entered into force on 1 January 2023, so this is the first year in which data is being collected against it and no ship has yet been rated. Every ship is presently inside her first monitoring period, which is precisely the point at which the planning and implementation steps determine what the first rating will be. The Committee has also undertaken to keep these Guidelines under review, noting that under regulations 25.3 and 28.11 a review of the technical and operational measures shall be completed by 1 January 2026.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks, one limb, and the question names its own four sections. That is a gift and a trap. The gift is that the structure is handed to you. The trap is that four equal paragraphs of description will not score sixteen marks, because the printed command is "describe with suitable examples, the critical role of each of these components" - three separate demands. You must (i) describe each step, (ii) give examples, and (iii) say why each is critical. An answer that does only the first has answered roughly a third of the question.

The route therefore spends one step per component, attaches the examples inside each step, and then adds a dedicated criticality step - the table at step 6 - which is where the "critical role" marks are actually banked. The framing at step 1 is worth the space because it lets you say something most candidates cannot: that the four steps are the framework of Part I specifically, not of the whole plan.

Which guidelines - and why this is the whole currency test

The SEEMP Guidelines are re-adopted periodically and each edition revokes the last. At this examination the operative edition is MEPC.346(78), the 2022 Guidelines, adopted 10 June 2022 - a year old - which revoked MEPC.282(70), the 2016 Guidelines.

This is the single most valuable thing to get right, and it is also the easiest to get wrong from later material. If you are revising from a 2024 or 2025 paper you will find a different resolution number, and it is wrong here. The 2022 Guidelines are the operative edition for the whole of 2023 and beyond into 2024; anything adopted later belongs to a later paper. Equally, an answer citing the 2016 Guidelines is a year out of date at this sitting, and quite a lot of circulating material still does.

The regulation number is the other easy mark to lose

A great deal of older material - textbooks, circulars and notes written before 2023 - cites the SEEMP as MARPOL Annex VI regulation 22. That was correct until chapter 4 was renumbered by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. Since then the SEEMP is regulation 26, with Part I at 26.1, Part II at 26.2 and Part III at 26.3 read with 28.8.

At this sitting the renumbering is only about seven months old, so a candidate working from anything written before late 2022 will have the old number. Getting it right, and being able to say why the old number exists, is a visible sign of current reading. Carry the neighbours too: regulation 20 the goal of chapter 4, regulation 27 the data collection system, regulation 28 the CII.

Where the decarbonisation position actually stood in June 2023

If the answer reaches for context it must be the context of this sitting, and this is where the traps cluster:

  • EEXI and CII entered into force on 1 January 2023, so at this examination the CII is five months old, 2023 is the first data year and it is still running, and no ship has yet been rated. Do not describe rating experience, rating distributions or how the fleet performed - none exists yet.
  • The operative strategy is the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018. The 2023 Strategy was adopted at MEPC 80 on 7 July 2023 - after this examination. Citing it here is future contamination and is the commonest single error a candidate revising from a later paper will make.
  • MEPC 80 has not yet met, so nothing from it exists. The only forward-looking statement legitimately available is the one in the Guidelines themselves: operative paragraph 4 records that under regulations 25.3 and 28.11 a review of the technical and operational measures shall be completed by 1 January 2026. That is a requirement with a deadline, not a process with a result.
  • Do not import any mid-term measure as adopted. None was, at this sitting.

The five facts from section 4 that most answers miss

  • Planning is expressly the "most crucial stage" (4.1.1). Say so and attribute it - it converts a generic opening into a cited one.
  • Some measures are mutually exclusive (4.1.3). Almost nobody says this, and it demonstrates real engineering understanding rather than a list of green measures.
  • Reasons for non-implementation must be recorded (4.2.2). Counter-intuitive, and precise.
  • Conditions outside the crew's control should be documented (4.2.2) - waiting for berths, extended port dwell, severe adverse weather - because they may affect the rating.
  • Search and rescue data is excluded from monitoring and recorded separately (4.3.7). A small, exact, memorable point that proves the Guidelines were opened.

Common mistakes

  • Citing the SEEMP as regulation 22. It has been regulation 26 since 1 November 2022.
  • Citing a 2024 guidelines edition. At this sitting the operative edition is MEPC.346(78), the 2022 Guidelines - anything later does not exist yet.
  • Citing the 2016 Guidelines, MEPC.282(70). They were revoked by MEPC.346(78) in June 2022.
  • Citing the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy. It was adopted on 7 July 2023 - one month after this examination. The operative strategy here is the Initial Strategy of 2018.
  • Describing CII rating experience or a rating distribution. 2023 is the first data year, it is still running, and no ship has been rated.
  • Presenting the four steps as the structure of the whole SEEMP. They are the framework of Part I.
  • Describing the steps without giving examples, when the question prints "with suitable examples".
  • Describing the steps without saying why each is critical, when the question prints "the critical role of each".
  • Confusing the SEEMP with the EEDI or EEXI. Those are technical and one-off; the SEEMP is operational and continuous.
  • Writing that Part I applies to ships of 5,000 GT and above. That is Part II; Part I is 400 GT and above.
  • Treating monitoring as something done only for the annual return. The Guidelines require regular intervals and daily fuel reporting.

Examiner traps

  • "Whose plan is it?" Part I is developed as a ship-specific plan by the company (3.6) - not written on board, though implemented on board.
  • "Is the SEEMP audited?" The answer splits. For a ship not subject to regulation 28 the Guidelines say expressly there is no requirement to define a goal, publish it, or be subject to external inspection, survey or audit in respect of the SEEMP (4.1.7.2). For a ship subject to regulation 28 the position differs, because Part III is verified. Knowing that the answer splits is worth a mark.
  • "What happens if the ship rates badly?" D for three consecutive years, or E in one year, triggers a plan of corrective actions in the SEEMP. Note that at this sitting that is a requirement, not yet an experience.
  • "Why must adverse weather be recorded?" Because it may affect the rating and cannot be reconstructed afterwards - 4.2.2.
  • "Where does safety come in?" Section 3.7 - safety considerations are paramount at all times, and the trade may determine which measures are feasible.
  • "Can the SEEMP sit inside another system?" Yes - ISO 14001 (3.3), and Part I may form part of the safety management system (3.4).
  • "Which GHG strategy applies?" The Initial Strategy of 2018. The 2023 Strategy is one month future at this sitting.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which regulation requires the SEEMP, and which guidelines are current? Regulation 26 of MARPOL Annex VI; the 2022 Guidelines, MEPC.346(78).
  • What did those guidelines revoke? The 2016 Guidelines, MEPC.282(70).
  • What are the three Parts and their thresholds? Part I 400 GT and above; Part II 5,000 GT and above; Part III for ships of 5,000 GT and above in the listed categories, under regulations 26.3 and 28.8.
  • Name the four steps. Planning, implementation, monitoring, self-evaluation and improvement.
  • Which step do the Guidelines call the most crucial, and why? Planning - it determines the current status and the achievable improvement.
  • Give two ship-specific and one company-specific measure. Speed optimisation and hull condition management; just-in-time arrival negotiated with the terminal.
  • What must be recorded when a measure is not implemented? The reason, for internal use.
  • What data is excluded from energy efficiency monitoring? Data from search and rescue diversions, recorded separately.
  • How does Part III mirror the cycle? Required CII and three-year implementation plan; attained annual CII; plan of corrective actions.
  • When must the review of the technical and operational measures be completed? By 1 January 2026, under regulations 25.3 and 28.11.

Memory framework

PIMS - Plan, Implement, Monitor, Self-evaluate. It is a Plan-Do-Check-Act cycle wearing IMO clothes, which is exactly why section 3.3 says a company already running ISO 14001 should treat efficiency monitoring as part of its existing management system, and section 3.4 allows Part I to form part of the safety management system.

For the four sub-parts of planning: SHIP, COMPANY, PEOPLE, GOAL - ship-specific measures, company-specific measures, human resource development, goal setting. For implementation: WHO, WHAT, BY WHEN. For monitoring: QUANTITATIVE, CONTINUOUS, SHORE-SIDE. For the four purposes of self-evaluation: did it work, why, what is the trend, what next.

Regulation and source map

  • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 - the SEEMP. 26.1 Part I, 26.2 Part II, 26.3 Part III
  • Regulation 20 goal of chapter 4; regulation 27 data collection system; regulation 28 operational CII, with 28.8 for Part III; regulations 25.3 and 28.11 the review to be completed by 1 January 2026
  • MEPC.328(76) - the 2021 revised Annex VI; renumbered the SEEMP from regulation 22 to 26, in force 1 November 2022
  • MEPC.346(78) - 2022 SEEMP Guidelines, adopted 10 June 2022; revokes MEPC.282(70). The operative edition at this sitting
  • MEPC.346(78) section 3.6 - the four steps and the continuous cycle; 3.7 safety paramount; 3.3 ISO 14001; 3.4 may form part of the SMS
  • Section 4.1 planning (4.1.1 most crucial stage; 4.1.3 mutually exclusive measures; 4.1.7 goal setting); 4.2 implementation (4.2.1 the system; 4.2.2 record-keeping); 4.3 monitoring (4.3.4 regular intervals and noon reports; 4.3.5 the monitoring system; 4.3.6 shore staff; 4.3.7 search and rescue); 4.4 self-evaluation (4.4.2 the four purposes; 4.4.3 cause and effect)
  • Sections 9 to 15 - Part III, including self-evaluation and the plan of corrective actions
  • MEPC.352(78) CII Guidelines G1; MEPC.355(78) correction factors and voyage adjustments G5
  • Initial IMO GHG Strategy, 2018 - the operative strategy at this sitting

Uncertainty and currency

The four steps, their content and every section number in this answer are taken from MEPC.346(78) read directly. Three limitations are recorded. MIW holds no quotation-ready consolidated MARPOL Annex VI - official-source PDFs of the consolidated 2022 edition and of Annex VI (5th ed. 2023) are held but are image-only with no text layer - so the numbering and content of regulations 20, 25, 26, 27 and 28 are established from the recitals and cross-references inside MEPC.346(78) rather than from a licensed Annex text, and no regulation is quoted verbatim. No CII rating, distribution or fleet statistic is asserted, and none could be - 2023 is the first data year and it is still running at this sitting. And the Initial GHG Strategy of 2018 is cited by identity and by its levels of ambition; MIW holds no text of it and it is not quoted.

One currency instruction for any later editor, and it is the most important on this paper. MEPC.346(78) is the correct edition here and must not be advanced. This question recurs verbatim at later sittings where a different guidelines edition, a different GHG strategy and real rating experience all apply. Every one of those is future at June 2023. Do not update the resolution number, do not substitute the 2023 GHG Strategy for the Initial Strategy, and do not add rating experience. The one thing that does not need reversing is the regulation 26 numbering, which had already taken effect seven months before this examination.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.
