Flashcards 12

1 What the SEEMP is and where the four steps live. 2 Planning. 3 Implementation. 4 Monitoring. 5 Self-evaluation and improvement. 6 Why the cycle is critical rather than administrative.

Step 6 is where the 'critical role' marks are banked; steps 2 to 5 carry the examples inside them.

MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26. The operative guidelines are the 2022 Guidelines, resolution MEPC.346(78), adopted 10 June 2022, which revoke the 2016 Guidelines, MEPC.282(70).

Two citation traps in one card - the old regulation 22 numbering, and a later guidelines edition carried back from a 2024 or 2025 paper.

Because that was the numbering before MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 was renumbered by resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. Since then the SEEMP is regulation 26, with Part I at 26.1, Part II at 26.2 and Part III at 26.3 read with 28.8.

The renumbering is only about seven months old at this sitting, so most circulating material still carries the old number.

Part I, Ship Management Plan to Improve Energy Efficiency - any ship of 400 GT and above. Part II, Ship Fuel Oil Consumption Data Collection Plan - any ship of 5,000 GT and above. Part III, Ship Operational Carbon Intensity Plan - required by regulations 26.3 and 28.8 for ships of 5,000 GT and above in the listed categories.

The four steps belong to Part I only, and the two tonnage thresholds are commonly transposed.

Section 4.1.1 calls it the most crucial stage, because it primarily determines both the current status of energy usage and carbon intensity and the expected improvement. It has four components: ship-specific measures, company-specific measures, human resource development, and goal setting - the goal to be measurable and easy to understand.

Attributing 'most crucial stage' to the Guidelines converts a generic opening into a cited one.

That not all measures can be applied to all ships, or even to the same ship under different operating conditions, and that some of them are mutually exclusive. It adds that initial savings can be reinvested in more difficult or expensive upgrades.

Almost no candidate says this, and it demonstrates engineering judgement rather than a list of green measures.

Procedures for energy management; defined tasks assigned to responsible personnel; defined levels of authority and lines of communication; procedures for internal audit and management review where relevant; and, for each measure, how it is to be implemented, who is responsible and the start and end dates. The system may be developed at the planning stage.

'Who, what, by when' is the whole of step 2 and is what makes the plan auditable.

First, if an identified measure cannot be implemented for any reason, the reason should be recorded for internal use. Second, events and operational conditions outside the control of the ship's crew - waiting for berths, extended port dwell times, operation in severe adverse weather - should be documented, because they may affect the ship's rating.

Both are counter-intuitive and precise, and the second links implementation to the CII outcome.

Quantitative monitoring by an established method, preferably an international standard, using a tool targeting the goal indicator - AER, cgDIST, EEOI or another CII; at regular intervals, with fuel oil consumption monitored by daily reporting such as noon reports; supported by a monitoring system established at the planning stage and carried out as far as possible by shore staff using existing records. Data obtained during a search and rescue diversion is excluded and recorded separately.

The search and rescue exclusion is small, exact and memorable, and proves the Guidelines were opened.

To evaluate the effectiveness of the planned measures and their implementation; to deepen the understanding of the ship's operation - what works, how and why; to comprehend the trend of efficiency improvement; and to develop the improved management plan for the next cycle. Section 4.4.3 adds that time should be invested in identifying cause and effect during the evaluated period.

The middle two are what convert data into a better plan rather than a score.

EEDI and EEXI are technical indices fixing what the ship is - design efficiency, assessed once. The SEEMP is an operational management plan governing how the ship is run, continuously, through a repeating four-step cycle.

It explains why the SEEMP is a cycle and the indices are not.

EEXI and CII entered into force on 1 January 2023, so this is the first CII data year, it is still running, and no ship has yet been rated. The operative strategy is the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018 - the 2023 Strategy was adopted on 7 July 2023, one month after this examination, and must not be cited. The only forward-looking statement available is that a review of the technical and operational measures is to be completed by 1 January 2026 under regulations 25.3 and 28.11.

This identical question recurs at later sittings with different answers to every one of these, and material revised from a later paper carries all of them backwards.

Guidelines at this sitting: MEPC.346(78), the 2022 Guidelines, adopted 10 June 2022, REVOKING MEPC.282(70) (2016). Chapter 4: reg 20 goal, reg 27 data collection, reg 28 CII; regs 25.3 and 28.11 - review to be completed by 1 January 2026. Three Parts: Part I (reg 26.1, 400 GT+) - the four-step cycle; Part II (reg 26.2, 5,000 GT+) - fuel oil data collection plan; Part III (regs 26.3 and 28.8, 5,000 GT+ in listed categories) - operational carbon intensity plan with required CII for three years, a three-year implementation plan, self-evaluation, and a plan of corrective actions for D three years running or E once. 3.6 states the four steps and calls them critical to the continuous cycle, with some elements changing each iteration; 3.7 safety paramount; 3.3 ISO 14001; 3.4 may form part of the SMS. 1 PLANNING (4.1) - the MOST CRUCIAL stage; ship-specific measures (some mutually exclusive), company-specific measures (just in time needs early communication), human resource development, goal setting - measurable and easy to understand; ships not subject to reg 28 need no goal, no publication, no external audit of the SEEMP. 2 IMPLEMENTATION (4.2) - establish a system: procedures, defined tasks, responsible personnel, levels of authority, lines of communication, start and end dates; record the reason if a measure cannot be implemented; document conditions outside the crew's control - berth waiting, port dwell, severe weather - because they may affect the rating. 3 MONITORING (4.3) - quantitative, established method, preferably an international standard; targets the goal indicator (AER, cgDIST, EEOI, CII); regular intervals, fuel by noon reports; continuous consistent reliable data is the foundation; shore-side where possible using logbooks and oil record books; SAR data excluded and recorded separately. 4 SELF-EVALUATION (4.4) - final phase, feeds the next planning stage; four purposes: effectiveness, understanding how and why, the trend, the improved plan; 4.4.3 invest time in cause and effect. WHY CRITICAL: no baseline means no provable saving; no assignment means a document not a practice; no consistent data means opinion; no evaluation means the loop never closes. Part III mirrors it. Contrast: EEDI/EEXI technical and one-off; SEEMP operational and continuous. Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 requires each ship to keep on board a Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan, developed and reviewed taking into account the guidelines adopted by the Organization - which at this sitting are the 2022 Guidelines, resolution MEPC.346(78) of 10 June 2022, revoking the 2016 Guidelines MEPC.282(70). Section 3.6 of those Guidelines states that Part I should be developed as a ship-specific plan by the company and should reflect efforts to improve energy efficiency and reduce carbon intensity through four steps - planning, implementation, monitoring, and self-evaluation and improvement - and that these components play a critical role in the continuous cycle.
Major trap
Answering the question as a description of four steps when the printed command asks for the critical role of each, with suitable examples - three demands, of which description is only one. Alongside it sit the currency traps, and on this question they are unusually dangerous because the identical stem recurs at later sittings where the answers differ. At this sitting the guidelines are MEPC.346(78), the 2022 edition; the strategy is the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018, because the 2023 Strategy was adopted on 7 July 2023, one month after this examination; and no ship has yet been rated under the CII, because 2023 is the first data year and it is still running. The regulation number is the one thing that does not reverse: the SEEMP is regulation 26, not 22, the renumbering having taken effect on 1 November 2022. Finally, the four steps are the framework of Part I only.

Also on the platform: QP2306 Q5 - anti-fouling coatings, a hull-maintenance measure of exactly the kind the planning stage selects · QP2306 Q9 - skin friction reduction, the technical side of the same efficiency problem · QP2306 Q4 - the safety management system, which the Guidelines expressly allow Part I to form part of

First in set First set here. The same task returns at December 2024 Q8.

Understand this first

Both halves of this question turn on the same hinge: when does the carrier's responsibility begin and end. That sounds like housekeeping and it is actually decisive, because the international rules that govern a sea carriage only bite during a defined window. Damage that happens outside that window is not governed by them at all - which cuts both ways, since the carrier loses his special defences and his financial cap, and the cargo owner loses the protections the rules impose. The oldest and still the most widely used scheme draws the window very tightly, from the moment the cargo comes aboard to the moment it leaves the ship, leaving a gap at each end that has to be filled, if at all, by whatever the parties agreed. A later scheme widens it to cover the whole time the carrier has the goods in the port, and in exchange takes away the long list of excuses and simply asks whether he did everything that could reasonably be expected. A third would widen it further still, to the whole journey from the shipper's premises to the receiver's, but it never came into effect. The second half then works a real claim inside the narrowest of those windows, and the thing to grasp is that it is a contest about who has to prove what rather than a list of excuses. The receiver only has to show the cargo went aboard sound and came off damaged. The ship then has to point to a recognised reason it is not answerable - and for a cargo like grain, which spoils itself if it is loaded too wet, there is a very good one. But that reason evaporates if the ship cannot show it prepared the holds properly and looked after the cargo during the passage, and it is almost always the paperwork, not the physics, that decides which way that goes.

Knowledge map
Carrier Responsibility under the Cargo Conventions, and Defences
  1. a
    1. 1Why the duration of responsibility matters
      • the period DECIDES WHETHER A LOSS FALLS INSIDE THE CONVENTION REGIME AT ALL
      • and therefore whether the carrier's EXCEPTIONS and LIMITATION apply
      • damage outside the period is not covered IN EITHER DIRECTION - no exceptions for the carrier, no mandatory obligations for the cargo
    2. 2Hague and Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle
      • Article I(e) - 'carriage of goods' covers the period FROM THE TIME THE GOODS ARE LOADED ON TO THE TIME THEY ARE DISCHARGED from the ship
      • THE VISBY AND SDR PROTOCOLS DO NOT CHANGE THE PERIOD - they change limitation, evidence in third-party hands, containers, and servants and agents
      • Article I(b) - the Rules apply only to a contract covered by a BILL OF LADING OR SIMILAR DOCUMENT OF TITLE
    3. 3Hamburg - port to port
      • the carrier is responsible DURING THE PERIOD IN WHICH HE IS IN CHARGE OF THE GOODS AT THE PORT OF LOADING, DURING THE CARRIAGE, AND AT THE PORT OF DISCHARGE
      • it deliberately CLOSES THE TWO GAPS the Hague regime leaves
      • the basis changes too - PRESUMED FAULT: liable unless he proves he and his servants and agents TOOK ALL MEASURES THAT COULD REASONABLY BE REQUIRED to avoid the occurrence and its consequences
    4. 4Rotterdam - door to door, and not in force
      • the period would run FROM RECEIPT TO DELIVERY, so a single contract including an INLAND LEG falls under one regime
      • SEAWORTHINESS WOULD BECOME A CONTINUING OBLIGATION THROUGHOUT THE VOYAGE, not only before and at its beginning
      • NAUTICAL FAULT ABOLISHED
    5. 5The comparison, and the Indian position
      • compare on four axes - PERIOD, BASIS OF LIABILITY, NAUTICAL FAULT, TIME BAR - plus deck cargo and status
      • Hague/Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle, excepted perils, nautical fault A DEFENCE, one year, deck cargo excluded, IN FORCE and dominant
      • Hamburg - port to port, presumed fault, nautical fault ABOLISHED, two years, deck cargo included, IN FORCE but limited reach
  2. b
    1. 6The grain claim - how it is framed, and who must prove what
      • ANSWER AS A SEQUENCE OF BURDENS, NOT A LIST OF DEFENCES
      • 1 - the consignee produces a CLEAN BILL (prima facie, and CONCLUSIVE in a good-faith third party's hands) and a damaged OUTTURN. GOOD IN, BAD OUT
      • 2 - THE CARRIER MUST BRING HIMSELF WITHIN AN EXCEPTION and THE BURDEN IS ON HIM
    2. 7The excepted perils that answer a grain claim
      • (m) WASTAGE IN BULK OR WEIGHT, AND INHERENT DEFECT, QUALITY OR VICE - the principal defence. Grain is hygroscopic; above its safe moisture content it self-heats, sweats, cakes and moulds without fault of the ship
      • (m) also answers short-delivery by natural moisture loss and draught-survey inaccuracy - TRADE ALLOWANCE
      • (i) ACT OR OMISSION OF THE SHIPPER - grain loaded wet, hot or infested; loading in rain against protest; a false moisture declaration
    3. 8The overriding obligations that can defeat every one of those defences
      • THE EXCEPTIONS DO NOT OPERATE IF THE CARRIER IS IN BREACH OF ARTICLE III
      • ARTICLE III RULE 1 - due diligence BEFORE AND AT THE BEGINNING OF THE VOYAGE: seaworthy, properly manned equipped and supplied, and HOLDS FIT AND SAFE for reception, carriage and preservation
      • on grain that means HOLD CLEANLINESS, DRYNESS AND FREEDOM FROM INFESTATION; HATCH COVERS WEATHERTIGHT AND TESTED; BILGES, STRUMS, SOUNDING PIPES AND NON-RETURN VALVES CLEAR; VENTILATORS AND CLOSING APPLIANCES SOUND; BALLAST AND FUEL LINES THROUGH THE HOLDS TESTED
    4. 9Procedural and quantum defences, and the evidence
      • ARTICLE III RULE 6 NOTICE - in writing AT OR BEFORE REMOVAL, or WITHIN THREE DAYS where the damage is not apparent. Absence is not fatal but is prima facie evidence of good delivery
      • THE ONE-YEAR TIME BAR - discharged from all liability unless SUIT IS BROUGHT WITHIN ONE YEAR. A complete answer, and the FIRST THING TO CHECK. Two years under Hamburg
      • TITLE TO SUE - does the claimant hold the bill and have the right to sue on it

Limb a) is three phrases and a status - TACKLE TO TACKLE (2), PORT TO PORT (3), DOOR TO DOOR (4), the last NOT IN FORCE - compared on four axes at (5): PERIOD, BASIS, NAUTICAL FAULT, TIME BAR, with India on HAGUE-VISBY (5). Remember9 route headings
Cover61 core points beneath them

Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 61 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Why the duration of responsibility matters
  • the period DECIDES WHETHER A LOSS FALLS INSIDE THE CONVENTION REGIME AT ALL
  • and therefore whether the carrier's EXCEPTIONS and LIMITATION apply
  • damage outside the period is not covered IN EITHER DIRECTION - no exceptions for the carrier, no mandatory obligations for the cargo
  • the claimant is then thrown back on the general law of bailment or on the contract terms
  • the three in-force regimes draw the period in three different places; the fourth would draw it wider still
2. Hague and Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle
  • Article I(e) - 'carriage of goods' covers the period FROM THE TIME THE GOODS ARE LOADED ON TO THE TIME THEY ARE DISCHARGED from the ship
  • THE VISBY AND SDR PROTOCOLS DO NOT CHANGE THE PERIOD - they change limitation, evidence in third-party hands, containers, and servants and agents
  • Article I(b) - the Rules apply only to a contract covered by a BILL OF LADING OR SIMILAR DOCUMENT OF TITLE
  • Article I(c) - 'goods' EXCLUDES LIVE ANIMALS AND DECK CARGO stated as carried on deck and so carried
  • EXAMPLE - steel coils damaged in a shore warehouse before loading, or stacked on the quay after discharge, are OUTSIDE the period
  • TACKLE TO TACKLE LEAVES TWO UNCOVERED GAPS, ONE AT EACH END
3. Hamburg - port to port
  • the carrier is responsible DURING THE PERIOD IN WHICH HE IS IN CHARGE OF THE GOODS AT THE PORT OF LOADING, DURING THE CARRIAGE, AND AT THE PORT OF DISCHARGE
  • it deliberately CLOSES THE TWO GAPS the Hague regime leaves
  • the basis changes too - PRESUMED FAULT: liable unless he proves he and his servants and agents TOOK ALL MEASURES THAT COULD REASONABLY BE REQUIRED to avoid the occurrence and its consequences
  • NO CATALOGUE OF EXCEPTED PERILS to shelter behind
  • NAUTICAL FAULT ABOLISHED; fire recast; DECK CARGO AND LIVE ANIMALS INCLUDED; DELAY expressly covered; time bar TWO YEARS
  • EXAMPLE - bagged cargo received into the carrier's custody in a port shed two days before loading and damaged by a roof leak is OUTSIDE Hague-Visby and INSIDE Hamburg
4. Rotterdam - door to door, and not in force
  • the period would run FROM RECEIPT TO DELIVERY, so a single contract including an INLAND LEG falls under one regime
  • SEAWORTHINESS WOULD BECOME A CONTINUING OBLIGATION THROUGHOUT THE VOYAGE, not only before and at its beginning
  • NAUTICAL FAULT ABOLISHED
  • THEY ARE NOT IN FORCE AT THIS EXAMINATION AND NEVER HAVE BEEN - state it plainly
  • relevant as the direction of travel and as the sharpest illustration of period extension - NOT AS APPLICABLE LAW
  • EXAMPLE - a container collected from an inland factory, railed, shipped and delivered to an inland warehouse
5. The comparison, and the Indian position
  • compare on four axes - PERIOD, BASIS OF LIABILITY, NAUTICAL FAULT, TIME BAR - plus deck cargo and status
  • Hague/Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle, excepted perils, nautical fault A DEFENCE, one year, deck cargo excluded, IN FORCE and dominant
  • Hamburg - port to port, presumed fault, nautical fault ABOLISHED, two years, deck cargo included, IN FORCE but limited reach
  • Rotterdam - door to door, continuing seaworthiness, nautical fault abolished, NOT IN FORCE
  • INDIA APPLIES HAGUE-VISBY through the CARRIAGE OF GOODS BY SEA ACT, 1925 AS AMENDED IN 1993 giving effect to the Visby and SDR Protocols
  • India is NOT a party to the Hamburg Rules and has not ratified Rotterdam
  • the general merchant shipping statute at this sitting is the MERCHANT SHIPPING ACT, 1958
  • SO LIMB B IS WORKED UNDER HAGUE-VISBY
6. The grain claim - how it is framed, and who must prove what
  • ANSWER AS A SEQUENCE OF BURDENS, NOT A LIST OF DEFENCES
  • 1 - the consignee produces a CLEAN BILL (prima facie, and CONCLUSIVE in a good-faith third party's hands) and a damaged OUTTURN. GOOD IN, BAD OUT
  • 2 - THE CARRIER MUST BRING HIMSELF WITHIN AN EXCEPTION and THE BURDEN IS ON HIM
  • 3 - the claimant may DEFEAT THE EXCEPTION by proving breach of the OVERRIDING OBLIGATIONS - want of due diligence, or failure to care for the cargo
  • BEFORE ANY OF THAT, TWO CONTRACT QUESTIONS - what does the bill actually say (clean? shipped or received-for-shipment? weight or quality qualification?)
  • and WHO CONTRACTED TO DO THE LOADING, STOWAGE AND DISCHARGE - where transferred to shipper or charterer, responsibility may go with them
7. The excepted perils that answer a grain claim
  • (m) WASTAGE IN BULK OR WEIGHT, AND INHERENT DEFECT, QUALITY OR VICE - the principal defence. Grain is hygroscopic; above its safe moisture content it self-heats, sweats, cakes and moulds without fault of the ship
  • (m) also answers short-delivery by natural moisture loss and draught-survey inaccuracy - TRADE ALLOWANCE
  • (i) ACT OR OMISSION OF THE SHIPPER - grain loaded wet, hot or infested; loading in rain against protest; a false moisture declaration
  • (n) insufficiency of PACKING and (o) insufficiency of MARKS where any part was bagged
  • (c) PERILS OF THE SEA - heavy weather beyond what the ship could reasonably guard against, with properly maintained closing appliances
  • (a) NAUTICAL FAULT AND MANAGEMENT OF THE SHIP - available under Hague-Visby, ABOLISHED under Hamburg. But CONFINED TO MANAGEMENT OF THE SHIP; ventilation is care of the CARGO and is NOT within it
  • (b) FIRE unless the actual fault or privity of the carrier - relevant where self-heating has become a hold fire
  • (p) LATENT DEFECTS not discoverable by due diligence; (q) the RESIDUAL exception, loss without actual fault or privity and without fault of servants or agents
8. The overriding obligations that can defeat every one of those defences
  • THE EXCEPTIONS DO NOT OPERATE IF THE CARRIER IS IN BREACH OF ARTICLE III
  • ARTICLE III RULE 1 - due diligence BEFORE AND AT THE BEGINNING OF THE VOYAGE: seaworthy, properly manned equipped and supplied, and HOLDS FIT AND SAFE for reception, carriage and preservation
  • on grain that means HOLD CLEANLINESS, DRYNESS AND FREEDOM FROM INFESTATION; HATCH COVERS WEATHERTIGHT AND TESTED; BILGES, STRUMS, SOUNDING PIPES AND NON-RETURN VALVES CLEAR; VENTILATORS AND CLOSING APPLIANCES SOUND; BALLAST AND FUEL LINES THROUGH THE HOLDS TESTED
  • ARTICLE IV RULE 1 PUTS THE BURDEN OF PROVING DUE DILIGENCE ON THE CARRIER
  • ARTICLE III RULE 2 - properly and carefully load, handle, stow, carry, keep, CARE FOR and discharge
  • on grain the live issue is VENTILATION - a defensible rule based on DEW-POINT COMPARISON between hold and outside air, a record of what was done and why, and ventilation correctly STOPPED when it would introduce moist air
  • ARTICLE III RULE 8 - contracting out of the Rules is NULL AND VOID, so the defences cannot be enlarged by contract
  • THE PRACTICAL CONSEQUENCE: a carrier who cannot produce hold-preparation records, hatch-cover tests and a proper ventilation log OFTEN LOSES A FACTUALLY CORRECT INHERENT-VICE DEFENCE, because he cannot discharge his burden
9. Procedural and quantum defences, and the evidence
  • ARTICLE III RULE 6 NOTICE - in writing AT OR BEFORE REMOVAL, or WITHIN THREE DAYS where the damage is not apparent. Absence is not fatal but is prima facie evidence of good delivery
  • THE ONE-YEAR TIME BAR - discharged from all liability unless SUIT IS BROUGHT WITHIN ONE YEAR. A complete answer, and the FIRST THING TO CHECK. Two years under Hamburg
  • TITLE TO SUE - does the claimant hold the bill and have the right to sue on it
  • ARTICLE IV RULE 5 LIMITATION - per package or unit, or PER KILOGRAMME OF GROSS WEIGHT, WHICHEVER IS THE HIGHER; on a bulk cargo the weight basis operates. NO FIGURE ASSERTED
  • the right to limit is LOST for an act or omission done with intent, or recklessly and with knowledge that damage would probably result
  • QUANTUM - sound arrived value less damaged arrived value; the claimant must MITIGATE by segregation, reconditioning or sale
  • THE EVIDENCE, WITHOUT WHICH NONE OF THE ABOVE WORKS - hold preparation records and photographs; hatch-cover ultrasonic or hose test certificates; loading and draught surveys and moisture certificates; VENTILATION LOG WITH HOLD AND AMBIENT DEW POINTS; weather logs; protest at loading; prompt surveyors and a JOINT SURVEY at discharge
  • CLOSE - the defences exist only because the loss fell INSIDE the period examined in limb a)
Model written answer

1. Why the duration of responsibility matters

The period of responsibility is not a technicality. It decides whether a loss falls inside the convention regime at all - and therefore whether the carrier is answerable under it, whether his exceptions and his limitation of liability apply, and whether the claimant is instead thrown back on the general law of bailment or on the terms of the contract.

Damage occurring outside the period is not covered by the Rules in either direction: the carrier does not get the benefit of the exceptions or the limits, and the cargo owner does not get the benefit of the mandatory obligations. The three regimes in force draw that period in three different places, and the fourth would draw it wider still.

2. Hague and Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle

  • The Hague Rules define 'carriage of goods' as covering the period from the time when the goods are loaded on to the time they are discharged from the ship - the classic "tackle to tackle" period.
  • The Hague-Visby Rules are the same Rules as amended by the Visby Protocol 1968 and the SDR Protocol 1979. They do not change the period of responsibility; they change the evidential effect of the bill in third-party hands, the limitation figures and their basis, the treatment of containers, and the extension of the defences to servants and agents.
  • Two further limits on scope matter as much as the period. The Rules apply only to a contract covered by a bill of lading or similar document of title, so a pure charterparty relationship is outside them; and 'goods' excludes live animals and deck cargo stated as carried on deck and so carried.
  • Example. Steel coils are damaged in a shore warehouse before loading, or in a stack on the quay after discharge. Neither loss is within the Hague-Visby period, and the claimant must look to the terms of the contract or to the general law. Damage caused by rough handling as the sling is landed on the deck is within it.
  • The consequence to state. Tackle to tackle leaves two uncovered gaps, one at each end, filled - or not - by the contract.

3. Hamburg - port to port

  • The Hamburg Rules make the carrier responsible for the goods during the period in which he is in charge of them at the port of loading, during the carriage, and at the port of discharge. That is "port to port", and it deliberately closes the two gaps the Hague regime leaves.
  • The change of period comes with a change of basis. Liability rests on presumed fault: the carrier is liable for loss, damage or delay unless he proves that he, his servants and agents took all measures that could reasonably be required to avoid the occurrence and its consequences. There is no catalogue of excepted perils to shelter behind.
  • The nautical fault exception is abolished, fire liability is recast, deck cargo and live animals are brought within the regime, delay is expressly covered, and the time bar is two years rather than one.
  • Example. Bagged cargo received into the carrier's custody in a port shed two days before loading and damaged by a shed roof leak is outside Hague-Visby and inside Hamburg, because the carrier was in charge of it at the port of loading.

4. Rotterdam - door to door, and not in force

  • The Rotterdam Rules would run the period of responsibility from receipt to delivery - "door to door" - so that a single carriage contract including an inland leg falls under one regime rather than being split between a sea convention and a road or rail one.
  • They would also extend the seaworthiness obligation to a continuing one, throughout the voyage, rather than only before and at its beginning, and would abolish the nautical fault exception.
  • They are NOT in force at this examination and never have been. That must be stated plainly. The Rules are relevant to this question as the intended direction of travel and as the sharpest illustration of how far a period of responsibility can be extended - not as applicable law.
  • Example. A container collected from an inland factory, railed to the port, shipped and delivered to a consignee's inland warehouse is a single door-to-door contract. Under Rotterdam it would be governed throughout by one regime; today it is not.

5. The comparison, and the Indian position

Hague / Hague-VisbyHamburgRotterdam
PeriodTackle to tackle - loading to dischargePort to port - in charge at the load port, carriage, discharge portDoor to door - receipt to delivery
Basis of liabilityDuty to care, with a catalogue of excepted perilsPresumed fault - carrier must prove all reasonable measures were takenFault-based, no nautical fault exception
Nautical faultA defenceAbolishedAbolished
SeaworthinessDue diligence before and at the beginning of the voyageSubsumed in the general dutyContinuing throughout the voyage
Deck cargo, live animalsExcluded from 'goods'IncludedIncluded
Time barOne yearTwo yearsTwo years
Status at this sittingIn force; the dominant regimeIn force, but with limited commercial reachNOT IN FORCE

India applies the Hague-Visby regime. The Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925, as amended in 1993 to give effect to the Visby and SDR Protocols, is the operative statute at this sitting. India is not a party to the Hamburg Rules and has not ratified the Rotterdam Rules. So the answer to limb b) is worked under Hague-Visby, and the general merchant shipping statute in force is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.

6. The grain claim - how it is framed, and who must prove what

Limb b) is best answered as a sequence of burdens, not as a list of defences. Setting the sequence out is worth more than any single exception.

  1. The consignee establishes a prima facie case. He produces a clean bill of lading - prima facie evidence that the grain was shipped in apparent good order and condition, and conclusive evidence in his hands if he took it in good faith as a third party - and an outturn showing damaged cargo. Good in, bad out.
  2. The carrier must then bring himself within an exception under Article IV rule 2, and the burden of proving it is on him. It is not enough to say the cause is unknown - although the residual exception at rule 2(q) exists precisely for the case where he can show the loss arose without actual fault or privity on his part and without fault or neglect of his agents or servants.
  3. The claimant may then defeat the exception by showing a breach of the overriding obligations: want of due diligence to make the ship seaworthy and the holds fit, or a failure to care properly for the cargo.
  4. Before any of that, two contract questions must be answered, because they may dispose of the claim entirely: what does the bill of lading actually say - is it clean, is it shipped or received-for-shipment, does it carry a weight or quality qualification - and who contracted to do the loading, stowage and discharge. Where those operations were transferred to the shipper or charterer, responsibility for damage caused by them may go with them.

7. The excepted perils that answer a grain claim

Article IV rule 2 gives a catalogue; these are the heads that actually arise on a grain cargo:

  • (m) Wastage in bulk or weight, and inherent defect, quality or vice of the goods. The most important defence on this facts. Grain is hygroscopic: shipped above its safe moisture content it will self-heat, sweat, cake and mould without any fault of the ship. Short-delivery claims on a bulk grain cargo are also commonly answered by natural moisture loss and by the inherent inaccuracy of draught survey - trade allowance.
  • (i) Act or omission of the shipper or owner of the goods. Grain loaded wet, hot, or already infested; loading in rain against the ship's protest; a false or inadequate declaration of moisture content.
  • (n) Insufficiency of packing and (o) insufficiency or inadequacy of marks, where any part of the parcel was bagged.
  • (c) Perils, dangers and accidents of the sea. Heavy weather of a severity the ship could not reasonably guard against, causing water ingress despite properly maintained and tested closing appliances.
  • (a) Act, neglect or default of the master or crew in the navigation or in the MANAGEMENT of the ship. Available under Hague-Visby and abolished under Hamburg - a difference worth naming. But it is confined to the management of the ship; an act done for the care of the cargo, such as mishandling ventilation, is not within it and is a breach of Article III rule 2.
  • (b) Fire, unless caused by the actual fault or privity of the carrier - relevant where self-heating has progressed to a hold fire.
  • (p) Latent defects not discoverable by due diligence - a hidden crack in a ballast line or a hatch coaming defect not findable on proper inspection.
  • (q) The residual exception, where the carrier can show the loss arose without his actual fault or privity and without the fault or neglect of his servants or agents.

8. The overriding obligations that can defeat every one of those defences

This is the half of the answer that separates a good script. The exceptions do not operate if the carrier is in breach of Article III.

  • Article III rule 1 - due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage to make the ship seaworthy, to properly man, equip and supply her, and to make the holds fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of the cargo. On grain that means hold cleanliness, dryness and freedom from infestation and residues; hatch covers weathertight and pressure- or hose-tested; bilge wells, strums, sounding pipes and non-return valves proved clear and operable; ventilators and air pipes sound and closing appliances working; and ballast and fuel lines through the holds tested for tightness. Article IV rule 1 puts the burden of proving due diligence squarely on the carrier.
  • Article III rule 2 - properly and carefully load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge the goods. On grain the live issue is almost always ventilation: whether the ship followed a defensible rule based on dew-point comparison between hold and outside air, whether the record shows what was actually done and why, and whether ventilation was correctly stopped in conditions that would have introduced moist air.
  • Article III rule 8 - any clause purporting to relieve the carrier otherwise than as the Rules provide is null and void, so the defences cannot be enlarged by contract.
  • The practical consequence, and the one that decides real cases: a carrier who cannot produce hold-preparation records, hatch-cover test results and a proper ventilation log will very often lose an inherent-vice defence that was factually correct, because he cannot discharge the burden the Rules place on him.

9. Procedural and quantum defences, and the evidence

  • Notice of loss or damage under Article III rule 6 - in writing at or before removal of the goods, or within three days where the damage is not apparent. Absence of notice is not fatal, but it is prima facie evidence that the goods were delivered as described.
  • The one-year time bar. Under Hague-Visby the carrier is discharged from all liability unless suit is brought within one year of delivery or of the date when the goods should have been delivered. It is a complete answer and it is the first thing to check.
  • Title to sue. Whether the claimant actually holds the bill of lading and has the right to sue on it.
  • Limitation of liability under Article IV rule 5 - a figure per package or unit, or per kilogramme of gross weight, whichever is the higher. Not a defence to liability but a cap on it, and on a bulk cargo the weight basis is the operative one. The right to limit is lost where the damage resulted from an act or omission done with intent to cause damage, or recklessly and with knowledge that damage would probably result.
  • Quantum. Damaged grain is rarely a total loss: it is usually sound-arrived-value less damaged-arrived-value, and the claimant is under a duty to mitigate - by segregation, reconditioning or sale of the damaged parcel.
  • The evidence, without which none of the above works. Hold-preparation and cleaning records with photographs; hatch-cover ultrasonic or hose test certificates; the loading survey, draught survey and moisture-content certificates; the ventilation log with hold and ambient dew points; deck and engine log entries for the weather; the pre-loading survey and any protest at loading; and prompt appointment of surveyors on both sides, with a joint survey at discharge wherever it can be arranged.

Close with the point that ties the two limbs together: the defences in limb b) are available only because the loss fell inside the period of responsibility examined in limb a). Move the damage to the quay before loading, and the Hague-Visby exceptions - and its limits - do not apply at all.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Eight and eight, printed - so unlike most of this paper the budget is fixed. Limb a) asks for a comparison across conventions with examples, and the marks are for the differences, not for a description of each regime in turn. Limb b) is a scenario, and the marks are for a defence strategy, not for a recitation of Article IV rule 2.

The route reflects that. Limb a) runs the three in-force regimes in ascending order of period - tackle to tackle, port to port, then the door-to-door regime that is not in force - and finishes with a comparison table and the Indian position, because the paper is an Indian one and the second limb has to be worked under some particular law. Limb b) is built as a burden-of-proof sequence rather than a list, and the exceptions are only the second of its four beats.

The three phrases that carry limb a)

Tackle to tackle. Port to port. Door to door. If you can write those three and attach the right convention to each, you have the spine of the limb. Then attach one consequence to each: tackle to tackle leaves a gap at each end; port to port closes both; door to door would cover the inland legs as well.

And remember the negative that is worth a mark by itself: the Visby and SDR Protocols do not change the period. They change limitation, evidence, containers and the position of servants and agents. A candidate who says Hague-Visby extended the period has misunderstood what the Protocols did.

The Rotterdam Rules are not in force - say it plainly

They must be discussed, because the question asks about the different conventions and the door-to-door period is the sharpest illustration of the point. But they must be labelled. They are not in force at this sitting and never have been. Presenting them as applicable law is the single most damaging error available on limb a), and it is easy to make because they are extensively written about.

Limb b) is a sequence of burdens, not a list

Almost every weak answer to a cargo-claim question produces the Article IV rule 2 catalogue and stops. The examiner is asking what defence is available, which requires knowing who has to prove what:

  1. Claimant: clean bill in, damaged cargo out. Prima facie case made.
  2. Carrier: bring yourself within an exception - and the burden is on you.
  3. Claimant: defeat the exception by proving a breach of Article III - unseaworthiness through want of due diligence, or failure to care for the cargo.
  4. Carrier, throughout: Article IV rule 1 puts the burden of proving due diligence on him.

State that sequence and the exceptions then fall into place as the second beat rather than as the whole answer.

Why grain is the cargo the examiner chose

Grain is hygroscopic and it is the classic inherent vice cargo. Loaded above its safe moisture content it will self-heat, sweat, cake and mould with no fault on the part of the ship at all, which makes Article IV rule 2(m) - wastage in bulk or weight, and inherent defect, quality or vice - the natural defence. Add (i), act or omission of the shipper, where the grain was loaded wet or hot or was mis-declared.

But the same facts make the counter-attack obvious, and it is where cases are actually decided: the cargo interest will say the ship ventilated badly. That is a failure to care for the cargo under Article III rule 2, it is not within the management-of-the-ship exception, and it defeats the inherent-vice defence. Which is why the ventilation log with hold and ambient dew points is the most valuable single document on a wet-damage grain claim.

Management of the ship versus care of the cargo

A distinction worth having ready. Article IV rule 2(a) excuses act, neglect or default of the master or crew in the navigation or in the MANAGEMENT OF THE SHIP. It does not excuse acts done for the care of the cargo. Mishandling ballast in a way that affects the ship is management of the ship; mishandling ventilation, which exists to protect the cargo, is not - it is a breach of Article III rule 2.

Add that the exception is abolished under the Hamburg Rules, which connects the two limbs neatly and shows the comparison in limb a) was not merely descriptive.

Check the time bar first

Under Hague-Visby the carrier is discharged from all liability unless suit is brought within one year. It is a complete answer, it costs nothing to check, and it is the first thing a practitioner looks at. Contrast the two-year bar under Hamburg. Notice of damage under Article III rule 6 - at or before removal, or within three days if the damage is not apparent - is not a bar, but its absence is prima facie evidence of good delivery.

Common mistakes

  • Describing the three regimes in turn without comparing them. The marks are in the differences.
  • Saying the Visby Protocol extended the period of responsibility. It did not.
  • Treating the Rotterdam Rules as in force. They are not, and never have been.
  • Forgetting that Hague-Visby excludes live animals and deck cargo stated as carried on deck, and that Hamburg includes them.
  • Omitting that the Rules apply only to a contract covered by a bill of lading or similar document of title.
  • Answering limb b) with the Article IV rule 2 list and no burden of proof.
  • Missing inherent vice under rule 2(m), which is the natural defence to a grain claim.
  • Failing to see that bad ventilation defeats the inherent-vice defence, because it is a breach of Article III rule 2.
  • Putting ventilation inside the management-of-the-ship exception. It is care of the cargo.
  • Forgetting the one-year time bar, which is a complete defence.
  • Quoting SDR limitation figures. They are amended over time and a stale number is worse than none.
  • Working limb b) under the wrong law. India applies Hague-Visby, through the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993.

Examiner traps

  • "Cargo is damaged in the port shed before loading. Which regime covers it?" Not Hague-Visby - outside tackle to tackle. Hamburg would, if it applied.
  • "Did the Visby Protocol change the period of responsibility?" No.
  • "Are the Rotterdam Rules in force?" No.
  • "Which regime abolishes nautical fault?" Hamburg - and Rotterdam would.
  • "Is deck cargo covered?" Not under Hague-Visby where it is stated as carried on deck and so carried. It is under Hamburg.
  • "Who proves due diligence?" The carrier - Article IV rule 1.
  • "Is bad ventilation management of the ship?" No. It is care of the cargo under Article III rule 2, and the exception does not cover it.
  • "The grain was wet when loaded. Is that a defence?" Yes in principle - inherent vice, and act or omission of the shipper. But only if you can prove the condition at loading and prove your own due diligence.
  • "How long has the consignee to sue?" One year under Hague-Visby; two under Hamburg.
  • "What single document decides most wet-damage grain claims?" The ventilation log, with hold and ambient dew points.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Define the Hague period of responsibility. From the time the goods are loaded on to the time they are discharged from the ship.
  • And the Hamburg period? While the carrier is in charge of the goods at the port of loading, during carriage, and at the port of discharge.
  • What is the basis of liability under Hamburg? Presumed fault - the carrier is liable unless he proves he and his servants took all measures that could reasonably be required.
  • What would Rotterdam change? Receipt-to-delivery period, continuing seaworthiness throughout the voyage, no nautical fault exception. Not in force.
  • Which regime does India apply and under what statute? Hague-Visby, under the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993.
  • State Article III rule 1 and Article III rule 2. Due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage to make the ship seaworthy and the holds fit; and properly and carefully load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge the goods.
  • Name four excepted perils relevant to grain. Inherent vice and wastage in bulk; act or omission of the shipper; perils of the sea; latent defects not discoverable by due diligence.
  • What defeats an inherent-vice defence? Proof that the carrier failed to exercise due diligence to make the holds fit, or failed to care for the cargo - typically ventilation.
  • What records would you produce to defend the claim? Hold preparation and cleaning records with photographs; hatch-cover test certificates; loading and draught surveys and moisture certificates; ventilation log with dew points; weather log; any protest at loading.
  • What is the time bar and what is the notice requirement? Suit within one year; notice in writing at or before removal, or within three days if the damage is not apparent.

Memory framework

Limb a) is three phrases and a status: TACKLE TO TACKLE (Hague / Hague-Visby, in force) · PORT TO PORT (Hamburg, in force) · DOOR TO DOOR (Rotterdam, NOT in force). Then four axes of comparison: PERIOD · BASIS · NAUTICAL FAULT · TIME BAR. And the Indian anchor: Hague-Visby, by the 1925 Act as amended in 1993.

Limb b) is four beats: GOOD IN, BAD OUT · FIND AN EXCEPTION · SURVIVE ARTICLE III · CHECK THE CLOCK. Inside the exceptions, for grain: VICE, SHIPPER, SEA, LATENT. Inside Article III: HOLDS FIT, CARGO CARED FOR. And the practical rule that decides real cases: NO RECORDS, NO DEFENCE.

Regulation and source map

  • Hague Rules 1924 - Article I(b) contract of carriage; I(c) goods, excluding live animals and deck cargo; I(e) the period, loading to discharge; Article II the scope of responsibility
  • Article III rule 1 due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage - seaworthiness, manning, holds fit; rule 2 properly and carefully load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge
  • Article III rules 3 and 4 the bill as prima facie and, for a third party in good faith, conclusive evidence; rule 6 notice at or before removal or within three days, and the one-year time bar; rule 8 contracting out is null and void
  • Article IV rule 1 unseaworthiness only where due diligence was wanting, burden on the carrier; rule 2(a) to (q) the excepted perils, including (a) nautical fault and management of the ship, (b) fire, (c) perils of the sea, (i) act or omission of the shipper, (m) wastage and inherent vice, (n) insufficiency of packing, (p) latent defects, (q) the residual exception
  • Article IV rule 4 reasonable deviation; rule 5 package or weight limitation - no figure asserted
  • Visby Protocol 1968 and SDR Protocol 1979 - limitation, evidence, containers, servants and agents. The period is unchanged
  • Hamburg Rules 1978, in force 1992 - port to port; presumed fault; nautical fault abolished; deck cargo and live animals included; delay covered; two-year time bar
  • Rotterdam Rules 2008 - receipt to delivery; continuing seaworthiness; nautical fault abolished. NOT IN FORCE
  • Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925, as amended 1993 - gives effect to Hague-Visby. Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 is the general statute at this sitting

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

MIW holds no licensed consolidated text of the Hague, Hague-Visby, Hamburg or Rotterdam Rules, nor of the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925. Every Article relied on is established from MIW's own verification record for an earlier sitting of this identical question, where the provisions were set out, and is given in substance. No Article is quoted verbatim anywhere in this answer.

Three things are therefore deliberately not asserted. No package or weight limitation figure - the Article IV rule 5 limits are expressed in Special Drawing Rights and have been amended over time, so the mechanism is described and no number is given. No number of ratifications or States party for any of the four conventions, because those figures move continuously; what is asserted about the Rotterdam Rules - that they are not in force - is certain and needs no count. And no case law in any jurisdiction: the burden-of-proof sequence is stated as it follows from the Articles themselves.

One further deliberate omission. The question does not say whether the bill of lading is a shipped or a received-for-shipment bill, or whether loading, stowage and discharge were transferred to the shipper or charterer by the contract. Both are left open and identified as questions the carrier must answer before choosing his defence, rather than assumed either way.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

Flashcards 12

1 Why the period matters. 2 Hague and Hague-Visby, tackle to tackle. 3 Hamburg, port to port. 4 Rotterdam, door to door and not in force. 5 The comparison and the Indian position. 6 How the grain claim is framed and who proves what. 7 The excepted perils. 8 The overriding Article III obligations. 9 Procedure, quantum and the evidence.

Eight and eight printed; limb a) is a comparison and limb b) is a burden-of-proof sequence, not a list.

Hague and Hague-Visby: from the time the goods are loaded on to the time they are discharged from the ship - tackle to tackle. Hamburg: while the carrier is in charge of the goods at the port of loading, during the carriage, and at the port of discharge - port to port. Rotterdam: from receipt to delivery - door to door, and not in force.

Three phrases carry the whole of limb a); everything else hangs off them.

No. The Hague-Visby Rules are the Hague Rules as amended by the Visby Protocol 1968 and the SDR Protocol 1979, and those Protocols changed limitation figures and their basis, the evidential effect of the bill in a good-faith third party's hands, the treatment of containers and the extension of defences to servants and agents. The tackle-to-tackle period is unchanged.

A very common misstatement, and correcting it shows you know what the Protocols actually did.

They are not in force and never have been. They must still be discussed because the question asks about the different conventions and the door-to-door period is the sharpest illustration of how far a period of responsibility can extend - together with continuing seaworthiness throughout the voyage and the abolition of nautical fault. But they must be labelled as not applicable law.

Presenting them as in force is the most damaging single error available on limb a).

Period - tackle to tackle against port to port. Basis of liability - a duty of care with a catalogue of excepted perils, against presumed fault where the carrier must prove all reasonable measures were taken. Nautical fault - a defence, against abolished. Time bar - one year against two. Add that deck cargo and live animals are excluded from Hague-Visby 'goods' and included under Hamburg.

Limb a)'s marks are in the differences, and four axes make them countable.

The Hague-Visby regime, under the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993 to give effect to the Visby and SDR Protocols. India is not a party to the Hamburg Rules and has not ratified the Rotterdam Rules. The general merchant shipping statute is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.

The second limb has to be worked under some particular law, and this is it.

The claimant establishes a prima facie case with a clean bill of lading - prima facie evidence of good order, conclusive in a good-faith third party's hands - and a damaged outturn. The carrier must then bring himself within an excepted peril, and the burden is on him. The claimant may then defeat it by proving breach of the overriding obligations - want of due diligence, or failure to care for the cargo. Article IV rule 1 keeps the burden of proving due diligence on the carrier.

Answering limb b) with a list of exceptions and no sequence misses what the question actually asks.

Grain is hygroscopic; loaded above its safe moisture content it will self-heat, sweat, cake and mould without any fault of the ship, which is Article IV rule 2(m) - wastage in bulk or weight and inherent defect, quality or vice. It is destroyed by proof that the carrier failed to exercise due diligence to make the holds fit under Article III rule 1, or failed to care properly for the cargo under Article III rule 2 - in practice, by bad or unrecorded ventilation.

The defence and its counter-attack are the two halves of the same question, and the case turns on the second.

No. Article IV rule 2(a) excuses act, neglect or default of the master or crew in the navigation or in the management of the ship. Ventilation is done for the care of the cargo, so it falls under Article III rule 2 and is a breach, not an excepted peril. Note also that the exception is abolished altogether under the Hamburg Rules.

The management-of-the-ship line is the classic dividing question in cargo claims and this is its clearest instance.

Due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage under Article III rule 1: holds clean, dry and free of infestation and previous residues; hatch covers weathertight and ultrasonically or hose tested; bilge wells, strums, sounding pipes and non-return valves clear and operable; ventilators, air pipes and closing appliances sound; and ballast and fuel lines passing through the holds tested for tightness. Article IV rule 1 puts the burden of proving that diligence on him.

It is the concrete engineering content of an otherwise abstract legal obligation.

Notice of loss or damage in writing at or before removal of the goods, or within three days where the damage is not apparent; absence of notice is not fatal but is prima facie evidence that the goods were delivered as described. The carrier is discharged from all liability unless suit is brought within one year under Hague-Visby, or two years under Hamburg. The bar is checked first because it is a complete answer and costs nothing to establish.

A complete defence that many candidates never mention.

The ventilation log, showing hold and ambient dew points and what was done and why - together with hold preparation records and photographs, hatch-cover test certificates, the loading and draught surveys and moisture-content certificates, weather log entries, any protest at loading, and a joint survey at discharge. A carrier who cannot produce them will often lose a factually correct inherent-vice defence because he cannot discharge his burden.

The single most practical point on the question - no records, no defence.

  • Hamburg Rules - in force 1992; time bar TWO years
  • Rotterdam Rules 2008 - NOT IN FORCE at this sitting
  • Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended 1993 - the Hague-Visby regime
  • NO SDR package or weight limitation figure is asserted, and no ratification count for any convention
Critical regulation
Hague Rules Article I(e) defines carriage of goods as covering the period from the time when the goods are loaded on to the time they are discharged from the ship - the tackle-to-tackle period, unchanged by the Visby and SDR Protocols. Article III rule 1 binds the carrier, before and at the beginning of the voyage, to exercise due diligence to make the ship seaworthy and to make the holds fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of the goods; Article III rule 2 requires him properly and carefully to load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge them. Article IV rule 1 places the burden of proving the exercise of due diligence on the carrier, and Article IV rule 2 gives the catalogue of excepted perils, of which rule 2(m) - wastage in bulk or weight and inherent defect, quality or vice - is the natural answer to a grain claim.
Major trap
In limb a), describing the three regimes in turn instead of comparing them - the marks are in the differences - and two specific errors: saying the Visby Protocol extended the period of responsibility, which it did not, and treating the Rotterdam Rules as in force, which they are not and never have been. In limb b), producing the Article IV rule 2 catalogue with no burden of proof, when the question is really a sequence: clean bill in and damaged cargo out makes the prima facie case, the carrier must then bring himself within an exception and Article IV rule 1 puts the burden on him, and the claimant defeats it by proving breach of Article III. The sharpest technical trap is putting ventilation inside the management-of-the-ship exception: ventilation is care of the cargo under Article III rule 2, and bad ventilation is precisely what destroys an otherwise correct inherent-vice defence. Finally, check the one-year time bar first, and quote no SDR figure.

Also on the platform: QP2306 Q3 - general average and the insurance response, which is where an unrecoverable cargo claim ends up · QP2306 Q6 - repair contracts, another agreement where a defined period and defined defences do the work · QP2306 Q1 - the compensation conventions, a different treaty answer to the same question of who bears a loss

First in set First set here. The same task returns at December 2024 Q7.

Understand this first

A ship pushing through water pays for it twice: once for shoving the water aside and making waves, and once for dragging it along the skin of the hull. On the big, slow, full-bodied ships that carry most of the world's cargo, the second of those is the larger bill by a wide margin, which is why so much effort now goes into it. The drag itself comes from a very thin region right against the plating, where swirling motions in the water keep carrying fast-moving fluid down onto the surface and slow fluid away from it; that constant exchange is what the hull feels as friction. So there are only a few honest ways to reduce it. You can wet less of the ship, which the hull designer does but only so far, because she still has to carry her cargo. You can make the wetted part smoother and keep it that way, which is what coatings and cleaning are for and which remains the biggest and cheapest gain of all. You can put a gas in between, so that some of the hull is touching air instead of water - and the two ways of doing that differ in whether you trap the air in a shaped pocket built into the bottom, or blow it out as a cloud of bubbles that the flow carries away aft. Or you can change the texture of the surface itself so that the swirls near the wall are held back, which sounds like making it rougher and in a sense is, yet gives less drag. Each has a catch. Blowing air costs power, and the deeper the ship the more it costs, so the saving has to be counted net. A shaped pocket is a structural alteration and stops working when the ship starts moving in a seaway. And a clever texture only works while it survives, which on a fouling, scraping hull is the whole problem.

Memory cue. Four families first - AREA, ROUGHNESS, GAS, SURFACE (2) - and the three named ones sort themselves: two GAS, one SURFACE. Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 66 separate facts to learn individually.
