Model written answer

1. What viscous resistance is, and why it is the target

Total resistance divides into frictional (viscous) resistance and residuary resistance, the latter comprising wave-making and eddy or form resistance. Frictional resistance is the tangential drag of water on the hull surface, and it depends on:

  • Wetted surface area - the area actually in contact with water;
  • Speed, raised to a power slightly below two;
  • Reynolds number, through the skin friction coefficient; and
  • Surface roughness, which adds a further allowance and which fouling and coating condition control.

Two facts make it the right target. First, at ship scale the boundary layer is turbulent over essentially the whole hull - there is no laminar flow worth preserving - and turbulent friction is an order of magnitude greater than laminar. Second, on a full-form, moderate-speed merchant ship such as a tanker or bulk carrier the frictional component dominates total resistance; on a fine, fast form the residuary share is much larger. So on the ships that carry most of the world's cargo, friction is where the power actually goes, which is exactly why the examiner calls it "a major component".

The mechanism worth stating, because all three named technologies interfere with it: immediately at the wall there is a thin viscous sublayer; above it, streamwise vortex structures continually sweep fast fluid toward the wall and eject slow fluid away from it, and that exchange of momentum is the wall shear stress. Reduce or disrupt that exchange and you reduce friction.

2. The logically distinct ways of attacking skin friction

There are only four, and placing the named technologies among them turns a list into an argument:

  1. Reduce the wetted area - hull form optimisation, constrained by the displacement the ship must carry.
  2. Reduce the roughness of the surface that is wetted - smooth coatings, fouling control, hull cleaning and propeller polishing, fair plating and flush appendages.
  3. Put a gas between the hull and the water, so that part of the surface is in contact with a fluid of far lower density and viscosity. Air cavity systems and microbubbles are both in this family, and they differ in how the gas is held.
  4. Change the nature of the surface itself, so that the near-wall turbulence transfers less momentum to it. Surface texturing is in this family, along with polymer and compliant-coating approaches.

Note the consequence at once: two of the three named technologies are active - they consume power to supply air - and one is passive. That distinction decides most of the comparison at step 6.

3. a) Hull Air Cavity Systems

Hull Air Cavity System
PrincipleRecesses or cavities are built into the flat of bottom and air is injected into them at low pressure. The cavity geometry - a step at the leading edge and a closing beach aft - traps and retains a stable gas pocket, so that over that area the hull is in contact with air rather than water. Water is displaced from part of the wetted surface rather than merely aerated
What it needsStructural modification of the hull, at the design stage or in a major conversion; low-pressure blowers; a control system holding the cavity across the operating range. Air demand is set by leakage from the cavity edges, not by the area covered - which is why the blower power is comparatively modest
MeritsThe largest and most stable gas coverage of the air-based methods, and the lowest parasitic power for a given covered area. Effective at moderate and low speeds. Well suited to flat-bottomed, full-form vessels - tankers, bulk carriers, barges and inland craft - which is exactly the tonnage where friction dominates
LimitsIt is a hull modification, so retrofit is a structural job and there is a penalty in internal volume and added weight. The cavity must be maintained across the ship's range of draught and trim, and is disturbed or collapsed by motion in a seaway, so the benefit falls away in the conditions where power is most needed. Escaping air may reach the propeller, causing thrust loss, cavitation and vibration. The recesses complicate hull inspection, coating and cleaning. And the geometry does not suit fine forms

4. b) Hull Surface Texturing

This is the one passive method of the three. Nothing is injected and no power is consumed; the micro-geometry of the surface itself is engineered so that the near-wall turbulence transfers less momentum to the wall.

  • Riblets are the principal form - very fine streamwise grooves, of shark-skin type, applied as an adhesive film or as a moulded coating. They do not make the surface smoother; they restrain the spanwise motion of the near-wall vortices and lift them slightly clear of the wall, reducing the momentum exchange that constitutes wall shear stress. A riblet surface is measurably rougher than a smooth one and yet has lower drag, which is the counter-intuitive point worth making.
  • Superhydrophobic surfaces combine micro and nano roughness with low surface energy so that a retained air film is held in the texture, giving partial slip at the wall - conceptually a bridge between texturing and air lubrication.
  • Lubricant-infused textured surfaces hold a liquid within the texture to present a slippery interface.

Merits. Entirely passive - no machinery, no air supply, no parasitic power, and therefore no net-benefit calculation to argue about. No hull structural change, so it is a coating operation rather than a conversion. It can in principle be combined with fouling control, since both occupy the same surface.

Limits, and they are severe at present. The riblet dimension must be matched to the local boundary-layer thickness, which varies along the hull and with speed and draught, so one texture cannot be optimal everywhere and the benefit is lost outside the design condition. Durability is the central problem: the texture is vulnerable to mechanical damage and, far more importantly, to fouling - and a fouled or damaged texture performs worse than a plain smooth surface, so the technology can turn from a benefit into a penalty in service. Applying a controlled micro-texture over tens of thousands of square metres to a consistent standard is demanding, and its compatibility with the anti-fouling scheme must be resolved because the two compete for the same surface.

5. c) Micro bubbles

Microbubble drag reduction
PrincipleAir is injected through porous plates or nozzle arrays set in the flat of bottom, producing a cloud of small bubbles entrained within the turbulent boundary layer. The bubbles reduce the effective density in the near-wall region and modify the turbulence structure, reducing the Reynolds shear stress and hence the wall friction. Unlike a cavity, the water is not displaced - it is aerated
The related regimeAs the air flux is increased the bubbles coalesce into a continuous air layer, which gives a substantially larger reduction. Microbubble, transitional and air layer regimes are points on one scale, selected by air flow rate - and the air layer regime needs a great deal more air
What it needsA continuous supply of compressed air from blowers or compressors, injectors in the shell, and a control system. No change to the hull form beyond the injector fittings, which makes it far more retrofittable than a cavity system
MeritsRetrofittable without structural modification. Coverage can be distributed across the flat of bottom. It works with the existing hull and can be combined with a good coating
LimitsThe parasitic power decides everything. The net benefit is the friction saving less the compressor power, and that power rises with the hydrostatic head at the injection point - so a deep-draught ship pays more air power for the same coverage, which is precisely the ship where friction dominates. The bubble cloud is swept aft and disperses, so the effect decays with distance from the injectors and several injection stations are needed. Bubble persistence depends on salinity and surface-active contaminants in the water. Air reaching the propeller risks thrust loss, cavitation and vibration. The benefit falls away in a seaway. And injectors and porous plates require maintenance and are liable to fouling

6. Comparing the three named technologies

Air cavitySurface texturingMicro bubbles
FamilyGas between hull and water - water displacedSurface changedGas between hull and water - water aerated
Active or passiveActive - blowersPassive - noneActive - compressors
Parasitic powerLow - air demand set by leakageNoneHigh and draught-dependent
Hull changeStructural - design or major conversionCoating operationInjectors only - retrofittable
Best suited toFlat-bottomed, full-form, moderate speedIn principle any hullFlat-bottomed hulls, shallower draught
Principal weaknessSeaway and draught/trim sensitivity; structural costDurability and fouling; benefit lost outside the design conditionNet benefit after compressor power; decay aft of injection
Propeller air ingestionA real riskNot applicableA real risk

Three conclusions worth stating explicitly:

  1. For the two air-based methods the decisive question is never the gross drag reduction but the NET benefit after the air supply power - and for microbubbles that penalty grows with draught, which works directly against the ships that would gain most.
  2. For texturing the decisive question is durability, not physics. The mechanism is sound; the difficulty is holding a micro-texture in service on a fouling, abrading surface for years.
  3. All three degrade in a seaway and none is a substitute for a clean, smooth hull. Hull and propeller condition management remains the largest and cheapest single lever on frictional resistance, and any of these technologies is applied on top of it, not instead of it.

7. The other methods currently under consideration

  • Hull roughness and fouling control - low-friction and fouling-release coatings, planned hull cleaning and propeller polishing, condition monitoring of performance, and attention to weld seams, plate unfairness, appendages, overboard openings and flush sea-chest gratings. Unglamorous and by far the most effective.
  • Reduction of wetted surface through hull form optimisation, within the displacement and stability the ship must have.
  • Air layer drag reduction as a distinct operating regime rather than a distinct technology, discussed at step 5.
  • Polymer and surfactant drag reduction - the Toms effect, in which minute quantities of long-chain polymer released into the boundary layer suppress turbulent bursting and sharply reduce friction. The mechanism is well established; the obstacles are continuous consumption, storage and cost, and above all the environmental objection to discharging the additive to the sea, which is why it remains a research route rather than a shipboard one.
  • Compliant or elastic coatings, intended to deform in response to the near-wall pressure fluctuations and damp the turbulence - long studied and not yet commercially established.

8. Where this stands at present

  • Maturity differs sharply across the three. Air lubrication in its cavity and air-layer forms is in commercial service on a limited number of ships, most of them flat-bottomed full-form vessels. Microbubble injection is closely related and less separable in practice, since a system designed for one regime often operates across the scale. Surface texturing remains at trial and early-application stage, held back by durability rather than by principle. No claim is made here about how many ships are fitted, because that figure moves and no verified figure is held.
  • Verification is the practical difficulty. A saving of this order is hard to demonstrate on a working ship: it must be separated from weather, sea state, loading condition, hull and propeller fouling, and the natural scatter of noon-report data. Credible assessment needs a performance monitoring system with correction for weather and displacement, and preferably shaft power measurement. That is why an owner should require the same evidential standard from a friction-reduction retrofit as from any other efficiency measure.
  • The regulatory driver is why these are under active consideration now. EEXI and the CII entered into force on 1 January 2023 - five months before this examination - so a technical index and an operational rating now bear on the same hull. This is the first CII data year and it is still running, so no ship has yet been rated, and every ship's present operation is what her first rating will reflect. The operative strategy is the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018.
  • There is also a route to credit. IMO guidance exists on the treatment of innovative energy efficiency technologies for the calculation and verification of the attained EEDI, under which a technology that reduces the propulsion power required - which is precisely what air lubrication does - may be credited, subject to verification. That is what turns these from interesting experiments into commercial propositions.
  • The engineering conclusion to close on. Skin friction is the largest single component of resistance on the ships that move most of the world's cargo, and there is no single technology that removes it. What is under consideration is a set of partial measures, each with a different cost, a different parasitic penalty and a different failure mode - and the sound approach is to secure the clean, smooth, well-maintained hull first, and then to apply whichever of these suits the hull form, the draught range, the trade and the seaway the ship actually works in.
Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem asks two things and the second is easy to miss. It asks for "various skin friction reduction methods currently under consideration" - a survey - and for "special mention" of three named technologies. A candidate who writes only about air cavities, texturing and microbubbles has answered the second half; one who surveys the field and treats the three thinly has answered the first. The route carries a survey step at 7 alongside the three limb steps, and that is deliberate.

Sixteen marks with no printed split, and three named limbs that do not divide evenly. Each named technology gets the same four-heading treatment - principle, what it needs, merits, limits - which makes the answer visibly systematic and makes the comparison at step 6 possible. Step 1 is not throat-clearing: you cannot say why these technologies are worth having until you have said what fraction of the problem friction is and how wall friction actually arises.

The classification that turns a list into an argument

There are only four ways to reduce skin friction: reduce the wetted area · reduce the roughness · put gas between the hull and the water · change the surface itself. Place the three named technologies among them in your opening and the rest of the answer has a spine.

The immediate payoff is that air cavities and microbubbles are the same family - both interpose gas - and they differ only in how the gas is held: a cavity displaces the water and holds a stable pocket by its geometry; microbubbles aerate the boundary layer and the cloud is swept away. Everything else about them follows from that one difference. Texturing is a different family altogether, and it is the only passive one of the three.

The single most important practical point

For an air-based system the number that matters is not the drag reduction but the NET saving after the air supply power. A system that cuts friction impressively and consumes a large blower or compressor load may deliver very little. And for microbubbles the penalty scales with the hydrostatic head at the injection point - meaning a deep-draught ship pays more air power for the same coverage, which is exactly the ship on which friction dominates.

That is why the air cavity is attractive despite being a structural job: its air demand is set by leakage from the cavity edges, not by the area covered, so it holds a large gas area for comparatively little power.

The counter-intuitive fact about riblets

A riblet surface is measurably rougher than a smooth one and yet has lower drag. That is worth saying because it shows you know the mechanism rather than the label: riblets do not smooth the surface, they restrain the spanwise motion of the near-wall vortices and lift them slightly clear of the wall, cutting the momentum exchange that constitutes wall shear stress.

And the corollary is the technology's weakness: because it works by a micro-geometry matched to the boundary layer, a fouled or damaged texture performs worse than a plain smooth surface. The physics is sound; the problem is holding the geometry in service for years on an abrading, fouling hull. Durability, not principle, is what holds texturing back.

The sentence that should close the answer

None of the three is a substitute for a clean, smooth, well-maintained hull. Hull roughness and fouling control - coatings, cleaning, propeller polishing, fair plating, flush openings - remains the largest and cheapest single lever on frictional resistance, and any of these technologies is applied on top of it, not instead of it. An examiner reading a page of enthusiasm for air lubrication will be looking for exactly that qualification.

Where the regulation comes in at this sitting

This is what makes it a live question rather than a technical curiosity. EEXI and the CII entered into force on 1 January 2023, five months before this examination, so a technical index and an operational rating now both bear on the same hull. 2023 is the first CII data year and it is still running, so no ship has yet been rated - do not describe rating experience. The operative strategy is the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018; the 2023 Strategy was adopted at MEPC 80 on 7 July 2023, one month after this sitting.

There is also a route to credit: IMO guidance exists on the treatment of innovative energy efficiency technologies for the calculation and verification of the attained EEDI, under which a technology reducing the required propulsion power may be credited subject to verification. That is what converts these from experiments into commercial propositions.

Common mistakes

  • Answering only the three named technologies and ignoring the printed request for various methods.
  • Describing the three without comparing them. With sixteen marks and no split, the comparison is where the upper band is.
  • Treating air cavities and microbubbles as the same thing. One displaces water and holds a stable pocket; the other aerates the boundary layer with a cloud that is swept aft.
  • Quoting drag-reduction or fuel-saving percentages. They vary enormously with hull, speed, draught and method of measurement.
  • Ignoring the parasitic power of the air supply, which is what actually decides whether an air system is worth fitting.
  • Saying riblets work by making the hull smoother. They do not - a riblet surface is rougher and still has lower drag.
  • Omitting the durability and fouling problem with texturing, which is the reason it is not in general service.
  • Forgetting propeller air ingestion as a risk of both air-based systems.
  • Forgetting that all three degrade in a seaway.
  • Presenting any of these as a substitute for hull cleaning and good coatings.
  • Citing the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, which is one month future at this sitting, or describing CII rating experience, of which there is none yet.

Examiner traps

  • "Which resistance component dominates on a loaded VLCC?" The frictional component - full form, moderate speed.
  • "Is the boundary layer laminar anywhere on a ship?" Effectively not at ship scale - it is turbulent over essentially the whole hull.
  • "What is the difference between an air cavity and microbubbles?" The cavity displaces water and holds a stable gas pocket by geometry; microbubbles aerate the boundary layer and disperse aft.
  • "Why does a deep-draught ship pay more for microbubbles?" The compressor works against the hydrostatic head at the injection point.
  • "Why do you need several injection stations?" Because the bubble cloud is swept aft and disperses, so the effect decays with distance from the injectors.
  • "Do riblets make the hull smoother?" No - rougher, and yet lower drag, because they restrain the near-wall vortices.
  • "When does a riblet surface become a penalty?" When it fouls or is damaged - it then performs worse than a plain smooth surface.
  • "What is the risk to the propeller?" Air ingestion, causing thrust loss, cavitation and vibration.
  • "What happens to all three in heavy weather?" The benefit falls away - cavities are disturbed, bubble layers dispersed.
  • "What is the cheapest way to reduce skin friction?" Keep the hull clean, smooth and well coated, and the propeller polished.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What does frictional resistance depend on? Wetted surface area, speed to a power slightly below two, Reynolds number, and surface roughness.
  • How does wall friction actually arise? Turbulent structures above the viscous sublayer exchange momentum with the wall; that exchange is the wall shear stress.
  • Name the four ways of reducing it. Reduce wetted area; reduce roughness; interpose gas; change the surface itself.
  • How is the air held in a cavity system? By the cavity geometry - a step forward and a closing beach aft - so air demand is set by leakage, not by area.
  • What does a cavity system cost you structurally? Hull modification, internal volume and added weight, plus sensitivity to draught, trim and seaway.
  • How do microbubbles reduce drag? By reducing effective near-wall density and modifying the turbulence structure, lowering the Reynolds shear stress.
  • What is air layer drag reduction? The same system at higher air flux, where bubbles coalesce into a continuous film for a larger reduction.
  • How do riblets work? Streamwise micro-grooves that restrain spanwise vortex motion and lift the vortices clear of the wall.
  • What is the Toms effect and why is it not used? Long-chain polymers suppress turbulent bursting; the obstacles are continuous consumption and the environmental objection to discharging additive to the sea.
  • How would you verify a claimed saving? Performance monitoring corrected for weather, sea state and displacement, preferably with shaft power measurement.

Memory framework

Start from the four families: AREA · ROUGHNESS · GAS · SURFACE. The three named technologies then sort themselves - two GAS, one SURFACE.

For the two gas methods, one word each: CAVITY DISPLACES · BUBBLES AERATE. And one consequence each: cavity = structure, low air power; bubbles = retrofit, high air power. For texturing: ROUGHER YET SLIPPERIER - AND IT WEARS OUT.

Treat each under the same four headings - PRINCIPLE, NEEDS, MERITS, LIMITS - and close on the qualification that carries the marks: NONE OF THEM BEATS A CLEAN HULL.

Regulation and source map

  • There is no regulation prescribing any of these technologies. They are naval architecture and engineering practice; the regulation is the efficiency framework that makes them worth fitting
  • EEXI and CII - in force 1 January 2023, five months before this sitting; 2023 is the first CII data year, still running, and no ship has been rated
  • Initial IMO GHG Strategy, 2018 - the operative strategy at this sitting; the 2023 Strategy is one month future
  • IMO guidance on the treatment of innovative energy efficiency technologies for the calculation and verification of the attained EEDI - referred to by description only; MIW holds no copy and no circular number is asserted
  • Resistance decomposition - frictional (viscous) plus residuary (wave-making and eddy or form)
  • Boundary layer - viscous sublayer, near-wall vortex structures, momentum exchange as wall shear stress
  • Four families of attack - wetted area, roughness, interposed gas, surface modification
  • Other methods - hull roughness and fouling control; hull form optimisation; air layer drag reduction; polymer and surfactant drag reduction (the Toms effect); compliant coatings

Uncertainty and the evidential position on this question

The True Source corpus holds no naval architecture or ship resistance material whatever - no resistance and propulsion reference, no towing-tank or trials material, and no guidance on innovative energy efficiency technologies. Nor does any solved MIW question address any of the three named technologies. This question therefore carries no primary source and none is claimed. The technology content is engineering judgement and industry guidance and is labelled as such.

The discipline that follows is that no quantitative claim is asserted anywhere. No percentage of total resistance attributable to friction for any hull form; no drag-reduction percentage for any of the three technologies; no net fuel saving; no air flow rate, injection pressure or blower power; no riblet dimension; no payback period. All are highly configuration-, speed-, draught- and hull-specific, and published figures vary by a wide margin between full-scale trials, model tests and computational studies. No correlation line, extrapolation method or roughness allowance is cited by name, no ship, supplier or trial is named, and no claim is made about fleet uptake.

The regulatory frame was re-anchored to this sitting rather than inherited from the energy-efficiency family, whose members all sit between 2024 and 2026 and therefore carry a later strategy and real rating experience. And the IMO guidance on innovative technologies is referred to by description and not by circular number, because MIW holds no copy: what is asserted is that such guidance exists and that a power-reducing technology may be credited under it subject to verification, and no clause or category is quoted.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing What viscous resistance is, and why it is the target
  2. __________framing The logically distinct ways of attacking skin friction
  3. __________a a) Hull Air Cavity Systems
  4. __________b b) Hull Surface Texturing
  5. __________c c) Micro bubbles
  6. __________comparison Comparing the three named technologies
  7. __________survey The other methods currently under consideration
  8. __________closing Where this stands at present
Flashcards 12

1 What viscous resistance is and why it is the target. 2 The four ways of attacking it. 3 Hull air cavity systems. 4 Hull surface texturing. 5 Micro bubbles. 6 Comparison. 7 The other methods under consideration. 8 Maturity, verification and the regulatory driver.

The stem asks for a survey AND for three special mentions, so step 7 is compulsory, not optional.

On wetted surface area, on speed raised to a power slightly below two, on Reynolds number through the friction coefficient, and on surface roughness. It arises because above a thin viscous sublayer at the wall, streamwise vortex structures continually sweep fast fluid toward the wall and eject slow fluid away from it; that momentum exchange is the wall shear stress.

Every one of the three named technologies interferes with that exchange, so stating it makes them comparable.

Reduce the wetted area, by hull form optimisation within the displacement required. Reduce the roughness of the wetted surface, by coatings, fouling control and cleaning. Put a gas between the hull and the water - air cavities and microbubbles. Change the nature of the surface itself - texturing, polymer and compliant approaches.

Placing the three named technologies among four families turns a list into an argument.

A cavity displaces the water: recesses built into the flat of bottom, with a step forward and a closing beach aft, trap a stable gas pocket so the hull is in contact with air over that area. Microbubbles aerate: air injected through porous plates or nozzles forms a cloud entrained within the boundary layer, reducing effective near-wall density and modifying the turbulence, and the cloud is swept aft and disperses.

Everything else about the two - air demand, hull change, retrofittability, seaway behaviour - follows from that one difference.

Because the cavity geometry retains the gas, so air need only be supplied to make up the leakage from the cavity edges - the demand is set by leakage rather than by the area covered. That is what gives a cavity system the lowest parasitic power of the air-based methods, despite the structural cost of building it.

It is the reason a structural modification can still be the better commercial choice.

Because the compressor must work against the hydrostatic head at the injection point, so the parasitic power rises with draught. The net benefit is the friction saving less that compressor power - and the penalty grows on exactly the ships where frictional resistance dominates.

The decisive commercial fact about the technology, and the one most candidates omit.

They are fine streamwise micro-grooves that restrain the spanwise motion of the near-wall vortices and lift them slightly clear of the wall, reducing the momentum exchange that constitutes wall shear stress. The counter-intuitive point is that a riblet surface is measurably rougher than a smooth one and still has lower drag.

Saying it proves you know the mechanism rather than the label.

Durability, not physics. The riblet dimension must match the local boundary-layer thickness, which varies along the hull and with speed and draught, so one texture cannot be optimal everywhere. More seriously the texture is vulnerable to fouling and mechanical damage, and a fouled or damaged texture performs worse than a plain smooth surface - so it can turn from a benefit into a penalty in service.

It explains why a mechanism that works in the laboratory is not in general service.

The same injection system operated at higher air flux, at which the bubbles coalesce into a continuous air film rather than remaining a dispersed cloud, giving a substantially larger drag reduction. Microbubble, transitional and air layer regimes are points on a single scale selected by air flow rate - the air layer regime requiring considerably more air.

It shows the two named technologies are less separable in practice than the question implies.

Both risk air reaching the propeller, causing thrust loss, cavitation and vibration. All three degrade in a seaway - cavities are disturbed or collapsed by the ship's motion, and bubble clouds and air layers are dispersed - so the benefit falls away in exactly the conditions where the power is most needed.

Two limitations examiners reliably ask about, and both are easy to omit.

Polymer drag reduction: minute quantities of long-chain polymer released into the boundary layer suppress turbulent bursting and sharply reduce friction. The mechanism is well established, but it requires continuous consumption with the associated storage and cost, and above all it discharges the additive to the sea, which is environmentally objectionable - so it remains a research route rather than a shipboard one.

It is the strongest of the 'other methods' the stem asks for, and its failure mode is not technical.

That none of these is a substitute for a clean, smooth, well-maintained hull. Hull roughness and fouling control - good coatings, planned hull cleaning, propeller polishing, fair plating and flush openings - remains the largest and cheapest single lever on frictional resistance, and any of these technologies is applied on top of it rather than instead of it.

An examiner reading a page of enthusiasm for air lubrication is looking for exactly this qualification.

Quick revision
15-second recall
FRAME. Total resistance = frictional (viscous) + residuary (wave-making + eddy/form). Friction depends on wetted area, speed to a power just under two, Reynolds number and roughness. At ship scale the boundary layer is turbulent over essentially the whole hull. On a full-form, moderate-speed ship friction dominates. Mechanism: a thin viscous sublayer, streamwise vortices above it exchanging momentum with the wall - that exchange IS the wall shear stress. FOUR WAYS TO ATTACK IT: reduce AREA · reduce ROUGHNESS · interpose GAS · change the SURFACE. Two of the three named are gas (active); one is surface (passive). (a) AIR CAVITY. Recesses built into the flat of bottom; air injected at low pressure; a step forward and closing beach aft trap a stable gas pocket - water is displaced, not aerated. Air demand is set by LEAKAGE, not by area, so blower power is modest. Merits: largest, most stable coverage; lowest parasitic power; good at moderate/low speed; suits flat-bottomed full-form ships. Limits: structural modification, lost volume, added weight; must hold across draught and trim; collapses in a seaway; air to the propeller - thrust loss, cavitation, vibration; complicates inspection and cleaning; not for fine forms. (b) SURFACE TEXTURING. Passive. Riblets - fine streamwise grooves, shark-skin type, as film or moulded coating; they restrain spanwise vortex motion and lift the vortices clear of the wall. A riblet surface is ROUGHER than a smooth one and still has LOWER DRAG. Also superhydrophobic (retained air film, partial slip) and lubricant-infused surfaces. Merits: no machinery, no air, no parasitic power; no structural change. Limits: riblet size must match the local boundary-layer thickness, which varies along the hull and with speed; DURABILITY IS THE PROBLEM - a fouled or damaged texture is WORSE than plain smooth; quality control over a huge area; competes with the antifouling scheme for the same surface. (c) MICRO BUBBLES. Air through porous plates or nozzles in the flat of bottom; a cloud entrained in the turbulent boundary layer reduces effective near-wall density and modifies the turbulence, cutting Reynolds shear stress. Water is aerated, not displaced. At higher air flux bubbles coalesce into an air layer - bigger reduction, much more air. Merits: retrofittable, no hull form change. Limits: net benefit = saving less COMPRESSOR POWER, and that power rises with hydrostatic head at the injector, so deep-draught ships pay more; the cloud is swept aft and decays, needing several stations; salinity and surfactants affect persistence; propeller air ingestion; degraded in a seaway; injectors foul. COMPARE: displaced / changed / aerated · blowers / none / compressors · low / none / high-and-draught-dependent power · structural / coating / injectors. OTHER METHODS: hull roughness and fouling control (coatings, cleaning, propeller polishing, fair plating, flush gratings) - the biggest lever; wetted-surface reduction by hull form; air layer regime; polymer drag reduction (Toms effect) - blocked by consumption and by discharge to the sea; compliant coatings. AT THIS SITTING: EEXI and CII in force 1 January 2023, five months old; first CII data year still running, no ship rated; operative strategy is the Initial 2018 Strategy. IMO guidance exists on crediting innovative energy efficiency technologies in the attained EEDI. CLOSE: none of them beats a clean hull.

Critical numbers
  • Total resistance = frictional (viscous) + residuary (wave-making and eddy or form)
  • Frictional resistance depends on wetted surface area, speed to a power slightly below two, Reynolds number and roughness
  • EEXI and CII in force 1 January 2023 - five months before this sitting

  • NO drag-reduction, fuel-saving, air-flow, riblet-dimension or payback figure is asserted anywhere
Critical regulation
No regulation prescribes any of these technologies; they are naval architecture and engineering practice, and the regulation is the efficiency framework that makes them commercially worth fitting. At this sitting that framework is EEXI and the operational carbon intensity indicator, both in force since 1 January 2023, with 2023 the first CII data year and still running so that no ship has yet been rated, and with the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018 operative. IMO guidance exists on the treatment of innovative energy efficiency technologies for the calculation and verification of the attained EEDI, under which a technology reducing the required propulsion power may be credited subject to verification.
Major trap
Answering only the three named technologies when the stem also asks for various skin friction reduction methods currently under consideration - a survey is required as well as the three special mentions. Then four technical traps: treating air cavities and microbubbles as the same thing, when one displaces water in a shaped pocket and the other aerates a boundary layer with a cloud that is swept aft; ignoring the parasitic power of the air supply, which is what actually decides whether an air system is worth fitting and which for microbubbles rises with draught; saying riblets work by making the hull smoother, when a riblet surface is rougher and still has lower drag, and omitting that a fouled texture is worse than a plain smooth surface; and presenting any of them as a substitute for a clean, well-coated hull. Quote no drag-reduction or fuel-saving percentage - they vary enormously with hull, speed, draught and method of measurement.

Also on the platform: QP2306 Q5 - anti-fouling coatings, which attack the same wetted surface and the same friction by chemical means · QP2306 Q7 - the SEEMP, whose planning stage is where a technology of this kind is selected and justified · QP2306 Q6 - ship repair contracts, under which a retrofit of any of these systems would be specified and priced

Q1 UNCLOS Pollution Definition, Coastal State Obligations, and the Three Compensation Tiers
16 marks		 The Convention's definition of pollution, and what makes it unusually wide → The general obligation, and why it is not confined to any zone → The coastal State's obligations - rule-making → The coastal State's obligations - enforcement, and its limits → Why the oil pollution compensation regime has three tiers → Tier one - the shipowner and his insurer → Tiers two and three - the Funds, and who actually provides the money → What the three tiers do NOT cover - and the trap UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) defines pollution of the marine environment as the introduction by man, directly or indirectly, of substances or energy into the marine environment, including estuaries, which results or is likely to result in such deleterious effects as harm to living resources and marine life, hazards to human health, hindrance to marine activities including fishing and other legitimate uses of the sea, impairment of quality for use of sea water and reduction of amenities. Article 192 imposes the general obligation to protect and preserve the marine environment, and article 211 requires that rules for vessel-source pollution be established through the competent international organization, with national law having at least the same effect.
UNCLOS article 1(1)(4) - the definition of pollution of the marine environment		 Two traps, one in each limb. In limb A, answering "the obligations of coastal states" with a list of enforcement powers and never mentioning the rule-making obligations under articles 207 to 212 - and in particular missing that the coastal State's discretion is widest for land-based sources and narrowest for ships, and that article 211(6) forbids it from imposing design, construction, manning or equipment standards on foreign ships even in a special area. In limb B, offering the Bunkers Convention 2001 as one of the three tiers, saying the Funds are financed by shipowners or governments rather than by receivers of contributing oil, and inventing Fund certificates carried by the ship - only tier one produces a ship's certificate. And quote no SDR figure: the limits are amended over time and a remembered number is probably a superseded one.
Q2 The Origin and Authority of IMO, Ratification, and Two Oversight Instruments
16 marks		 The origin - from a 1948 conference to the Organization of today → The authority vested in IMO - and the decisive limitation → The organs through which the authority is exercised → The ratification process - briefly, as the question asks → Keeping a convention current - tacit acceptance → Short note - the Casualty Investigation Code → Short note - the IMO Member State Audit Scheme → What the two short notes have in common The Casualty Investigation Code, resolution MSC.255(84) of 16 May 2008, is made mandatory by SOLAS regulation XI-1/6, which requires each Administration to conduct investigations of marine casualties and marine incidents in accordance with the Code, with effect from 1 January 2010. The IMO Member State Audit Scheme became mandatory on 1 January 2016 through amendments to SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, the Load Lines Convention and its 1988 Protocol, the Tonnage Convention and COLREG, and applies the IMO Instruments Implementation Code, resolution A.1070(28) of 4 December 2013, as its audit standard.
6 March 1948 - IMCO Convention adopted at Geneva		 Budgeting. Five printed deliverables against sixteen marks - trace the origin, elaborate on authority, explain the ratification process briefly, and two short notes - and the usual failure is writing two of them well and running out of time. The substantive traps are: answering "authority" with a description of the organs, when the answer is that IMO regulates States and cannot enforce anything against a ship; writing the Council as 52 Members, when the enlargement adopted in December 2021 is not in force at this sitting; saying the audit scheme is voluntary or that it audits ships, when it has audited States compulsorily since 1 January 2016 against the III Code; citing A.1187(33) for the list of obligations, which is six months future here - the list is A.1157(32); and presenting the Casualty Investigation Code as a blame-finding instrument when its governing principle is that it is separate from and must not prejudice any liability proceeding.
Q3 Running Down Clause, General Average Expenses, and a Claim for Unrepaired Damage
16 marks		 Where these two clauses live, and why the question pairs them → The Running Down Clause - what the hull policy covers → What the Running Down Clause excludes, and where the rest goes → General Average Expenses - what they are → How H&M and P&I each treat a general average claim → Damage found in dry dock a year later - is it admissible? → The unrepaired damage claim, and how its value is ascertained Marine Insurance Act, 1963 section 69(3), reproducing section 69(3) of the Marine Insurance Act 1906: where the ship has not been repaired and has not been sold in her damaged state during the risk, the assured is entitled to be indemnified for the reasonable depreciation arising from the unrepaired damage, but not exceeding the reasonable cost of repairing such damage. The Unrepaired Damage clause of the hull policy restates this as the reasonable depreciation in market value at the time the insurance terminates, capped at the reasonable cost of repairs and at the insured value, and excludes liability altogether where a subsequent total loss is sustained during the period of the insurance.
Three fourths - the proportion of collision liability indemnified by the hull policy		 In limb a), treating the Running Down Clause as covering collision with anything. It responds only to collision with another VESSEL - a ship that strikes a jetty, buoy, cable or rig recovers nothing under it and the whole liability is the club's. The related trap is answering "how are claims treated by H&M as well as P&I" with a single sentence after two long definitions, when the allocation IS the question. In limb b), two traps: concluding that a claim discovered a year later fails, when it attaches to the policy on risk at the date the damage was sustained; and answering the valuation with the cost of repair, when the measure for unrepaired damage is the reasonable depreciation, merely capped at repair cost - and is lost entirely if the ship becomes a total loss within the same policy period.
Q4 Communication in Emergency Preparedness under the ISM Code
16 marks		 Where the printed words come from, and what is actually being asked → Why communication is the load-bearing element of emergency preparedness → The ISM framework that supports emergency communication → Item (i) - contact between ship and the office → Item (ii) - communication equipment → Item (iii) - dealing with the media → Item (iv) - dealing with the next of kin ISM Code paragraph 8.3 - the safety management system should provide for measures ensuring that the Company's organization can respond at any time to hazards, accidents and emergency situations involving its ships. Read with paragraph 8.1, requiring the Company to identify potential emergency shipboard situations and establish procedures to respond to them, and paragraph 8.2, requiring programmes for drills and exercises. Support comes from section 4, under which the Designated Person is the link between the Company and those on board with direct access to the highest level of management, and section 5, which requires a clear statement of the Master's overriding authority and his right to request the Company's assistance as may be necessary.
ISM Code section 8 - three paragraphs: 8.1 identify and establish procedures, 8.2 drills and exercises, 8.3 respond at any time		 Answering only half the question. The stem prints two demands - underline the importance of communication in emergency preparedness, and state the measures required for four named items - and a candidate who gives four lists without the argument, or the argument with four thin lists, has answered part of it. The substantive traps are: not recognising that the opening sentence is ISM paragraph 8.3 and writing a general management essay instead; treating media handling as the Master's job when it is a company function with a single authorised spokesperson; releasing names before the next of kin are informed; forgetting internal communications under the equipment limb when most emergencies are lost internally; answering the equipment limb with GMDSS carriage requirements instead of the measures asked for; and presenting media and next-of-kin procedures as ISM requirements when the Code requires only that procedures exist and says nothing about their content.
Q5 Alternatives to Tin-Based Antifouling Paints
16 marks		 Why there are alternatives at all → The second ban, and why it bites on this question now → (i) Controlled Depletion Polymer (CDP) → (ii) Self-Polishing Copolymer (SPC) → (Ill) Hybrid TBT free paint → (iv) Fouling Release paint → Choosing between them → What else is in the field, and why this is now a fuel question The AFS Convention 2001, in force 17 September 2008, prohibits the application or re-application of organotin compounds acting as biocides in anti-fouling systems, requiring instead that a ship either bears no such system or carries a coating forming a barrier to leaching. Resolution MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021 and in force 1 January 2023, extends the controlled list to cybutryne: it may not be applied or re-applied, and an existing system containing it must be removed or overcoated with a sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system and in any event within 60 months of the last application.
AFS Convention 2001 - in force 17 September 2008		 Two traps, and the first is about marks rather than knowledge. This stem asks only about the four coating types - it does not ask about the Anti-Fouling Convention, the certificate or the records - so a candidate who has revised the two-limb version of this question will spend half a sixteen-mark answer on material nobody asked for. The technical traps are: describing CDP and SPC as the same mechanism at different speeds, when one dissolves and the other hydrolyses and is polished away - which is why CDP roughens and SPC smooths; implying a hybrid contains some tin when it is a blend of the two tin-free binder chemistries; treating fouling release as a very good antifouling paint when it contains no biocide and kills nothing; and omitting that fouling release is speed-dependent and therefore poor for slow or idle ships. Finally, not knowing about cybutryne - in force since 1 January 2023 - forfeits the one genuinely current fact on the question.
Q6 Key Issues in Ship Repair Contracts
16 marks		 What a repair contract is, and the one idea behind all five limbs → A. Payment terms → B. Ambit of the specification and additional work → C. Contract period, liquidated damages and force majeure → D. Guarantees and insurance → E. Termination events → The two clauses the question does not name, but which decide all five There is no regulation on this question, and saying so is itself a point. A ship repair contract is a commercial contract governed by the law the parties choose; no IMO instrument and no national maritime statute determines its content. Two general propositions apply and both are jurisdiction-dependent: a stipulated sum for delay is enforceable as liquidated damages where it is a genuine pre-estimate of the loss and may be unenforceable as a penalty where it is not; and in common-law systems force majeure is a creature of contract, with only the narrow doctrine of frustration behind it if no clause is drafted.
NO figure is asserted anywhere on this question - no liquidated damages rate, no cap percentage, no retention percentage, no grace period, no guarantee period in months, no insurance limit, no interest rate. All are negotiated and vary by yard, region, vessel and market		 Budgeting, first of all: five printed limbs against sixteen marks with no split, so nothing can be written at length and nothing may be omitted - and the command verb is illustrate, so a bare list of headings has not answered the question. The substantive traps are: omitting the repairer's lien from the payment limb, when it is the commercial fact that dominates the whole contract; agreeing a variation on price alone and thereby surrendering the delay remedy, which is the classic real-world failure and the point that links limbs B and C; asserting that force majeure is a general legal principle when in common-law systems it exists only if drafted, and that it gives money when it gives an extension of time; forgetting that the guarantee excludes consequential loss, so the yard replaces the part and the owner bears the off-hire; and writing termination as a bare right with no consequences, when the consequences - valuation, release of the ship against security, seaworthy and towable condition, return of materials - are the substance of the limb.
Q7 SEEMP - The Four-Step Energy Efficiency Cycle
16 marks		 What the SEEMP is, and where the four steps live → Step 1 - Planning → Step 2 - Implementation → Step 3 - Monitoring → Step 4 - Self-evaluation and improvement → Why the cycle is critical, not merely administrative MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 requires each ship to keep on board a Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan, developed and reviewed taking into account the guidelines adopted by the Organization - which at this sitting are the 2022 Guidelines, resolution MEPC.346(78) of 10 June 2022, revoking the 2016 Guidelines MEPC.282(70). Section 3.6 of those Guidelines states that Part I should be developed as a ship-specific plan by the company and should reflect efforts to improve energy efficiency and reduce carbon intensity through four steps - planning, implementation, monitoring, and self-evaluation and improvement - and that these components play a critical role in the continuous cycle.
MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26 - the SEEMP; 26.1 Part I, 26.2 Part II, 26.3 Part III		 Answering the question as a description of four steps when the printed command asks for the critical role of each, with suitable examples - three demands, of which description is only one. Alongside it sit the currency traps, and on this question they are unusually dangerous because the identical stem recurs at later sittings where the answers differ. At this sitting the guidelines are MEPC.346(78), the 2022 edition; the strategy is the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018, because the 2023 Strategy was adopted on 7 July 2023, one month after this examination; and no ship has yet been rated under the CII, because 2023 is the first data year and it is still running. The regulation number is the one thing that does not reverse: the SEEMP is regulation 26, not 22, the renumbering having taken effect on 1 November 2022. Finally, the four steps are the framework of Part I only.
Q8 Carrier Responsibility under the Cargo Conventions, and Defences
16 marks		 Why the duration of responsibility matters → Hague and Hague-Visby - tackle to tackle → Hamburg - port to port → Rotterdam - door to door, and not in force → The comparison, and the Indian position → The grain claim - how it is framed, and who must prove what → The excepted perils that answer a grain claim → The overriding obligations that can defeat every one of those defences → Procedural and quantum defences, and the evidence Hague Rules Article I(e) defines carriage of goods as covering the period from the time when the goods are loaded on to the time they are discharged from the ship - the tackle-to-tackle period, unchanged by the Visby and SDR Protocols. Article III rule 1 binds the carrier, before and at the beginning of the voyage, to exercise due diligence to make the ship seaworthy and to make the holds fit and safe for the reception, carriage and preservation of the goods; Article III rule 2 requires him properly and carefully to load, handle, stow, carry, keep, care for and discharge them. Article IV rule 1 places the burden of proving the exercise of due diligence on the carrier, and Article IV rule 2 gives the catalogue of excepted perils, of which rule 2(m) - wastage in bulk or weight and inherent defect, quality or vice - is the natural answer to a grain claim.
Hague Rules Article I(e) - the period runs from loading on to discharge from the ship		 In limb a), describing the three regimes in turn instead of comparing them - the marks are in the differences - and two specific errors: saying the Visby Protocol extended the period of responsibility, which it did not, and treating the Rotterdam Rules as in force, which they are not and never have been. In limb b), producing the Article IV rule 2 catalogue with no burden of proof, when the question is really a sequence: clean bill in and damaged cargo out makes the prima facie case, the carrier must then bring himself within an exception and Article IV rule 1 puts the burden on him, and the claimant defeats it by proving breach of Article III. The sharpest technical trap is putting ventilation inside the management-of-the-ship exception: ventilation is care of the cargo under Article III rule 2, and bad ventilation is precisely what destroys an otherwise correct inherent-vice defence. Finally, check the one-year time bar first, and quote no SDR figure.
Q9 Skin Friction Reduction - Air Cavities, Surface Texturing and Microbubbles
16 marks		 What viscous resistance is, and why it is the target → The logically distinct ways of attacking skin friction → a) Hull Air Cavity Systems → b) Hull Surface Texturing → c) Micro bubbles → Comparing the three named technologies → The other methods currently under consideration → Where this stands at present No regulation prescribes any of these technologies; they are naval architecture and engineering practice, and the regulation is the efficiency framework that makes them commercially worth fitting. At this sitting that framework is EEXI and the operational carbon intensity indicator, both in force since 1 January 2023, with 2023 the first CII data year and still running so that no ship has yet been rated, and with the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018 operative. IMO guidance exists on the treatment of innovative energy efficiency technologies for the calculation and verification of the attained EEDI, under which a technology reducing the required propulsion power may be credited subject to verification.
Total resistance = frictional (viscous) + residuary (wave-making and eddy or form)		 Answering only the three named technologies when the stem also asks for various skin friction reduction methods currently under consideration - a survey is required as well as the three special mentions. Then four technical traps: treating air cavities and microbubbles as the same thing, when one displaces water in a shaped pocket and the other aerates a boundary layer with a cloud that is swept aft; ignoring the parasitic power of the air supply, which is what actually decides whether an air system is worth fitting and which for microbubbles rises with draught; saying riblets work by making the hull smoother, when a riblet surface is rougher and still has lower drag, and omitting that a fouled texture is worse than a plain smooth surface; and presenting any of them as a substitute for a clean, well-coated hull. Quote no drag-reduction or fuel-saving percentage - they vary enormously with hull, speed, draught and method of measurement.
